The rest was named and not specified, and a screen could not be written from it. | 12 §10.1 to §10.3. The example screen is corrected. |
| D-292 | The toolkit never reads keys: it acts on a fixed set of navigation names that a keymap gives to keys, offered to the focused widget, then menu hotkeys, then the screen's `keys`, then the toolkit. A widget that edits text takes a key that types before its name is considered. A context the keymap does not name falls back to `menu`, and with no keymap the keyboard's own navigation keys carry the names. The toolkit draws its own parts with theme styles and marks of fixed names, each with a plain default. | Design, from building presentation | Navigation has to work in a total conversion with its own keymap and theme, and in a profile with neither. Typing `q` into a name field must not close the screen. | 12 §9.1, §10.3. The names are part of the modding surface. |
| D-293 | A key press is always delivered as a key and text only for what was not key presses. The terminal frontend delivers Control-C as close. `Frontend::present` returns nothing; the terminal frontend keeps the last error for whoever asks. | Design, from building presentation | A frontend that reported one keystroke twice would type every letter twice. In raw mode Control-C no longer interrupts, and a player must always be able to leave. 12 §3 fixes the trait's shape. | 12 §9.1; `TermFrontend::take_error`. |
| D-294 | `clash-scene` depends on `clash-core` directly, for values, variables and its own random stream, and on `serde_json`, to hold interface effects as written until they are built. It keeps the workspace's determinism lints although it is outside the simulation. | Design, from building presentation | Screen variables are the values rules will write. Frames must be reproducible (12 V8), and the lints cost nothing there. | 01 §3 says a crate may name what its arrows reach. Wall-clock time enters the scene only as a number from the application. |
| D-280 | A chunk's derived flags are one byte per cell, holding five flags, kept up to date by every structural write. They are not five bitsets and are never rebuilt lazily. `walk_blocked` and `solid_top` are given exact rules, with a named step height of 0.5 m. | Design, from building world storage | Reads take a chunk by shared reference and may run in parallel, so a lazy rebuild would need interior mutability. One load answers every flag of a cell, where five bitsets cost five. The specification named the flags without saying when each is set. | 02 §6.2. About 8 KB for a chunk that is not uniform, where bitsets would take 5 KB; a uniform chunk keeps one value. A scan for one flag tests one bit of each byte. `CellBits` remains for sets of cells. |
| D-281 | `Paletted` keeps its representation private. A palette that fills at 8 bits or wider is cleared of unused entries before it is widened. A column holds its chunks inline, a chunk keeps its four sparse layers behind one pointer, and the cosmos keeps grids in a table indexed by grid id. | Design, from building world storage | A public variant could be built with an index its palette lacks, and a read of it would panic. A cell that keeps changing would otherwise fill a palette with stale entries. Each of the other three removes a pointer or a hash from every read of a cell, or bytes from every chunk. | 02 §4, §6.1, §6.2, §6.3. A uniform chunk is 184 bytes. Grid ids are never reused, so the slot of a grid that has gone stays empty. |
| D-282 | Every read of the world returns `Result<_, WorldError>`: not loaded, outside the realm with the edge crossed, or unknown grid. A `GridView` gives the same reads for many cells of one grid. | Design, from building world storage | The specification said reading an unloaded cell is an error value and sketched signatures that could not carry one. | 02 §7.1. Callers handle the refusal or pass it on; nothing reads through to a default. |
| D-283 | Until the loader feeds them, the world's definition types are plain `serde` structs that refuse unknown keys, and `WorldDefs::build` takes them with their qualified ids, in any order, and returns the resolved tables or every mistake found. Material 0 is the built-in `engine:none`, as definition 0 of each element type is. A bare reference is looked up in the definition's own namespace, then in every namespace in sorted order. A state may give its bits as a number. Blocks and fixtures must give height and fill. | Design, from building world storage | `clash-data` was being written at the same time and could not be depended on. A reserved material makes every form of no element one value, which hashing needs. The search of every namespace stands in for import lists the loader will supply. | 02 §5.1, §5.4; plans/world-model.md W3, marked `beta`. The test content is in `mods/core/world/` and moves under `data/` when typed loading lands. Quantity parsing in `clash-world` is to be replaced by the loader's. |
| D-284 | A realm's cells run from 0 to its size on each horizontal axis. Positions order by storey, then row, then column. Half way round a wrapped axis the lower coordinate goes forward. A heightmap entry is the highest storey that holds any element or can be stood in. A column hashes by what its cells hold, not by how much of it is stored. | Design, from building world storage | Each was left open by the specification and each decides a result that must be the same on every run: which cell a wrapped position is, the order of a query's answer, the sign of a delta, where falling stops, and whether undoing an edit restores the hash. | 02 §3.1, §3.3, §4.2, §6.3. |
| D-285 | The size-literal check is the fifth step of `cargo xtask check`. It reads the source of `clash-world` and `clash-gen` and refuses the literals 32, 64, 1024, 2048, 4096 and 8192 outside `clash_world::dims`, except in comments, strings, test modules and lines marked `dims-ok`. It does not look for 8. | Design, from building world storage | Completes D-250. The height of a chunk in storeys is too common a number to tell apart from every other eight; a size built from it is caught through its companions. | xtask; plans/world-model.md F1, W1. A crate that starts to deal in these sizes is added to the list of those checked. |
| D-270 | The expression grammar is completed where the specification left it open. A map literal `{key: value}` is a production. A quote inside a string is written twice. `namespace:name` is one name only when nothing separates its parts. A unit after a number is a scale and may follow after spaces; `C`, an offset, is refused. Comparisons do not chain. A number format in a text is `.N`. Nesting, tree depth and source length are limited, and exceeding a limit is a load error. | Design, from building the parser | The specification wrote map literals in prose and left them out of the grammar, and gave no way to put a quote in a string. `-5C` would have read as minus 278.15 kelvin. A parser without limits can be made to exhaust the stack by content, which rule R6 forbids. | 05 §4.1, §4.6, §4.7; package X1. In a text template a map literal that opens an expression is bracketed, because `{{` is a literal brace. |
| D-271 | The registry of 05 §5.3 is plain data in `clash-expr`: names, types, documentation and flags, each registration returning a dense id. Getters, setters and function bodies stay in the simulation, keyed by those ids, and are reached through one host interface when an expression runs. The expression crate has its own `Value`, wider than `clash_core::Value`: null, ids, subject handles, lists and maps. A handle is a kind and 64 opaque bits; an id is a definition type and its dense number. | Design, from building the compiler | `clash-expr` sits below the simulation (01 §3) and cannot name `SimView` or `EffectCtx`, which the sketch in 05 §5.3 did. `clash-core` was read-only for this package and its `Value` holds four scalar kinds. | 05 §3, §5.3; packages X2, X3, K4. The simulation keeps a table of getters indexed by property id. `clash_core::Value` converts into the wider type and back when it fits. |
| D-272 | Operators are completed where the specification left them open. `^` always gives a real. `%` and `div` round toward minus infinity. `floor`, `ceil` and `round` give an int. Integer overflow is null with a warning. Null counts as false in `and`, `or`, `not` and `if`. `== null` and `!= null` are recognised where written; any other comparison with a null at run time is false, `!=` included. Each operation that meets a null warns. A map key is a text, and an id used as one is its qualified id. `m.key` reads a map entry. An int stays an int where a real is wanted. | Design, from building the machine | Each was needed to write one evaluator, let alone two that agree. Truncating `%` gives negative remainders that surprise anyone working with coordinates. A null that compared unequal to everything would make `x != 3` true for a target that is gone. | 05 §3, §4.3, §4.4, §4.5, §4.7; package X3. |
| D-273 | In a mind context the compiler takes a property or variable as the character's own state when it is read from the subject the context binds as own, or from a value reached from it through properties registered as yielding something of the same owner. Everything else must be registered safe for belief. Variables carry the flag as properties and functions do. | Design, from building the checker | 05 §7.3 allowed "the character's own state" without saying how a compiler tells. A single flag on a property cannot: `mass` is fine of `self` and truth of `target`. | 05 §5.3, §7.3; package X2. Goals, methods and screens bind their character with `own_subject`. |
| D-274 | Run-time warnings are limited by count, not by time: a site (one operation, one kind of fault) reports its first three and then the occurrences whose count is a power of two. The limiter belongs to the host; the machine hands it every warning. | Design, from building the machine | The simulation has no wall clock (01 R5), and a limit by time would make the same run report different warnings. | 05 §4.5; package X3. Warnings carry an origin number given at compile, from which the simulation knows the rule and the JSON path. |
| D-275 | What `set` and `add` assign to is a place, compiled by the expression crate: a local, an event field, a variable or a settable property, with optional keys. The crate resolves and checks it and never writes; the rule runtime evaluates the subject and keys and performs the write. | Design, from building the compiler | Places are written in the expression language and need its resolver, while writes must go through the engine's own mutation paths (01 R3; 05 invariants E1, E2). | 05 §10.3; packages X2, K5. |
| D-260 | A definition type is a plain struct that derives `serde::Deserialize`, read by a deserialiser of the loader's own over the merged tree. The loader checks keys itself, feeds a harmless stand-in past a value of the wrong kind, and reads a definition again with a stand-in for each missing field, so one load reports every mistake in it. A definition struct must not use `flatten`, `untagged` or an internal `tag`. | Design, from building the data pipeline | Derived structs keep definition types ten lines long in the crates that own them. `serde` stops at the first error and buffers values for those three attributes, which loses positions; the stand-ins and the second reading get every error anyway, and the three attributes are the price. | 04 §7.1, §8.2. A field that takes more than one shape is read with `clash_data::by_kind`, which keeps positions. |
| D-261 | The field types of 04 §7.1 ask the loader for themselves under reserved names and are answered from the tree and the link tables. A duration in a definition is a `Time`, or a `Dur` read through `clash_data::dur`. `Power`, `Force`, `Volume`, `Rate<Q>`, `Frequency`, `Located<T>` and `Gated<F, T>` join the table. A speed is a length per time and a power an energy per time. | Design, from building the data pipeline | `clash_core::Dur` reads as a bare count of microseconds, which is right for a save and wrong for data, and `clash-core` cannot know about units. The audit's units had no field types. Treating speed and power as rates makes `km/h` and `J/s` follow from one rule. | 04 §7.1. A quantity type reads a bare number from any other format, so it can sit in saved state. |
| D-262 | `scale` and `add` on a field a definition leaves out act on a default the definition type declares for that purpose, as JSON; a field with none is an error. Within one change the order is plain fields, `delete`, `extend`. `before` and `after` are read only where the list already exists. An amount to `add` in degrees Celsius is a difference. A value written with a unit keeps its dimension through `scale` and `add`. | Design, from building the data pipeline | Merging works on trees and cannot see the defaults in a Rust struct, so "the field's default" needs somewhere to be written down. The other four were unstated and each has two reasonable readings. | 04 §6.1. `Def::DEFAULTS`. Fields of list entries have no declared default. |
| D-263 | Inline definitions are definitions: each is read as its own type and takes a dense id, and the field that holds it keeps that id. A first pass reads every definition only to find them and the tags, before ids are assigned. Their ids are `<parent id>#<JSON path>`, and a JSON path names a list entry by its `id` where it has one. One given an `id` of its own is defined under it in the writing mod's namespace. | Design, from building the data pipeline | Ids must be assigned before anything is read (04 §7.2), and the loader cannot know which fields hold inline definitions without reading. A path by entry id does not move when another mod inserts an entry. | 04 §5, §7.2, §8.2. Loading reads each definition twice. Patching an inline definition from another mod is not built. |
| D-264 | Search directories are tried in order and the first to hold a mod id supplies it, with a warning for a copy further on; two directories in one search directory with the same id are an error. A profile must list every mod its mods require. A profile is found by file, or by name in `profiles/` beside the mods directory and then in each mod's `profiles/`. A patch or override must come after its target in load order. | Design, from building the data pipeline | Document 04 named the places without the rules. A working copy in the user's directory must be able to stand in for an installed mod; within one directory nothing says which is meant. A profile that grew by itself would not be the list a save records. | 04 §3.1, §3.3, §6.3. |
| D-265 | Lenient loading takes its placeholder from the definition type, and stops at a broken definition of a type that supplies none. The engine's own definitions of a type are supplied by the type, live in `engine`, take the first dense ids, and are found by a bare reference after the mod's own namespace and its imports. Subsystem namespaces the engine registers are refused as mod ids. | Design, from building the data pipeline | Only the owning crate can say what an inert block or material is. Dense id 0 must be `none` (04 §4.4) while everything else is in sorted order. | 04 §4.4, §8.3. `Def::placeholder`, `Def::builtins`, `DefTypes::reserve_namespace`. |
| D-266 | The content fingerprint hashes each definition's type, id and values in order, without positions. Key order is kept. A string carries the namespace of the mod that wrote it. Abstract definitions are left out. | Design, from building the data pipeline | Document 04 said "canonical form" without saying which. Key order can carry meaning; the writing mod decides what a bare reference means; positions are not content. | 04 §9. Moving a definition between files of one mod changes nothing; moving a value to another mod does. |
| D-267 | Diagnostic codes are a letter for severity and four digits, grouped by what they are about, and each crate that adds a stage keeps its own table of codes with explanations. Diagnostics are given by file path and position. A bare reference that also matches in a later namespace warns even when the first match is the mod's own. | Design, from building the data pipeline | The specification's own example (`E0203` for an unknown key) fixes the form. Reading order is what a person fixing a file wants, and it does not depend on the order stages run in. | 04 §8.2, §4.3. The `06` group is left for expressions. |
| D-268 | An arrow in 01 §3 also allows whatever the crate at its head may depend on. `clash-tools` names `clash-data` as a dependency of its own. | Design, from building the data pipeline | The linter has to call the loader, which it could already reach through `clash-sim`; re-exporting a whole crate through two others to avoid one line in a manifest helps nobody. | 01 §3. Upward and sideways dependencies stay forbidden. |
| D-320 | The loader is the one way definitions reach the engine. The world's and the interface's definition types are registered with it (`clash_world::register_defs`, `clash_scene::register_defs`), `clash-tools`' `engine_types` calls both, and so `clash-tools` depends on `clash-scene`. The world content of `core` is under `mods/core/data/world/`, with two small realms for tests and tools. The repository's default profile is `profiles/default.json` and lists `core` and `ui`. The temporary readers in the world's tests and in `clash_scene::defs` are gone. | Design, from joining the loader to the world and the scene | Three crates were built at once and each read JSON its own way; the linter saw none of the engine's types. The linter must load with every type the game has, and the interface's belong to the scene. | 01 §3; 04 §3.5, §9; plans/world-model.md W3, W5; plans/first-playable.md U1, U6a. Supersedes the reader of D-283. |
| D-321 | A build stage follows loading. A crate that builds tables from definitions offers one function from `Defs` to them that returns located diagnostics (`WorldDefs::from_defs`, `UiDefs::from_defs`) and one that only checks (`check_defs`), which the linter runs after a load without errors. Its diagnostics are placed through the finished trees. The world's tables use the loader's dense ids: `engine:none` of materials, floors, blocks and fixtures is the type's builtin. The world's reference fields stay text, resolved by the rule of D-283 when the tables are built, and its own quantity types stay. | Design, from joining the loader to the world and the scene | A type's `validate` and a cross check see the typed definitions and not where their values were written, and `clash-data` was closed to this change, so a check that is to point at a value had to run over the finished `Defs`. Rewriting the world's structs to `Ref` and the loader's quantities would have collided with two packages being built in the same crate, and the loader has no density or energy per volume. | 04 §7.1, §8.1, §9, §13. A world mistake is reported only once the loader's own stages pass. Open: `Ref` fields and the loader's quantities in `clash-world`; a way for `validate` to reach a definition's tree in `clash-data`. |
| D-322 | Interface definitions are asked for by qualified id (`ui:default`). A bare colour name in a theme, a glyph, a style or an inline tag means the `color` definition of that name in the theme's own namespace, or else the first of that name in order of qualified id; a bare figure name in a screen is read the same way from the screen's namespace. An art asset is a path within the mod that defines the screen. A screen must stand with every loaded theme. | Design, from joining the loader to the world and the scene | These names are plain text shared with theme colours, styles and terminal colours, so they cannot be `Ref`s yet, and a colour name is written by every mod and resolved by the active theme, so the imports of one mod cannot decide it. The player may pick any loaded theme. | 12 §4.3, §10.3; plans/first-playable.md U1, U6a. Figure and art names become references with U6b. |
| D-323 | Document 04 lists the rules a definition struct follows in one place. A field of more than one shape may be read by a visitor of its own in place of `by_kind`, as long as it takes each kind of value as the loader hands it over: the world's `EdgeSpec` and `GeneratorSpec` were untagged enums and are read so. | Design, from joining the loader to the world and the scene | The rules were spread over the loader's module documentation and a report. A visitor reads under `serde_json` as well, which the unit tests of the world and the scene use; `by_kind` reads only under the loader. | 04 §7.1. |
| D-310 | A ray treats a block or fixture as filling its cell's footprint up to its height. Sight stops at what is `opaque`, movement at what is not `passable`, a projectile at what closes the whole width of its cell below its top. What closes part of it is reported with its share, `coverage × 3 / height` and at most 1, and the ray goes on. A floor is met at the base of the upper cell where a ray passes between storeys and stops every ray. The block and fixture of the cell a ray starts in never stop it. A ray is followed for at most 10 km. | Design, from building world queries | 02 §22 named a filter and gave no rule for it. A query draws no random numbers, so a pillar that closes half its cell cannot be hit or missed by the world; whoever fires rolls against the share. Coverage is a share of the whole cross-section, and a ray that meets a thing is already known to be below its top. | 02 §22. Combat takes the hits in order and rolls for each that does not stop the ray. |
| D-311 | The queries return refusals as the reads do (D-282): `line_of_sight`, `field_of_view`, `neighbours` and `footing` return `Result<_, WorldError>`, `find_path` returns a `PathFail` that says why, and a ray that comes to unloaded space or an edge ends there marked `Refused` with the error, keeping what it met. Rays and lines of sight take no plane mask: terrain is on every plane, and occupants join a ray through the `RayObstacles` trait, asked once for each cell the ray passes. | Design, from building world queries | The sketch in 02 §22 could not carry an error. The occupant index was being built at the same time and owns planes and kinds; a trait lets its hits be met in order with the terrain's without the ray knowing what an occupant is. | 02 §22. Package W7 implements `RayObstacles` over the occupant index; it must leave out whoever cast the ray, whose own cell is asked about too. |
| D-312 | A line of sight is not blocked by what is in the two cells that hold its ends, is always walked from the same end, and takes the shorter way round a wrapped axis. Through unloaded space it is an error; between grids and beyond the length of a ray it is false. | Design, from building world queries | One sees out of one's own cell and sees the wall one looks at. Walking from a fixed end makes the answer the same whichever point is given first, which a walk from the first point given does not promise where a line passes exactly through a corner. | 02 §22. |
| D-313 | Field of view on a storey is symmetric shadowcasting over whole numbers, where a cell blocks when something `opaque` in it stands taller than the eye. Downward it shows what lies under every seen cell that has no floor and holds nothing opaque. Upward it shows each storey above as seen from the cell over the viewer's head, for as long as nothing closes those cells; this replaces "upward through open sky". The viewer looks down past whatever shares its own cell. A storey that holds nothing within reach is marked whole without reading a cell. The answer is a `FovSet` of bits by offset from the viewer, kept by the caller. | Design, from building world queries | Looking straight down and straight up through seen cells is not symmetric: someone hovering over the roofs saw the street and the street did not see them. Seeing each upper storey from the cell overhead is the exact mirror of looking down, so two viewers on different storeys see each other or neither does, and the property test covers it. Whole numbers keep the result the same on every machine. | 02 §22. A view and a line of sight can differ by a cell at a corner. Radius 30: about 70 µs in the open, 45 µs for one open storey, a few µs indoors, 0.1 µs when nothing has changed. |
| D-314 | A ramp, stairs or ladder joins its cell to the cell directly above it, and holds up whoever is in that upper cell: the cell's footing is the connection, though it is not standable by the rule of 02 §5.2. `neighbours` reports raw geometry for the ten cells round a cell: flags, footing, the height in the way of walking, the connection, liquid, rough, and whether what is in the way has a state in which it is not (`opens`). | Design, from building world queries | 02 named the flags and did not say which cells they join. Maps put stairs up and stairs down in one column (10 §3.1), and a ramp generated where ground rises a storey has open air above it, so the upper cell needs something to stand on. | 02 §5.2, §22. Whatever decides that an occupant is unsupported (W7, W14) asks `Cosmos::footing`, not `standable` alone. |
| D-315 | The world offers a search over cells, `Cosmos::find_path`: A* over the ten steps, with the mover's rules given as a `MoveRules` (which cells it can be in, what each step costs) and `MoveProfile` as a plain walker that does not cut corners. Costs are metres; a rule's cost below a step's own length is raised to it. Ties are broken by the estimate of the whole path, then of what is left, then by position, and steps are tried in listed order. The budget is 20,000 cells unless the caller says. | Design, from building world queries | 02 §22 left path-finding to document 09, whose lowest level is exactly this search and whose package comes four waves later; movement, the first walk and tests need paths before then. Keeping the rules outside the search lets a mind answer from belief (01 R11) and the simulation's movement definitions replace the plain walker. | 02 §22. 09 §5 builds its hierarchy over this in `clash-sim`. 60 steps: about 50 µs in the open, 60 µs through a building. |
| D-316 | A kept answer carries a `RevStamp`: the structure revisions of the chunks its reads were confined to, with a chunk that holds only its tract's fill counted as revision zero and an unloaded tract as absent. A `FovSet` asked the same question of an unchanged world returns without working anything out, and a `Path` says whether it is current. Loading a tract must start its chunks' revisions where no earlier loading of that tract left them. | Design, from building world queries | 02 §22 said cached results are keyed on chunk revisions and nothing more. Revisions restart when a column is built, so a tract unloaded, changed by a pending edit and loaded again could look unchanged to a cache that did not look in between. | 02 §6.2, §22. Package W8 owes the last sentence; until then whoever keeps an answer across the unloading of its tracts drops it. |
| D-300 | `WorldCtx` holds the cosmos, the timeline and the event sink; the definitions are the cosmos's own. Every edit returns a `Result`, and an element setter returns what was there. A replaced element starts undamaged; `set_material` keeps the element and its damage. `set_damage` is the plain write that damage packets are built on. `add_decal` takes a kind and an amount and stamps cause and tick itself. Marks and cell variables emit events of their own: `DecalAdded`, `DecalsRemoved`, `CellVarChanged`. | Design, from building world edits | The sketch gave edits no way to refuse an unloaded cell, and a decal that carried a second cause beside the edit's. Old damage on a new wall is wrong, and on a transmuted one is right. Nothing told a renderer or a rule that a mark or a variable had changed. | 02 §7.2, §7.4. `WorldEvent` is an enum with named fields, marked non-exhaustive; rules name events in snake case. |
| D-301 | A stability check is asked for when a block or floor is removed, replaced by another definition, made of another material, changes state so as to stop being `load_bearing` or an `anchor` or to lose volume, or takes more damage. Adding an element and a state change that keeps those ask for none. The queue holds one request for each cell, the first, and is state. A write that would stretch a column's stored chunks past `COLUMN_MAX_CHUNKS` is refused as out of reach. | Design, from building world edits | "Removed or weakened" was not defined, and a door opening must not start a structural evaluation of the building around it. A column stores its chunks in an unbroken run, so one write far above a tract could otherwise allocate without limit. | 02 §7.2, §14.2. Package W13 takes requests with `Cosmos::take_stability_requests` and adds the wake-up that merges them. |
| D-302 | `place`, `relocate`, `remove` are functions of `WorldCtx`. A footprint is a box from the occupant's cell, which is its corner with the lowest coordinates, at most 16 cells a side. A plane mask is 32 bits; plane 0 is the built-in `engine:default` and a plane's id is its bit. Index entries carry kind, planes and flags. An occupant where its tract stores no chunk is indexed beside the chunks. Queries list `(cell, occupant)` pairs and treat unloaded space as empty. `relocate_as` changes a footprint and `update_occupant` what the world knows of an occupant. | Design, from building world edits | They emit events and need the tick. 02 §8.1 said a mask was eight bits and §8.2 that there are thirty-two planes. A flier far above the ground should not make a tract store thousands of empty chunks. Every occupant is in loaded space, so an unloaded cell holds no one and a query need not fail. | 02 §8.1, §8.2, §6.3. The occupant index and records are not hashed or saved. A chunk's empty index is one pointer, keeping a uniform chunk under 200 bytes. |
| D-303 | Areas and sites are tables of the cosmos, with ids unique in the cosmos; an area names its grid and a site its realm. A radius shape carries its centre. An occupant is inside an area when any cell of its footprint is. Gravity, drift and the clock are each taken from the highest-priority area that sets them, and of equals the one made first. An area anchored to an entity follows `relocate`. `Cosmos::ambient` reports gravity also as a vector, and drift as the area set it. An area touches at most 4096 tracts and lists at most 65,536 cells. | Design, from building world edits | Ambient conditions are read for a cell without knowing its realm, and an anchored area can follow its anchor into another grid. The sketch's `Radius(m)` had no centre. A time-stop over a low-gravity room should stop time and keep the gravity. | 02 §4, §8.3, §8.4. A tract belongs to at most one site. What velocity a vortex gives at a cell is left to the medium (11 §4.7). The journal span and a subgrid anchor are stored only. |
| D-304 | `enclose` fills breadth first by steps north, east, south, west, up and down. A block covering 0.95 of its cell stops it, a floor that is not a grate stops it between storeys, and a barrier edge closes a space. The leak named is the cell before a `void` edge or link, else the first cell under open sky the fill reached, else the first cell past the limit. An enclosed area is worked out again only when a change matters to the fill, in a cell of it or beside, above or below one. `AreaClosed` reports a breach mended. | Design, from building world edits | The specification said what stops the fill and not how it walks, which cell a refusal names, or what counts as a change on the edge. Diagonal steps would leak through the corners of walls. Nothing reported the mark being cleared. | 02 §8.3, §7.4. A change that only opens an area already breached is skipped. An area whose space is partly unloaded when a change is made is left as it was. |
| D-305 | When an edit leaves an occupant no room, the world's rule is: a block or fixture that newly makes the cell `walk_blocked` displaces the creatures, items and other occupants in it, not devices. Each goes to the nearest position within two cells, by rings and then storey by storey and row by row, where none of its cells is `walk_blocked` or holds an occupant on a shared plane that blocks movement, and which can be stood in if its present cell can. | Design, from building world edits | 02 §23 promised displacement and left "cannot share a cell with" and "nearest" open. The world knows no mover. A device stands in the cell of its own element. | 02 §23. `edit.displace_range` is the constant `DISPLACE_RANGE_CELLS` until settings are bound. |
| D-306 | `clash-world` depends on `serde_json`, to carry an area kind's `rules`, `leak` and variables as written until the loader and the rule runtime read them. | Design, from building world edits | The loader was being written at the same time. The same reason and the same crate as D-294. | `area_kind` and `plane` are definition types built by `WorldDefs::build` like those of D-283, and register with the loader when they do. |
| D-330 | `clash-data` depends on `clash-expr`: "clash-data → core, expr". Expression fields, the compile stage and the `function` and `variable` definition types live in `clash_data::expr`. `clash-expr` still depends only on `clash-core`, and every crate above reaches it through `clash-data`. | Owner, through the build's orchestrator | 04 §7.3 says expressions are compiled during loading, and the two crates were built side by side without knowing each other. A third crate between them would have had to own both the field types and the loader's stage. | 01 §3; 04 §7.3. The loader is handed a registry the simulation filled, so the data layer still names no kind of subject and no property. |
| D-331 | A definition type states what is in scope in each of its expression fields in `Def::compile`, by building a `Scope` and handing the field to `CompileCx::compile`. A scope names kinds and types as a mod writes them. `Expr<T>`, `Cond` and `Text` each hold what was written, where, the JSON path and, once compiled, the compiled form. A field that is not handed over is not compiled. | Design, from building expression fields | The context of a field (05 §7.3) is knowledge of the type that owns it, and often differs between two fields of one struct. Naming kinds by text lets a crate below the simulation declare a scope without the registry's ids. An attribute on the field could not express a scope computed from the definition, such as an event's subjects. | 04 §7.1, §7.3; 05 §7.3, §14. `Def::compile` runs after every definition is read and before `validate`. A new reserved reader, `by_site`, gives a field its place and JSON path. |
| D-332 | The loader is given the registry with `Loader::expr_registry` and works on a copy. Before compiling it adds the ids of every definition type, what each crate's registry steps add, `variable` declarations and `function` definitions. The finished registry is shared from `Defs`. `function` and `variable` are registered with `clash_data::expr::register_defs`, like any crate's types. A loader given no registry has the built-in functions only. | Design, from building expression fields | The registry of 05 §5.3 is filled by the simulation, which sits above the loader, while ids, functions and variables are only known once mods are read. Registering the two types explicitly keeps `DefTypes::new()` empty, which tests and tools rely on. | 04 §7.3. The application and the tools call `register_defs`. Definition ids written in place (`a#b`) are left out of the registry. Events that mods define arrive through a registry step of the crate that owns them. |
| D-333 | The compiler's diagnostics are given codes of group `06`, in a table of the compile stage's own: `060` and `061` syntax, `062` and `063` names, `064` types, `066` purity and belief, `067` what the stage finds itself, `069` what goes wrong at run time. A warning that a mod's `strict_vars` makes an error keeps its code. | Design, from building expression fields | D-267 gave codes a letter and four digits and left `06` free; the compiler's own codes have three. One form lets `explain` and editors treat every diagnostic alike. | 04 §8.2; 05 §14. `clash_data::expr::CODES`; `clash_data::explain` and `clash-tools explain` read both tables. A test fails if a compiler code has none. |
| D-334 | Whether a function is random and whether it is safe for belief is worked out, not declared. It is random if it calls a random function or its value is a `pick` or `run` source; it is safe for belief if its body compiles in a mind context that binds only its parameters; and each follows from what it calls. A function that calls itself on every path is a load error; other recursion is refused at run time past the call depth, giving null with a warning. A function sees only its parameters. `returns` left out is `any`. A parameter's default is a constant. | Design, from building functions | 05 §12 said random is inferred and left belief unsaid, while §7.3 holds every function a mind context reaches to it. A declaration could be wrong; a body cannot. Unconditional recursion is the only kind a compiler can see without running anything. | 05 §12, §13.2. The stage registers every signature before it reads any body. `clash-expr` gained `Registry::set_function_traits` and the faults `CallDepth` (`X908`) and `Unavailable` (`X909`). Until queries are built a function whose value is a query counts as not safe. |
| D-335 | A variable's `scope` names a kind of subject, and `cell` is the kind `loc`. With no `default` the variable reads as null until set and its type is the declared one or null. A declaration may say `belief_safe`. A variable is not declared in place. `strict_vars` is a key of `mod.json` and applies to what that mod wrote. | Design, from building variables | 05 §6 named the scopes and the keys without saying what a missing default means or how a declared variable is read in a mind context (D-273 gave variables the flag). Strictness that followed the definition and not its author would make one mod's choice break another's rules. | 05 §6; 04 §3.2. |
| D-336 | In an expression field a JSON string is the text or the id itself where the field wants one, a quantity where it wants a number and the string is one, and otherwise an expression. A JSON list is a list literal. A condition's structure is kept as a tree whose strings are compiled one by one. A value source is one object with exactly one head key; `call` takes its arguments by name and is built; a source over a query, `exists` and `run` are read, kept and give null with a warning until queries are built. | Design, from building expression fields | 05 §7.1 gave `"250 ms"` as a literal, and the expression language has no compound units or offsets, which data does. Compiling each string of a condition by itself keeps every position exact, and evaluates the same: null counts as false in `and`, `or` and `not`. | 05 §7.1, §7.2, §7.4. Packages K5 and K6 replace the kept query with a compiled one. |
| D-340 | A console value is a number with an optional unit, a word, a reference, a quoted value, JSON or raw text. A quoted value is on one line with two escapes, `\"` and `\\`. JSON runs from `{` to the matching `}`, over lines in a file. Raw text ends at the first space outside brackets and outside single-quoted strings. A `#` where an argument could start begins a comment. References gain `@e:17v2` and a general `@kind:name`. | Design, from building the console | 14 §3.2 listed the forms and showed unquoted expressions with spaces in brackets (`watch dist(self, @sel)`) without saying where a value ends, what a quote may hold or how a comment starts. An expression's own strings are single-quoted, so two escapes are enough. | 14 §3.2. `clash_sim::console::parse_line`, `print_line`. The canonical form is a fixed point of parse and print. |
| D-341 | A bare word that names a parameter not yet given, followed by a positional value, names that parameter. A parameter may take the rest of the line. `as=` is accepted by every command and is not a parameter. A command name is one word or two, the longer tried first. A quoted value given to a `string` is the text itself and to anything else an expression. A verb whose name a built-in command has is not registered again and is reached with `do`. | Design, from building the console | The specification's own lines need each of these: `spawn town_guard at @c:…`, `eval self.pool.blood.frac`, `as=g1`, `map export`, and `run`, which is both a command of 14 §3.3 and a verb of 05 §10.5. | 14 §3.2, §3.3. `CommandTable::bind`, `CommandTable::add_verbs`, which returns the names left out. |
| D-342 | The console, the test-script format and the runner live in `clash_sim::console` and are written against one trait, `ConsoleHost`, which the simulation implements. The session, not the simulation, keeps the names given with `as=`, the states kept with `save`, and the records seen. A reference inside an expression is resolved by the console and handed over as a local, `$at0` onward. | Design, from building the console | Every later package needs the console and the runner to test itself, so they are built before the simulation and must not depend on what it will be. References are not part of the expression language (05 §4.1), and locals are what a context already declares. | 14 §3.3. `console::testing::FakeHost` is a host for tests. The game overlay and the terminal viewer each drive a `Session`. |
| D-343 | In a test script a refused command fails the script unless the next line is `expect reason`. `expect event` without `within=` looks at the whole run. `expect hash` may be written without a value, and then fails until blessed. Expectations are read from the line itself, so a field may have any name. A saved console session is such a script: lines carried out, and lines refused followed by `expect reason`. | Design, from building the runner | 14 §7.1 gave the lines and not these cases. A refusal nobody expected is the commonest way a script goes wrong silently, and a script cannot be written with a hash before it has been run. | 14 §3.3, §7.1. `parse_script`, `Session::check`, `Session::transcript`. |
| D-344 | "The same result" in the four run modes is the state hash after every command, and every expectation. The save-and-load mode makes one run for each split point, a split point being the end of a command: all of them for a script of at most eight commands, eight spread evenly otherwise. The threads mode runs with one thread and with four. If the straight run fails the other modes are not run. `--bless` changes hashes and frames and no other byte. | Design, from building the runner | A single pass that saves after every command cannot say which save lost something; one run per split point can, which package TL6 asks for. Comparing the hash after every command finds the first line that differs and not only the last. | 14 §7.2. `Runner`, `MAX_SPLIT_POINTS`, `DEFAULT_THREAD_COUNTS`. Supersedes the three split points of world-model package W11. |
| D-345 | (Exit code 3 superseded by D-583.) `clash-tools scenario run` exits 0 when every script passed, 1 when one failed or does not parse or none was found, 2 for a command line it did not understand, and 3 when the scripts parse and there is no simulation registered to run them against. | Design, from building the runner | Until the simulation exists the tool can only check syntax, and a caller must be able to tell "parsed" from "passed". | 14 §7.2. `clash-tools/src/scenario.rs`. |
| D-346 | Console diagnostics take the `14` group of codes: `E1401` onward for the syntax of a line, `E1421` onward for binding a line to its command, `E1441` onward for test scripts. | Design, from building the console | D-267 gives each crate that adds a stage its own table of codes. | 14 §3.2. `clash_sim::console::codes`. |
| D-420 | A component store checks an id's generation itself: it keeps the whole `EntityId` of each owner, holds at most one component for an entity slot, and answers an id of another generation as absent. Its index from slot to dense position is kept in pages of 1024 slots and needs no "empty" value, since an entry counts only if the owner at that position has the slot. Whoever destroys an entity removes its components before the slot is reused. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §3 kept bare slot indices and left the generation to the entity table, which the stores cannot see: they are built apart from the kernel and must not hand a dead entity's component to its successor. One flat index for each store costs four bytes for every entity for every component type, and a damaged save naming a huge slot would ask for gigabytes. | 06 §3; package E1. A lookup is three indexed reads and a comparison. A position always fits in 32 bits, because owners have distinct slots. A store's index is at most 32 MB of page pointers however high a slot is. |
| D-421 | Dense order is state. A new component goes to the end, a replaced one keeps its position, and a removed one's position is taken by the last. A store is saved as `(owner, component)` pairs in that order and hashed in that order; the index is rebuilt on loading and is neither saved nor hashed. Two components for one slot in a save are refused. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §3 said the order is saved and restored and did not say how removal reorders or whether the hash follows it. A system that walks a store sees the order, so sorting on save would make a run that saved differ from one that did not (R7), and a hash blind to the order would call two states equal that go on differently. Pairs let a component that takes no bytes be saved, which a bare list cannot (01 §11.1). | 06 §3, invariant B8; package E1. Stores with the same contents reached by different histories hash differently. Nothing may rely on a store being in id order. |
| D-422 | The component types are one list in `clash_sim::component`, from which a macro writes the struct of stores (`Components`), `ComponentKind` with its names, `ComponentBundle` (one entity's components, out of the stores) and the `Component` trait's implementations. There is no registry filled at run time and no type-erased store. A component's module reports what a change did through its return value, and there is no change log. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §3 said each type "is registered" without saying where. Content never adds component types (D-064), so the set is known when the engine is compiled, and a struct gives a fixed order for saving and hashing, typed access, and two stores borrowed at once for nothing. 06 §3 asks for events from the owning module, which a returned report serves without the stores knowing the event queue. | 06 §3; packages E1 to E9. A later package adds a type by one line at the end of the list; that changes the layout of the saved components, so the blob that holds them takes a new version. `Components::remove_entity` is the one call that clears an entity from every store, and its bundle is what a stored entity keeps (06 §12.3). |
| D-423 | `Physical` stores what a thing itself measures (own mass, volume, height, footprint, cross-section), supplied by the body or the item, each finite and at least zero. It derives one thing: the mass of everything inside, summed over the contents in order of id. An entity with no `Physical` weighs nothing and passes on what is inside it. A sum is made again from its parts whenever one changes, for the container and every container around it, and is never adjusted by a difference. The cached sum is saved and hashed. Containment is read through a trait, and a chain deeper than 64, or one that closes on itself, is refused. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §3.1 said mass is "own plus everything it holds" and left the rest open. Floating-point sums depend on order and do not undo, so a cache kept by adding and subtracting would drift from recomputation and break invariant B3; a fixed order makes the result independent of how the kernel lists contents. Saving the cache means loading needs no rebuild and no containment, and hashing it means a cache gone wrong shows. Size depends on parts, scale and posture, which only the body knows. | 06 §3.1, invariant B3; packages E1, E4, E8, K1. The kernel implements `Containment` over places and calls `physical::refresh` on the old and new container when it relocates. Changing a mass costs one pass up the containment chain, each level summing its contents. |
| D-424 | `Identity` names its definition by a pair, the definition type's number in the expression registry and the dense id within the type (`DefKey`), and keeps a name and a description only when the entity has its own: absent means the definition's. Templates are listed in the order applied. Tags stay on the kernel entity. | Design, from building the beta | An entity may be made from a creature, an item or a type a later package adds, so one typed id cannot name it, and the pair is what a property hands to the expression language (D-271). A copy of the definition's name on every entity would cost text for each of a thousand unnamed things and would go stale when a mod is reloaded. 03 §4.2 already gives tags to the kernel. | 06 §3.1; packages E1, E9, K4, K7. Both numbers are runtime ids: the simulation's save path writes them through the save's id table. `TemplateId` is declared with `Identity` and becomes the id of the `template` type in E9. |
| D-350 | The view model is plain data that only knows what it is told: cells by `observe_cell` and its companions, sprites, heard markers and the shapes of grids. It holds no handle on the world. The omniscient mode is one function in a module of its own, `clash_scene::view::omniscient::fill`, which is given the world and a box of cells; it is the only code in the scene that names the world's read API, and a test that reads the crate's source keeps it so. Cells are kept in slabs of one storey of one tract. A single item is a sprite and a pile is a mark on the cell. A sprite is known by a number its feeder chooses. Two view models that hold the same knowledge are equal. | Design, from building the view model | 12 §5 said what the view model is for and not what it holds or how it is fed, and perception and playback, which will feed it, are not built. Rule R11 is easier to keep by construction than by review: what cannot reach truth cannot leak it. Equality by knowledge is what the check of invariant V4 needs. | 12 §5; plans/first-playable.md U4. Perception and playback call the observe functions; the application calls the omniscient fill for a debugging view. `clash-scene` names `clash-world` as a dependency of its own, which its arrow to `clash-sim` already allowed (D-253). |
| D-351 | What a cell shows is completed where 12 §6.1 left it open. The list decides the character and its colour; the background is that of the highest entry that gives one. An element whose glyph gives no `ch` is not drawn. A cell never seen shows nothing, and so does a known open cell over one never seen. Each storey down keeps 70% of the brightness. Light scales brightness in a straight line from 25% at none to the whole. A remembered cell keeps a quarter of its saturation and 55% of its brightness, and anything seen through a remembered cell is drawn as remembered. A decal tints the character of terrain by up to 70%, and a field too weak for its glyph tints the background by up to half. | Design, from building the view model | The specification gave the order and no amounts, and two builds must draw the same frame (12 V8). A seen cell that went black in the dark would be indistinguishable from one never seen. Looking down past a cell nobody has seen would show the player what their character does not know. | 12 §6.1. The amounts are constants of `clash_scene::view`, not settings; `look_down` is a setting. Golden frames change if any of them does. |
| D-352 | Dimming reaches a sixteen-colour terminal through the fallback colour, not through an attribute: the character of a remembered cell falls back to dark grey, and that of a cell at less than half its brightness to the darker of its colour's pair. Backgrounds keep their fallback. | Design, from building the view model | Brightness is carried by the exact colour, which sixteen colours cannot show, and nothing essential may be conveyed only by what a terminal cannot draw (D-077). The dim attribute on top of a colour already darkened would dim twice where 24-bit colour is shown. | 12 §6.1, §4.2. On sixteen colours, what is remembered is grey and what is seen is in colour. |
| D-353 | A map view zoomed out by `n`, from 1 to 16, shows for each block of `n` by `n` cells its most significant cell: significance is the order of 12 §6.1, with what shows from below under a floor, the backdrop under that and what is not known last; of equals, the first row by row. Blocks are aligned to multiples of `n`. The factor is the camera's times the view's own `zoom`. The camera's centre is the middle cell of a view. | Design, from building the views | "The most significant glyph" was not defined. Using the order the cell already uses means a zoomed-out map never hides what a single cell would show first. Aligned blocks keep the picture still while the cursor moves inside a block. | 12 §6.2. |
| D-354 | A section view cuts along a line through the camera's centre, west to east or north to south as the camera says, with east or south to the right, one column per metre and one row per storey, higher storeys above and the camera's storey on the middle row. It does not look down: an empty cell is air. A floor is drawn with the theme mark `section_floor`, in its own colours. A section is not zoomed. | Design, from building the views | 12 §6.2 gave one sentence. A floor's glyph is how it looks from above; edge on it is a line, and a row of dots between two walls reads as nothing. | 12 §6.2, §6.5. Which line is chosen is the camera's, not yet a field of the widget. |
| D-355 | An ambience's `tint` is a coloured filter, each channel multiplied by the tint's over 255, and `dim` then takes that share of the brightness. Its particles are read, checked and kept, and not drawn until playback owns the scene's clock. An overlay's `cell` is kept as its source and evaluated through one interface (`OverlayValues`) that is handed the view model's cell and nothing else; a view names an overlay by qualified id, or one of the engine's five by bare name; `light` shades the background from black to the theme colour `overlay_light` by the cell's light level. `networks`, `areas`, `jobs` and `temperature` are known names that draw nothing yet. | Design, from building the views | A filter leaves black backgrounds black, where mixing toward the tint would lift every empty cell of a terminal. Bindings are package U6b, and an overlay that could ask the world would break rule R11. | 12 §6.2, §6.4. The fields of both definitions are tabled. Their mistakes are found by `clash_scene::check_defs`, at the value that is wrong. |
| D-356 | The look of every element state and material is compiled once per theme into `WorldGlyphs`, by layer, definition and state. A colour that a world glyph or a material names must exist in every loaded theme, and a glyph's `ch` must be one character: `clash_scene::check_defs` reports either at the file that wrote it, and with no theme loaded checks against the engine's plain theme over the loaded `color` definitions. `mods/core` therefore defines the colours its materials name that are not terminal colours: `gray`, `dark_gray`, `light_gray` and `brown`. The views draw their own parts with the theme marks `pile`, `suspected`, `heard`, `backdrop` and `section_floor`, the style `cursor` and the colour `overlay_light`, each with a plain default. | Design, from building the views | Drawing must look nothing up by name (12 §4.2). The world stored glyph and colour names as text that nothing had ever resolved, and four of the names in `core` were known to no theme. | 12 §4.3, §6.5; `mods/core/data/colors.json`. Shapes, `by_level`, `bold` and `layer` are not part of the world's `GlyphSpec` and are not drawn for elements. Fields and decals have ids and no definitions: `WorldGlyphs::set_field_look` and `set_decal_tint` are where their looks go. |
| D-400 | An action definition is resolved and compiled in place, and the struct the registry holds is what the runtime walks: phase ids become positions when the definition is finished, types and expressions are compiled after, and every expression of one action is compiled against one layout of slots. The one bound subject is `self`, the actor, at slot 0; the locals are `$window` (on a reaction), then the roles, the parameters and the variables, each in the order written. A set of channels is a bitset of 256 bits and a channel's dense id is eight bits wide, so there are at most 256 channel definitions. | Design, from building action definitions | The loader keeps one value for each definition, so a second, compiled table would be a copy to keep in step. A fixed layout lets a running action be a definition id, a phase index and a vector of values. A set that holds every channel id cannot fail to hold one, and the loader already refuses more definitions than an id can count. | 03 §6.1, §6.2. `clash_sim::action`: `ActionDef`, `ChannelSet`, `PhaseIndex`, `Local`. |
| D-401 | The variables of an action that are not parameters or roles are declared in its `vars`, each with a type and, if wanted, a constant it starts as; without one it starts as null. A local that is not declared is a load error where it is read. | Design, from building action definitions | 03 §6.4 gave action instances locals and used `$power` without saying where it came from. The compiler refuses an unknown local, which is the check that catches a misspelt name, and a variable never set would be null in the first sum made with it. | 03 §6.1, §6.4. The locals `strike` leaves (`$landed`, `$absorbed`, `$passed`, 07 §3.4) must be declared by the action, or added to its scope by combat package C2 for an action with `attacks`: the example in 07 §3.1 does not load until one of the two is done. |
| D-402 | An entry of a phase's `next` has an optional `if` and either `goto` with a phase id or `"finish": true`. If no entry holds the default applies: the following phase, and the end of the action after the last. A phase that no path from the first leads to is a load error; an entry written after one that always holds is a warning. A window's `closes` names another phase than the one that opens it, which some path from that phase leads to; if the action ends without entering it the window closes then. | Design, from building action definitions | 03 had no way to end an action from a phase that is not the last written, so the `release` of its own charge-and-release example ran on into `fizzle`, and it did not say what happens when no entry holds. A phase that can never run is a misspelt `goto` far more often than it is meant. | 03 §6.4, §7.1. `Transition`, `Next`, `Phase::exits`. |
| D-403 | A phase's `tick` is `every` and `do`. `until` is tested at ticks, so `until` on a phase with no `tick` is a load error, as are `max` and `until` on a phase that is not held. Tempo divides `time` and nothing else. A constant duration below zero, and a constant tempo or tick interval of zero or below, are load errors. | Design, from building action definitions | 03 named `tick`, `until` and `max` without their fields or their bounds, and §6.5 defined tempo by `time` alone, which a held phase does not have. A condition that is never tested is a phase that never ends. | 03 §6.4, §6.5. |
| D-404 | A parameter is written as its type or as an object with `type`, `of`, `min`, `max`, `default` and `check`. Types are written as the expression language writes them, with `cell` for a location and `list` for a list of what `of` says. A default is compiled in the mind context of the actor and may read only the locals before it; `check` is compiled there too, pure, and gives null or the reason. `requires` and `fail_text` are compiled in the mind context and pure, and `requires` is tested against truth when the action starts; `requires_at_start`, `tempo` and the fields of a phase read truth. | Design, from building action definitions | 05 §7.3 puts an action's `requires`, defaults and `check` in the mind context without saying which fields read truth, and 03 gave parameters no field table. Defaults are worked out in the order written, so one that read a later parameter would read null. | 03 §6.1, §6.3. `Param`, `ListBounds`, `ActionDef::mind_scope`. The `choice` parameter type of 08 §9.2 is left to the abilities plan. |
| D-405 | The first role of a joint action is whoever starts it: its participant is `self` and it cannot be forced. The participants of the other roles are given with the command by the role's name, as parameters are. A joint action has at least two roles and no `uses` of its own. Each role is a local of kind `entity`. | Design, from building action definitions | 03 §8 said phases run on the clock of the first role and that `self` is whoever started the action, without saying that these are the same participant, or how the others are named in a command. | 03 §8. `Role`, `LocalSource::Role`. |
| D-406 | A window kind's `notice` defaults to zero, `noticed_if` to true and `reach` to zero. Both expressions read truth and bind `self` (the candidate), `source` (whose action or rule opened the window, or null) and `window`, in that order. A phase's window names those it is for by expressions that each give an entity. A channel may carry a `name`. | Design, from building action definitions | 03 §7.1 named the fields by example and §7.2 bound only `self`. A reason that an action cannot start must be able to say which channel in words a player reads. | 03 §6.2, §7.1. `WindowKindDef::scope`, `ChannelDef`. |
| D-407 | Until the rule runtime compiles effects, an effect list of an action is kept as the JSON it was written as, in order and with the place of every value, behind one type that the rule runtime replaces. The reasons the action system gives are constants with a code in the `engine` namespace, a sentence as a text template and the values carried. A cost, a cooldown and a rank belong to a grant (08 §5.1) and not to the action, so their reasons are the abilities plan's. The definition types are registered by `clash_sim::action::register_defs`. | Design, from building action definitions | Effects need the rule runtime, which needs actions. The `reason` definition type is a tooling package's, and 14 §5 asks that no refusal lack a reason from the first one given. | 03 §13; 14 §5. `EffectsSource`, `clash_sim::action::reason`. The crate root may re-export the function when the kernel lands. |
| D-360 | The simulation's world, timeline and entity table are private. Ground truth is read through one type, `Truth`, from `Sim::truth()`; simulation code changes the world through `Sim::world`, a closure given the world's context, after which the kernel passes on what the world reported and follows it. A world that already exists is handed over with `Sim::from_parts`. | Design, from building the kernel | 03 §4.1 made every part a public field. Whoever holds the cosmos and the timeline can move an occupant or destroy a clock without the kernel knowing, and a read of truth could not be told from any other. A context that is handed out and taken back leaves no moment at which place and index disagree. | 03 §4.1. Nothing a character decides by may hold a `Truth` (01 R11). `Sim::view` of 01 §5.3 is not built: it is the view limited by perception. |
| D-361 | The kernel entity also holds a presence: the kind, planes, mass, flags and footprint the world is told. Its tags are strings. `place`, `presence`, `control`, `clock` and `anchor` are read through accessors and each changed by one function; an owned kernel entity never leaves the crate. | Design, from building the kernel | The world neither saves nor hashes occupants (D-302), so something must remember how to place each again. A loader's tag is a number in one set of definitions; a rule may add a tag no definition carries and a save names tags by string. A public field cannot keep an index or a clock tree in step. | 03 §4.2. Document 06's `Physical` writes presence with `Sim::set_presence`. `actions` is a plain list rather than a small vector. |
| D-362 | `relocate` decides what to do to the occupant index by what the index holds. What an entity contains moves with it without events of its own. Nesting is at most 64 deep. An entity that leaves the world, and what it contains, has its wake-ups cancelled. Removing an entity first puts what it held where it was. The kernel follows an occupant the world displaced. `reindex` rebuilds the index after a load, and what cannot be placed leaves the world and is reported. | Design, from building the kernel | 03 §4.2 named the function and left these open. An index emptied by an unloaded tract should mend, not refuse. T9 needs a rule the kernel can keep by itself; timers that should survive are the business of whoever stores the entity. Contents lost inside nothing would be unreachable. | 03 §4.2. Portal events wait for portals. Package T7 takes timers off the timeline before it stores an entity. |
| D-363 | Which clock an entity is on is worked out from where it is, not stored; what is stored is which wake-ups are an entity's, and they are scheduled through `Sim::schedule_for`. A contained entity is on the clock its outermost container is on. An entity out of the world has no clock unless it has its own. Anchoring gives a personal clock at rate 1 where there is none; taking the personal clock away lifts the anchor. An entity has own time, summed over the clocks it has been on. | Design, from building the kernel | A stored assignment is one more thing to go stale when an area is made, moved or removed; the world already answers the question. The timeline cannot find an entity's wake-ups by itself. 03 §3.4 said "as its outermost container" without saying whose clock that is, and gave no meaning to an anchor without a personal clock or to time "since this entity last decided" across clocks. | 03 §3.4, §5.2. A change of an area's priority takes effect at the entity's next change of clock. Time on a clock destroyed by removing its area is counted to the last change of clock. |
| D-364 | `advance` returns a request to the application before a decision. While a decision is waiting it still runs what is due at the current tick, and then returns the decision of the lowest entity id. `Work(n)` yields only if more is due. After 100,000 wake-ups at one tick it reports a fault and yields. Faults go to the output and to no handler. Wake-ups are dispatched to one plain function for each domain. | Design, from building the kernel | Requests raised by two wake-ups of one tick could not otherwise be returned in entity order (03 §5.2), and time still does not move (T8). Putting wake-ups off to the next tick needs the timeline to postpone them without changing their handles, which it cannot do yet. A fault delivered to handlers could raise more faults. | 03 §4.3, §5.2; 05 §13.2. Handlers are not state: a loaded simulation has the defaults. |
| D-365 | An input log entry carries the tick and the number of wake-ups handled when its command was accepted. A refused command leaves the state as it was and is not logged. The kernel's commands are debug commands to create, move and remove an entity, set an element, set control and schedule a wake-up; `Command` and `DebugCommand` are open sets. `submit` pumps events before it returns. | Design, from building the kernel | With `Work(n)` a command can be accepted in the middle of a tick's wake-ups, and the tick alone does not say where. A refusal that left a trace would make a replay, which does not repeat it, diverge. | 03 §4.4. The log does not yet carry state hashes at intervals (14 §8.1). |
| D-366 | A `SimEvent` is a stamp (universal tick, clock, that clock's local time, cause) and a body, a Rust enum with a name, named subjects in order and discrete fields read by name. `actor` and `instigator` are bound from the cause. The world's occupant is bound as `target`. The output waiting to be drained and the input log are saved and not hashed. | Design, from building the kernel | 03 §12 said what an event carries and not on which clock or how subjects are named. Rules attached to an area kind already write `target` for who entered (05 §9.3). Draining the output is not a step of the simulation and must not change its hash. | 03 §12, §13. An event with no entity and no place is on universal time. |
| D-367 | A `Reason` is a stable dotted code, a sentence and the values involved by name. Each package lists its codes with their meaning in a table of its own, as diagnostics do (D-267). | Design, from building the kernel | 14 §5 described reasons and left their form open. One type for commands, relocation, clocks and events lets a screen and a rule treat every refusal alike. | 14 §5. `KERNEL_REASONS` is the kernel's table; the generated reference reads every table. |
| D-368 | Event types are kept by the simulation: the kernel's and the world's from tables, a mod's from `event` definitions with `subjects`, `fields`, `hook`, `mutable` and `doc`, and a package's by registration. A raised event is checked against its type. A field is null, a plain value or a subject. Handlers are registered globally or attached to a subject, with priority, order, filter, `as` and `skip_if_consumed`; they are indexed by the first entry of their filter. Cancelling a hook stops delivery and raises `event_cancelled`. Hooks nest eight deep. | Design, from building the kernel | 05 §8 gave the behaviour and left the interface to the rule runtime, which is built later and must have one to register through. The expression registry cannot hold the types until subject kinds exist. An index on one entry is enough to make filtered handlers free for events they cannot match. | 05 §8. Handlers are Rust closures until K5 and are not state. `clash_sim::register_defs` registers the `event` type with the loader. The event's name is its qualified id. |
| D-369 | The simulation saves its own state as one blob of kind `CSIM`: everything but the definitions and the world. Loading takes the definitions and the world it was saved with; `reindex` follows. The layout has a version that is raised with any change to a state type. | Design, from building the kernel | The world has a store of its own (02 §11) and the cosmos is not a serde type. 03 §4.1 asked for the whole simulation to serialise without saying where the boundary is. | 01 §11; 03 §4.1. Definitions in saved state are dense ids until package K7 remaps them through the save's id table. |
| D-370 | The generation interfaces (`RegionSurveyor`, `TractRealizer`, `GenCtx`, `RealmInfo`, `TractRecords3x3`, `RealizedTract`) are defined in `clash-world`, in `clash_world::generate`, and implemented and re-exported by `clash-gen`. A generator is a `WorldGenerator`: both traits, `Send`, `Sync` and `'static`. The records a realiser is shown are as the survey wrote them, with the devastation and without how far along its life a tract is or what is waiting for it. `RealizedTract` holds a `Column`, not chunks and fills. | Design, from building tract loading | Loading a tract runs the realiser and belongs to the world, which cannot depend on `clash-gen` (01 §3); 02 §1 and the plan put the traits in `clash-gen`. A realiser that could see whether a neighbour had been loaded could make cells that depend on the order of loading. A chunk can only be built through a `ColumnBuilder`, which gives a column. | 02 §9.3; plans/world-model.md W8. No input of a realiser holds a cell, and a generator cannot borrow the cosmos: neighbouring cells are out of reach by type. What spans tracts is agreed in records or follows from position. |
| D-371 | A realm definition may give its generator parameters, `generator_params`: a map of values carried as written and read by the generator. Generators register a factory by name in a `GeneratorRegistry`, and `bind` builds the generator of every realm definition into a `Generators`, which loading takes. A realm that names no generator, or one nobody registered, is refused when a tract of it is to be generated, not when definitions load. A wrong parameter is a `GenError` with the realm definition's id and the parameter's path. | Design, from building tract loading | The test generators must be built only from definitions that data names (01 R2), and document 10's `worldgen` definition type, which will hold a generator's settings, is several packages away. `mods/core` already holds a realm whose generator does not exist; a world may hold realms that are never generated. | 02 §4.1, §9.3, §23. `core:test_city` names every state its city is built from. The linter does not bind generators yet, so a wrong parameter is reported with definition and path and without file and line; `Generators::param_errors` is the list it would show. When `worldgen` definitions land, a name that is not a registered generator is looked up among them. |
| D-372 | A batch of tracts is realised through `realize_batch` on a `BatchRunner`, a trait of the world with one function that runs numbered jobs on whatever threads it likes. Each result goes into a place of its own and the batch comes back in job order. `SerialRunner` is the world's; `clash_gen::RayonRunner` runs a batch on a `rayon` pool. `load_tracts` plans a batch, realises it, then installs the tracts one by one in the order asked. | Design, from building tract loading | The world was given no dependency on `rayon`, generators live above it, and the outcome must not depend on scheduling (01 §4). Keeping the threads behind one small trait leaves the world free of them and makes the order of results a matter of construction, not of care. | 02 §9.3, §9.4. `rayon` joins `clash-gen` (01 §13). Realising the same tracts in any order on 1, 2 and 7 threads gives the same hashes, tested. A `test_city` tract takes about 0.4 ms on one thread. |
| D-373 | Unloading is refused while any occupant has a cell in the tract, and changes and emits nothing then. `TractUnloading` is emitted by `unload_tract` only when it goes ahead. Reconciliation is what joins the steps of 02 §9.5: for a tract that is to go and holds occupants it emits `TractUnloading` once and leaves the tract loaded, and the first reconciliation that finds the tract empty unloads it without a second event. A tract announced, emptied and wanted again is unloaded and loaded again in one call. | Design, from building tract loading | 02 §9.5 has the simulation remove occupants between two steps of one procedure, and the world never calls upward (01 R4). A refusal that had already told the simulation to take its entities out would not be a refusal. Unloading and reloading keeps `TractUnloading` and `TractLoaded` in pairs, so the simulation never has to undo having emptied a tract. | 02 §9.5, §10.2. The simulation answers `TractUnloading` by removing its occupants from that tract before the next safe point. `Reconciled::waiting` lists the tracts still held up. |
| D-374 | A tract is stored when its column has changed since it was loaded, as one blob of kind `TRCT`, version 1: the two fills, the chunk height of the first stored chunk, and the chunks without those at either end that hold only the fill, each as its three element layers with compacted palettes and its four sparse layers. The codec is two functions, `encode_column` and `decode_column`. A tract that has not changed is not stored and is generated again. `TractStore::put` and `delete` take effect for later `get`s at once. | Design, from building tract loading | 02 §9.4 and §9.5 cannot be built without somewhere to put a changed tract, and persistence (W10) is a later package. Two functions are the smallest seam it can replace. Trimming pure-fill chunks makes equal columns give equal bodies, as a column's hash already does not see them. | 02 §11. The blob holds runtime ids and no entity section, fields or fingerprint: W10 replaces the body, adds the save-id table and the `redb` store. A damaged blob is an error (tested by cutting and by changing every byte). |
| D-375 | A grid keeps one number, one more than the highest any revision counter stood in a column that has left it. A column that joins the grid starts every counter there, a chunk created later starts where its column started, and a chunk height without storage counts as standing there too, not at zero. | Design, from building tract loading | D-316 required that a tract loaded again not look unchanged to a cache keyed on chunk revisions. A number for each tract ever unloaded would grow without limit; one number for the grid is enough, because every column that joins starts above every column that left. | 02 §6.2, §22. Supersedes the sentence of D-316 that a chunk holding only its fill counts as revision zero: that holds for the first columns a grid loads. Revisions stay out of hashes and saves; after a save is loaded every cache is new. |
| D-376 | Tickets are a table of the cosmos with ids that are never reused. A ticket reaches its radius from its anchor, and a hot one `WARM_MARGIN` further as warm. Anchors built are a fixed tract and an entity; an entity's ticket follows its occupant, takes on its realm, and stays where it was last while the entity is not in the world. `TicketReason::Eoc` carries a number the world gives no meaning. A tract is loaded at a ticket's reach and unloaded more than one tract beyond every reach. | Design, from building tickets | 02 §10.1 named `Body` and `Subgrid` anchors and an `EocId`, none of which exist yet, and did not say what a ticket does when its entity is inside another or changes realm. A ticket that vanished with its entity would drop the tracts around a character who climbs into a locker. | 02 §10.1. Holders of tickets for bodies and subgrids move a fixed anchor with `move_ticket` until W14 and W20a. `world.hot_radius` is `HOT_RADIUS` until settings are bound. |
| D-377 | Reconciliation is one function, `WorldCtx::reconcile_activity(io)`, which returns what was loaded, unloaded, refused, lapsed, is waiting and failed. It lapses and moves tickets, applies the budget, unloads, then loads by priority, distance from the anchor, position and realm. It does nothing when nothing has changed since the last call. It counts and unloads only tracts the loading procedure loaded: a column put in by `test_support` is outside it. | Design, from building tickets | The simulation calls it between every two events, so an idle call must cost almost nothing. Tests of every other package set their scenes with `test_support` and would lose them to the first reconciliation otherwise. | 02 §10.2. A tract that could not be loaded is reported and tried again when something changes. The activity of a tract is as of the last reconciliation. |
| D-378 | Over budget, the ticket refused is the one of the lowest priority, and of equals the one made last; never one of priority 255. A refused ticket is removed, and its holder is told by `TicketRefused` and in the result. Tracts kept only by the unloading margin go first when room is short. If pinned tickets alone want more than the budget, none is refused and the tracts that do not fit are not loaded, reported with `OverBudget` and tried again. | Design, from building tickets | 02 §10.3 gave the order by priority alone and did not say what gives when pinned tickets exceed a budget whose purpose is to bound memory.
