Refusing the newest of equals leaves standing what the world has already been built around. | 02 §10.3. Forcing a process or a body to resolve on refusal is the holder's part (W13, W14). `max_loaded_tracts` is set with `Cosmos::set_max_loaded_tracts` until settings are bound. |
| D-390 | Maps are compiled in a build stage, into `clash_gen::map::MapDefs`, from the loaded `Defs` and the world's `WorldDefs`; they are not held in `Defs`. `MapDefs::resolve(map, parameters, seed, turn)` gives a `ResolvedMap`: for each storey a grid of cells, each layer either left or given a state, with the areas, marks and the rest beside them, in the coordinates of the turned map. A map whose `if` is false resolves to nothing. `check_defs` is the same build for the linter. | Design, from building the map format | A map names element states, and those are known only once the world's tables are built from the finished `Defs` (D-321), so "compiled maps in `Defs`" could not be had. One pure function from a placement to cells is what every tract a map covers can call and get the same answer from. | 10 §3.7, §3.8; plans/worldgen.md WG1. Package WG2 writes a `ResolvedMap` through `WorldEdit` and clips it to tracts. A `MapId` is the loader's dense id. |
| D-391 | A character that is not a space writes all three elements of its cell: what its cell spec states, else what the `default` states, else nothing. `clear` empties a layer and the new `keep` leaves one as the world has it. A `set` entry and a nested map are laid over what is there layer by layer: a layer they state replaces, and one they do not keeps what lies under them, as the `default` shows through a character. | Design, from building the map format | "Everything else replaces" did not say what happens to a layer a spec leaves out. A house must clear the boulder in its parlour without saying so, a rug laid in a room must not take up the floor, and a stall set on a square must be able to leave the paving. | 10 §3.1, §3.2, §3.5. A resolved cell holds, for each layer, `None` or a state that may be `none`. |
| D-392 | Weighted alternatives chosen once for a placement are written `{ "one_of": [...], "once": true }`; a bare list is the same without `once`. A weight is a number or an expression over the parameters, worked out once for the placement. An alternative of no weight is never chosen; if all have none the first is. Alternatives do not nest. The `default`, the `roof` and a `set` entry's `spec` may be alternatives too. | Design, from building the map format | 10 §3.2 put `once` on a list, which JSON cannot carry. | 10 §3.2. |
| D-393 | A parameter is a `bool`, an `int`, a `real` or a `string`, written as `pick`, `range`, a plain value or `default`, an expression over the parameters before it, or a `type` alone for one the placement must give. `$name` in a state is the text of a `string` parameter, checked at load with every value the map's own rule can give and at resolution otherwise. Expressions in a shared palette see no parameters; a `$name` in its states is filled in by each map that lists it. A placement that gives an unknown parameter, omits a required one or gives a wrong type is refused. | Design, from building the map format | 10 §3.4 showed `pick` and `range` and said nothing of types, order or what a placement may give. An expression is compiled once against one layout of locals, and a palette is shared by maps with different parameters. | 10 §3.4. `ResolveError` carries the reason. |
| D-394 | The generation context binds a map's parameters and nothing else. Random functions are allowed in it and draw from a generator seeded, afresh for every evaluation, by `pos_hash` of the placement's seed, a domain named `gen.map.*` for the kind of choice, the cell and a number that tells apart the choices at one cell. The host that evaluates map expressions has no other generator. A call of a function that only a running simulation answers is a load error. `region.*` is left for the package that surveys regions. | Design, from building the map format | Marking the context pure would have refused `rand`, `chance` and `pick`, which 10 §3.4 allows; what makes them safe is where they draw from. A generator for each evaluation, seeded by position, makes the result independent of the order cells are worked out in (invariant X6) by construction. | 10 §3.4, §13; 05 §7.3. A map expression that reads a property finds no subject to read it from. |
| D-395 | A turned map is measured from the north-west corner of its turned footprint. Choices are made as the map is drawn, before turning, so the same seed gives the same map turned. A state faces a direction when its name is a compass direction, alone or after an underscore, and its definition has the states for all four quarter-turns; a turn replaces it by the state facing the turned direction, and every other state is carried over. | Design, from building the map format | 10 §3.3 said states with a facing turn and the world model gives a state's bits no meaning (02 §5.1). Names are already how a mod tells states apart, and asking for all four keeps turning a group action: four quarter-turns and two mirrors give the map back. | 10 §3.3, §13 (a gap: bits used as a number cannot be a facing). `ResolvedMap::turned`. |
| D-396 | A map's `anchor` is the cell, or mark, that a placement puts where it says the map goes. A map that names none is placed by the north-west corner, at level 0, of its turned footprint, as 10 §4.1 has it. | Design, from building the map format | The plan lists `anchor` among a map's parts and 10 §3 did not define it. A gate that must meet a road wants its door, not its corner, on the cell named, whichever way it is turned. | 10 §3.1, §3.5. `ResolvedMap::anchor_cell`. |
| D-397 | The `roof` preset lays the map's `roof` cell spec, or its `default`, over every cell the storey below draws. Presets are a fixed set of one. | Design, from building the map format | "A floor over everything that had a block below" would roof the walls and leave the rooms open, and named no floor. | 10 §3.1, §13. A roof of another shape is drawn as rows. |
| D-398 | A nested map must lie wholly inside its parent, storeys included, and is laid in after the parent's rows and `set`. `turn` is degrees, `"mirror"`, an object or `"any"`; by tag and under `"any"` one that fits is chosen by positional hash. `in` scatters copies through an area: cells tried in an order the seed decides, kept while a map fits and none kept is within `apart`. Each copy's seed is a positional hash of the parent's seed and its cell. A nested map's marks join its parent's; its joints, anchor and front do not. A map nests inside itself if any map it could place could place it. Until settings load, `gen.max_nesting` is `MapLimits`. | Design, from building the map format | 10 §3.5 gave two examples and no rules, and a resolved map is one box of cells. Bed marks inside a bedroom placed in a house must count for the house (10 §11.2). | 10 §3.5, §3.7. |
| D-399 | What the world cannot yet take is carried in the resolved map as data with ids as written: spawns, items, conduits, fields, devices and links, berths and approaches, watched cells, cell variables, decals, pattern cells, joints, portals and vehicles. Each is checked as far as a map alone allows: a link names devices of the same map, a joint lies on the edge it opens through, a cell's `area` and an `areas` entry name an `area_kind`, and a point may name only a mark that is the same in every placement. | Design, from building the map format | The packages that own those things are later, and a map that loads today must not change meaning when they land. | 10 §3.1, §3.2, §3.6, §3.8, §13. The ids are resolved by the packages that own them: E and S for creatures and items, G for devices and conduits, W16 for fields, V for vehicles. |
| D-450 | One function registers every definition type of the engine, `clash_scene::register_engine_defs` (and `engine_types`), and everything that loads whole mods calls it: the application, the tools, the examples. A loader may be told to pass over definitions of unregistered types (`Loader::only_known_types`), for the tests and tools of a crate that sits below the owner of some types a bundled mod holds. | Design, from merging the build | Each example had registered the types it knew, and broke when a bundled mod gained a definition of a type owned elsewhere. | An unknown type stays an error everywhere else. A reference to a definition that was passed over is an unknown reference. |
| D-380 | `WorldEdit::damage(loc, layer, packet)` returns a `Result`, and what a packet does carries the packet's cause, not the edit's. In the rule of 02 §12.2 the test for "unaffected" comes first; an element that is damaged and not destroyed stops at 254 parts; with no threshold any intensity above zero has full effect. A target that is affected takes all of the packet that does not pass (`absorbed + passed = energy`), one that is not takes none and passes none, and where there is nothing to hit all of it passes. The rule alone is `resolve_packet`. `ElementDestroyed` says how: by damage of a type, crushed, or fell. `damage_along` gives the hit a direction for debris. | Design, from building damage and collapse | The sketch could not refuse an unloaded cell. `absorbed` was named and not defined, and with a `factor` above one "energy that did harm" would exceed the energy delivered (invariant W8). Read literally, something with no capacity was destroyed by a packet below its threshold. A packet travels through walls and bodies and must keep one cause. Rules and devices need to tell a wall that was shot from one that fell. | 02 §7.2, §7.4, §12.1, §12.2. The body model (06) calls `resolve_packet`. Debris bodies are W14's: the hook throws nothing and all of an element is left as rubble. |
| D-381 | Rubble keeps matter. A destroyed element leaves the volume it lost: all of it, or the difference when its `on_destroy` state is smaller. A fixture that names a `heap` piles up: its bits are an amount in litres, its mass and capacity follow the amount, and a state written without one holds the definition's volume. Rubble lies where something dropped into the cell comes to rest (`Cosmos::resting_cell`). A cell is full at 3000 litres: a pile that reaches that in a cell with no block becomes the heap block, and the rest goes on top. A fixture of another kind in the way is buried and joins the pile; the larger of two piles gives the pile its material. Rubble that is destroyed leaves none. | Design, from building damage and collapse | 02 §12.3 said amounts add up and a full cell becomes a heap, and gave neither a unit nor what is full; §5.4 and §12.3 disagreed on whether an element with `on_destroy` leaves rubble. A tower that comes down must leave a heap that weighs what the tower did, for the load pass and for whoever digs. A heap that left rubble when destroyed could never be cleared. | 02 §12.3, §23. `WorldDefs::rubble_units`, `bulk`, `bulk_mass`. Rubble by damage type and as a field is W25's. Beside a standing block a pile only grows, to 65 m³ at most. |
| D-382 | Any edit that takes away what an occupant stood on raises `OccupantUnsupported`: its cell's floor, or the solid top or the ramp, stairs or ladder of the block or fixture below, when `Cosmos::footing` then finds nothing under it. An occupant that fills several cells is held up while any cell of its lowest storey is. A fixture left with nothing under it, in a changed cell or the cell over one, falls at the stability check as a mass of its own. | Design, from building damage and collapse | 02 §14.3 raised the event only for a floor that fell; a floor that is shot away or removed by a rule leaves an occupant just as unsupported, and one test in the edit covers every path. A desk on a floor that was destroyed would otherwise hang in the air. | 02 §7.2, §7.4, §12.3, §14.3. The world does not move the occupant. A fixture meant to hang where there is no floor falls if the cell under it changes. |
| D-383 | In the support rule, "horizontally adjacent" is the eight cells round a cell, each step counting one. A material with no `span` has span zero. A block flagged `liquid` is not structure: it does not fall and holds nothing up. Anchors, floors laid on anchors and implicit fill are always supported and are never examined; what is beyond a check's reach or not loaded is assumed supported. A check examines the island joined to its changes along every way support or load passes, downward included, within 24 cells and 40 storeys of them and 131,072 elements. Requests are taken together by clock and grouped by reach. Ties between equally near supports are broken by position and a fixed order of neighbours. | Design, from building damage and collapse | The figure of 02 §14.5, 39 tonnes of slab on one pillar, is a square of nine by nine cells, which only diagonal steps give. Floors laid on the ground would otherwise join every building of a street into one structure. The load a lost support carried lands on other supports all the way down, so a check that looked only upward could not crush them. Deep water stands on water, which bears nothing: as structure a lake would fall at the first check beside it. | 02 §14.1, §14.2, §23. `Cosmos::assess_stability` and `Assessment`. One check of a standing ten-storey building thirty cells square, 10,250 elements, takes about a millisecond. |
| D-384 | In the load pass every block overloaded in one pass is crushed together, and the passes run again within the same check, up to 48 times. A fixture counts with the floor of its cell or, without one, the block below. Where ambient gravity is zero or less nothing is crushed and nothing falls: what has no support stays, and falls when weight returns. A change of gravity over an area asks for a check of every floor and every block that is not an anchor in its loaded cells: when an area that sets gravity is made, removed, moved or reshaped, and by `WorldCtx::set_area_ambient`. `Cosmos::set_area_ambient` asks for none. | Design, from building damage and collapse | "The check runs again" did not say whether one block goes at a time. Overloaded supports give way together, and one at a time costs a pass for each. An unsupported slab in free fall has nowhere to fall to, and a mass that never lands would be state that never ends. The `Cosmos` function has no timeline to schedule a check with and could not be re-signed while others build on it. | 02 §14.1, §14.5, §8.3. Sideways and upward gravity do nothing to structures. |
| D-385 | The world's processes are one `ProcessTable<WorldProcess>` in the cosmos (`WorldActivity`), with wake-ups of `WakeDomain::World`, and `WorldCtx::handle_wake` is the one entry point for them. A settle check is a process on the clock of the cells it is for. A falling mass is a process of its own: one vertical run of unsupported elements with the fixtures in their cells, on the clock of where it is, moved to the right clock whenever an area's clock changes. It lands where `resting_cell` says, looked at again on arrival; strikes with `carried + m·g·h` at `energy / (1 m² × 0.1 m)`; exposes the occupants of the cell once; and either goes through with what passed, at the speed that gives it, or becomes rubble. A collapse is joined by any failure whose box meets or adjoins its own, and settles when nothing of it is in flight and no request waits in or beside its box. Its cause is a process with the instigator of the earliest request. | Design, from building damage and collapse | `ProcessTable` names a process by the wake-up's `id` alone, so two tables in one domain would collide; one table with an enum is what `clash-time` documents. Masses of one collapse can be in different clocks, and a time-stop cast over a falling tower must hold it. 02 §14.3 gave the energy of the blow and not its intensity. A collapse had no identity for its two events to share. | 02 §4, §14.3; 01 §5.1. Later packages add their kinds to `WorldProcess`. Whoever saves the cosmos saves `Cosmos::processes` with it and the timeline; `restore_activity` puts it back. The collapse of a ten-storey building, 11,025 elements, takes about 22 ms in all. |
| D-386 | Left out of damage and collapse for now: the ticket a collapse holds (W9; the place is marked in the code), resolve mode (02 §14.4), debris bodies (W14), rubble by damage type and as a field, floating floors (W25), and the tremor's factors on span and bearing (W24). Putting an element in place still asks for no check (D-301): whoever builds asks with `WorldCtx::check_stability`. | Design, from building damage and collapse | Each belongs to a package that is not built, and a tower must be able to fall before any of them. Asking on every placement would evaluate a building once for every cell laid while a map is stamped. | plans/world-model.md W12, W13, both `beta`. The scripts `wall_punch`, `tower_pancake` and `weakened_columns` are integration tests until the script runner exists (W11). |
| D-410 | `Cosmos::light` takes the moment as a parameter: a `SkyTime` with the local time of the realm's clock and the share of the sky's light that gets through. A light level is a `Real` from 0 with no upper end, where 1 is a cell open to a sky at level 1. Every read returns a `Result`, as the world's other reads do. | Design, from building light | 15 §3.3 wrote `light(loc)` and said the sky gives light by local time, but the world does not hold the timeline (D-300), and the scene plays events back at moments of its own choosing. The weather's light (09 §14.1) needs a way in before there is weather. | 15 §3.3. Whoever reads light reads `Timeline::local_now(realm.clock)` first. Perception multiplies a `line` signal by the level as it stands. |
| D-411 | `sky` is a definition type of the world: the length of a day, the time of day at which the realm's clock starts, levels by time of day joined by straight lines round midnight, how the light spreads under cover (`spread.loss`, default 0.25 a cell; `spread.reach`, default 8 cells, at most 16), and a backdrop that is stored only. A stratum's `sky` is a checked reference; a stratum that names none has no sky light. The setting `light.spread_loss` is withdrawn: spread belongs to each sky. | Design, from building light | 15 §3.3 named a sky definition and gave it no fields, and 02 §4.1 kept `sky` as written text. There are no calendars yet (09 §14.2), and a day must come from somewhere. A fog and a clear sky should be able to spread differently, which one setting cannot say. | 15 §3.3, §15; 02 §4.1. `mods/core/data/world/lighting.json` defines `day_night`, `high_altitude` and `orbit`, the three the example realm of 02 §4.1 names. A realm that names a sky no mod defines no longer loads. When calendars are built the day's length moves to them. |
| D-412 | Sky light spreads from the cells open to the sky by steps to the ten neighbours: one cell sideways, a cell and a half diagonally, three cells between storeys. A diagonal step needs both cells beside the corner clear and a step between storeys needs the upper cell to have no floor. A cell stops light when something `opaque` in it stands taller than 1.5 m; a window, an open door and a hole pass it alike. A cell that stops light is lit by its neighbours and passes nothing on. Distance is kept in half cells as a whole number. | Design, from building light | 15 §3.3 said light spreads through non-opaque cells, losing a fixed fraction a cell, to a cap, and nothing about diagonals, storeys, windows or what a wall looks like. Whole numbers make the spread the same on every machine and in any order. Four-way steps alone give diamonds of light; unguarded diagonals leak through corners (as D-304 found for enclosures). A wall that took no light would be drawn black beside a lit floor. | 15 §3.3. A wall is as bright seen from the dark side as from the lit one: a cell has one level. Glass that passes part of the light is not built. |
| D-413 | A floor, block or fixture gives off light by a `light` field, `{ intensity, radius }`, and a state may give another or, with intensity 0, none. A source lights every cell it sees within its radius by `intensity / (1 + d²)`, and it sees what `field_of_view` gives an eye in its cell at 1.5 m. A radius is at most 16 m and defaults to where the light falls to 0.01. Sources are added in a fixed order and a chunk counts at most 128. Moving sources are handed in with the question as a list and add by the same rule; the world keeps none. The static part is never below zero, and nor is the whole. | Design, from building light | 15 §3.3 said tagged sources light what they have line of sight to, and did not say how an element is tagged, from what height, in what order sums are made, or how a carried lamp reaches the world, which does not know items. The field of view is symmetric, exact in whole numbers and already built; a ray to every cell is neither cheaper nor the same at corners. A sum of reals depends on its order, so the order is fixed. | 15 §3.3; 02 §5.4. Items, creatures and fields that give light are supplied by the simulation as moving sources. A lamp that is switched is a fixture with two states. |
| D-414 | The light cache is a table of the cosmos beside the chunks, keyed by grid and chunk position, behind a lock. It keeps how far each cell is from the open sky and what the fixed sources give it, neither of which depends on the time of day. For each chunk it also keeps what the chunk looks like to light, read again when the chunk's structure revision or any in its tract moves, and notes the box of cells that changed. A chunk's light is worked out from the chunk and its twenty-six neighbours, and again only when one of them changed within light's reach: the furthest any loaded sky spreads or any loaded source shines. Loading or unloading a tract makes its chunks be read again whatever their revisions say. | Design, from building light | 15 §3.3 said the static part is cached per chunk and rebuilt when the chunk's revision or its sources change, but light crosses chunks, and a roof anywhere above changes what is open to the sky. A stamp of twenty-seven revisions would work every neighbour out again after any edit, a desk included. Reads take the cosmos by shared reference (D-280), so a cache they fill needs a lock; keeping it out of the chunk keeps chunks free of one. A reloaded tract restarts its revisions (D-316). | 15 §3.3; 02 §22. Not saved, not hashed, empty in a clone of the cosmos, and trimmed past 1024 chunks. `Chunk::any_state` lets a chunk be ruled out as holding a source without scanning its cells. |
| D-415 | `light` of a cell that is not loaded or lies beyond an edge is the error every read gives. `fill_light` writes zero for such cells and fails only for an unknown grid or a box of more than 1,048,576 cells. Light neither comes out of unloaded space nor passes through it. | Design, from building light | Neither 15 nor 02 said. A screen at the edge of what is loaded should still draw, and a single read should not guess. Treating what is not loaded as dark and solid keeps every answer a function of loaded cells; the neighbours are worked out again when the tract loads. | 15 §3.3. A room lit only through a window that faces an unloaded tract is dark until that tract loads. |
| D-440 | Stats are read through three kinds of subject, `attrs`, `pools` and `tracks`, which `entity.attr`, `entity.pool` and `entity.track` give: the same entity seen as its stats. Each definition is a property of its kind under its qualified id, and under its bare id where no other definition of the type and no property of the kind has that name. A pool's level is `self.pool.blood`; its fraction is `self.pool.frac('blood')`, not `self.pool.blood.frac`. Each sort also has a form that takes an id, for a stat chosen at run time. None is settable and none is safe for belief. | Design, from building the beta | 06 wrote both `self.pool.blood`, a number, and `self.pool.blood.frac`, and a property has one type. A pool could be a subject of its own only with a kind for every pool definition, since a handle's 64 bits are all taken by the entity's id, and kinds are the engine's to define. The checker looks a property up by its exact name and knows no namespaces, so a bare id can stand for a definition only where it is the only one. | 06 §4.4, §13; the examples of 12 §10.1, §10.6 and 14 §3.2 rewritten. A misspelt stat is a load error with a suggestion. Two mods that define the same bare id are read by qualified id. The fixture strings `self.pool.blood.frac` in the console's and the scene's tests are opaque to their fake hosts and are their owners' to change. |
| D-441 | An entity with stats has every attribute that is defined, at its definition's base until it is given a base of its own or a modifier. A value is `bound((base + Σ add) × Π mul)`. Modifiers are kept and applied in order of source, and for one source in the order added; a source is a kind (trait, item, wound, ability, template, rule, other) and a number its owner chooses. A base of the entity's own stands in for the definition's, an expression included. A base that comes to null counts as zero. `max` joins `min`. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §4.1 said "`add`, then `mul`" and left the rest. Floating-point sums depend on order, so a cache can equal a recomputation bit for bit (B3) only if the order is fixed and both use it. A property typed `real` that was null for every attribute an entity had not been given would make every use of one a warning. | 06 §4.1. `Stats` holds a value for every attribute: eight bytes each and a binary search to read. Reading an attribute of something with no stats is null. |
| D-442 | A derived attribute depends on the attributes its `base` reads directly, found in its bytecode when it is compiled. A dependency that leads back to itself is an error at the `base` of each attribute on the cycle. A change works out exactly the dependents, in an order fixed at load in which each follows what it reads, and the pools whose `max` or `regen` read one that changed. Anything else an expression reads is not followed: whoever changes it calls `refresh`, which works everything out from scratch. An attribute of the owner read through a function is null with a warning. | Design, from building the beta | A cycle must be a load error, so what depends on what must be known at load, and a function's body cannot be seen from outside the data layer. A dependency nobody can see would go stale silently, which invariant B3 forbids; refusing the read keeps the cache right by construction. Reactive expressions (05 §7.5) will be able to follow the rest, and are the kernel's. | 06 §4.1, §6.5; package E2. `AttributeDef::reads`, `StatDefs::attribute_order`. A base that needs another attribute inside a function passes it as an argument. |
| D-443 | A pool keeps its level as of a moment of its owner's local time, the `max` and `regen` last worked out, the rates applied, and how many of its thresholds it is at or above. Its level between changes is a line clamped to zero and `max`. A crossing is reported whenever that count changes, whatever moved the level: time, a verb, or a change of `max`. Rising to a threshold exactly crosses it upward and falling to it exactly crosses it downward. A pool is not said to cross what it starts beyond. The bounds are not thresholds unless listed. A `max` that is null, negative or not finite is zero, and such a pool is below every threshold. `start` and `regen` default to zero. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §4.2 named the mechanism and left every edge open. A threshold at 0 or 1 can only ever be met exactly, so equality has to count, and has to count by direction or a pool resting on a mark would be on both sides of it. Thresholds are fractions, so a shrinking `max` crosses them as surely as a draining level. | 06 §4.2. Thresholds are at most 256, in any order, none twice. A pool that reaches a mark exactly and turns back reports both crossings. |
| D-444 | A pool with a threshold in the way of its net rate keeps one wake-up, on its owner's clock, due when the line meets the threshold, rounded up to a whole microsecond of local time. It is in the domain `Timer`; its `id` is the owner's entity bits and its `arg` the pool's dense id with the top bit set, which is what tells it from the kernel's own timers. Every change to a pool brings it up to date, cancels the wake-up and schedules again. When the wake-up fires the level is put on the threshold. The stats hold their clock; `set_clock` moves them, and a change of the clock's rate needs no call. A crossing more than about 146,000 years of local time away is not scheduled. | Design, from building the beta | Draining is a timer of 03 §9 and must speed and slow with its owner (03 §3.5), which a wake-up on the owner's clock does for nothing. `clash-time` is not this package's to give a new domain. The timeline rounds a due tick up and local time down, so the level worked out at the wake-up can be a hair short of the mark; the mark is where the line is. | 06 §4.2, §15; 03 §3.3. `stats::is_pool_wake`, `stats::on_wake`. The kernel's timers leave the top bit of `arg` clear, or ask `is_pool_wake` first. |
| D-445 | When its owner leaves the simulation a pool is brought up to date and its wake-up cancelled, and it stands still. When the owner returns, the caller says how much local time passed for it: the pools move by that in one step, each threshold passed is reported once in order, and the next crossings are scheduled. Leaving is never refused, even if the clock is gone. | Design, from building the beta | 03 §9 gives a timer `Pause` or `Elapse` and 06 said nothing of pools. Which it should be depends on who was away and how (an anonymous passer-by, an agent the cold simulation steps), which the kernel knows and a pool does not. | 06 §4.2; 03 §9. `stats::suspend`, `stats::resume`. The kernel calls `suspend` before it stores, unloads or destroys an entity, so no wake-up outlives its pool. |
| D-446 | A rate applied to a pool is a number under a source, one for each source and pool, and goes with its source as a modifier does. Only the definition's `max` and `regen` are expressions, worked out again when an attribute they read changes. Adding to a pool stops at its bounds and reports what was added; a cost is taken in full or refused. The hook `pool_changing` is raised by whoever runs a verb, not for rates or regeneration. | Design, from building the beta | A component is saved, and an expression is not something a save can hold; whoever owns an expression for a rate applies its value again when it changes. 08 §5.2 needs all or nothing, and a drain that takes what is there (02 §16; 08 §9.4) needs to stop at the bound, so they are two operations. A hook that ran for a line moving would run every tick. | 06 §4.2, §4.4; 08 §5.2. `stats::set_rate`, `stats::pool_add`, `stats::pool_spend`. |
| D-447 | A track's `levels` gives, for `$level`, the total its counter must have reached. The track is at the highest level whose total it has; a level reached is kept. The counter only grows: a negative amount is refused. `max_level` ends a track, and none passes level 10,000. `on_advance` is a list of effects kept as written. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §4.3 said "the amount needed for each level", which reads as a total or as a step; a total needs no sum kept and makes the level a function of the counter. A curve that never rises would otherwise level for ever. Effects are the rule runtime's to compile (K5). | 06 §4.3. `stats::advance_track` reports each level once, lowest first. |
| D-448 | The functions of `clash_sim::stats` return what changed as values, from which the kernel raises `attr_changed`, `pool_crossed` and `track_advanced`. `attr_changed` is new. The fields of each event and of the hook `pool_changing`, the arguments of the six verbs, and the reasons for refusal are written into 06 §4.4. A refusal is a `StatsError` with a stable code, a sentence and the values involved. | Design, from building the beta | The package is built apart from the kernel and cannot raise an event. Reactive expressions (05 §7.5) need an event for every property they may read, and nothing announced an attribute. 06 named the events and verbs without their fields or arguments. | 06 §4.4, §13. The events are registered by the stats' registry step. The verbs are not registered until the rule runtime runs them. |
| D-449 | `Stats` is the third component type. The functions that change it are handed the store, the definitions, the timeline, a runner and a host, and answer their own properties from the store being changed: the host answers the rest and does not hold the stats. The registry step registers the kind `entity` if nobody has. `clash-sim` names `clash-time` in its manifest. | Design, from building the beta | An expression about an entity's stats is evaluated while those stats are borrowed to be changed, so the simulation's host cannot be the one to read them. A tool that loads mods with no kernel still has to compile `self.attr.x`. The arrow to `clash-time` is already there through `clash-world` (D-268). | 06 §3.1; package E2. `StatsCx`, `stats::property`. The kernel registers `entity` before it loads. The component store's tests that listed two component types now list three. |
| D-430 | A screen's bindings are compiled by the screen itself in `Def::compile`. A bound property (`Prop::Bound`) holds a `Binding`: the source, where it was written and, once compiled, the bytecode. The names are bound in fixed slots: `view`, `party`, `app`, `screen` and `it` as subjects 0 to 4, and the parameters as locals in order of name. `screen` is a map of the screen's variables, and a `screen.name` the screen does not declare is a load error. `view` and the members of `party` are the kind `entity` where the registry has one, and `any` where it has none. The scene's definition types are read with their place by the loader and without one by any other deserialiser (`clash_data::by_site_or_plain`), and what was read without a place is never compiled. `clash-scene` names `clash-expr` as a dependency of its own. | Design, from building bindings | 12 §10.4 named the bindings and not their types, and a `Prop` had to stay readable by the toolkit's tests, which parse screens with `serde_json`. A screen's variables differ from screen to screen, so they cannot be registered properties. The linter and the gallery load without a simulation, and a screen that names `view` must still load there. | 12 §10.1, §10.3, §10.4; 04 §7.1. `clash-data` gained `by_site_or_plain`. A binding's diagnostics carry the codes of group `06` and point inside the string. When the simulation registers `entity`, every `view.x` in every mod is checked against it. |
| D-431 | An ordinary screen is a mind context whose own are the party: `view`, `party`, a parameter of type `party_member`, whatever is reached from them through properties that yield something of the same owner, and the elements of such lists. A row of an own list binds `it` as own. A `ui_part` is compiled as an ordinary screen's whatever screen it is placed in. | Design, from building bindings | D-080 and 12 §10.4 said the party is read in full and everyone else as perceived, and the mind context of D-273 had one own subject and no way to say that a list, a local or a row is the character's own. Without it a list of the party's own members could show nothing but what is safe for belief. | 12 §10.4; 05 §7.3. `clash-expr` gained `Context::add_own_local`, own elements of an own list, and `Checked::is_own`. A screen whose context is `debug` binds nothing as own and reads truth. |
| D-432 | The scene's link to the simulation is `SceneHost`: `submit`, and for reading `view`, `party` and `reader`, which is a `clash_expr::Host` and cannot write. An effect that submits builds a `UiCommand`, the kind of command (`act`, `command`, the `Ui` command of `run`), its name and its arguments as values; a host whose command can be made from one is submitted to, and refuses with a reason. `NoHost` refuses everything. The application is reached through `AppState`: the lists and flags `app` reads, and one function for services. `app` is a kind of subject, with `app_entry` for the entries of its lists, registered by a registry step of `clash_scene::register_defs`; all of it is safe for belief, and the scene answers it. | Design, from building bindings and effects | The simulation's kernel is being written at the same time, so its command type and its view cannot be named. A generic command keeps the scene's one route to the simulation as it was (12 V1) and lets the kernel decide what each command means. Registering `app` makes a misspelt `app.savs` a load error and puts it in the reference. | 12 §3, §10.4, §10.5, §14; 05 §5.3. The kernel implements `From<UiCommand>` for its command, or a wrapper that does, and `SceneHost` for a handle on itself. `clash_scene::testing` has a fake host and a fake application. |
| D-433 | Lists of UI effects are read into typed effects when their definition loads. Each effect's arguments are tabled in 12 §10.5. A new effect, `answer`, closes a screen opened by `confirm` or `pick` and gives its answer. `confirm` and `pick` open the screens of the roles `confirm` and `picker.list` and stop; the rest of the list runs when the answer comes, for a pick with the choice as `it`. An effect that cannot be run is reported with its reason and the list goes on. A list runs in the screen that asked, and with no screen once that has closed. `open` and `replace` take a bare screen name by the rule of D-322. | Design, from building effects | 12 §10.5 named the effects and gave no arguments, and "ask before continuing" needs something to continue: the rest of the list. A popup has to say what was answered, and no effect did. Typing the effects at load is the only way an unknown one can be a load error where it is written. | 12 §10.5, §10.7. The scene reports what each effect came to as `UiOutcome`s. `act` with `"needed"` or `"all"` only marks the command until package U7 asks for the parameters. |
| D-434 | A screen with a `popup` does not fill the frame: `width`, `height`, `place` and `dim`. The frame is drawn from the lowest screen that still shows upward, input goes to the top screen, and a closed screen gives way to the one beneath as it was left. Beneath a dimming popup each colour keeps 44% of its brightness and a character falls back to dark grey on sixteen colours. | Design, from building the stack | 12 §10.1 had no way to say a screen is a popup, and `confirm` and the pickers are popups. Dimming by the fallback colour follows D-352: brightness that only 24-bit colour can show would not reach a terminal. | 12 §10.1. The share is a constant of the scene. A click outside a popup does nothing. |
| D-435 | A `preference` has `kind` (`flag`, `number`, `text`, `choice`), `default`, `options`, `min`, `max`, `name` and `doc`. The scene keeps the values by qualified id, starting from the defaults and the profile's `preferences`, refuses a value a preference cannot hold, and tells the application of each change through the `set_pref` service. The engine acts on the preferences whose bare names are `theme` and `keymap`, whichever mod defines them. Changing the theme makes every open screen again from its definition and gives each what the old one held. `pref.id` with a bare id is resolved by the rule of D-322. | Design, from building preferences | 12 §10.4 gave one example. A total conversion without the `ui` mod defines its own `theme` preference, so the engine cannot know it by a qualified id. Styles are resolved when a screen is made (U6a), so restyling is making again; the state a player would notice losing is carried over. | 12 §10.4; 04 §3.3. `mods/ui` defines `theme`, `keymap`, `playback_speed` and `cutscenes`, and a second theme, `ui:night`. The application persists preferences. |
| D-436 | A slot is known by a qualified name: the namespace of the screen that places it, a colon and the name the screen gives it. A `ui_part` has `into`, `order`, `if`, `required` and `widget`. Parts are shown lowest `order` first and among equals by qualified id, one below another. A part whose slot no screen places is a load error unless it says `"required": false`. A part's bindings see `view`, `party`, `app` and `screen.name`, unchecked, and not the screen's parameters. | Design, from building slots | 12 §10.6 wrote `"into": "ui:hud.bars"` while the example screen of §10.1 named its slot `condition.extra`: the two agree only if a slot's name is its own and the namespace is its screen's. 12 said `"optional": true`, but `optional` is a key every definition carries and means something only with `patch` (04 §5, §6.3), and the loader refuses it elsewhere. A part is compiled once and may go into any screen. | 12 §10.2, §10.6. The check is a cross check of the loader, placed at `into`. |
| D-480 | A tract blob is kind `TRCT`, version 2: an optional column and the simulation's entity section. Element states in it are written by save id. The tables of save ids belong to the cosmos (`SaveIds`), one for each kind of definition saved state names (`DefKind`: floor, block, fixture, material, area kind, realm). A definition joins its table the first time the save names it; save id 0 of the element types and of materials is `engine:none`. The tables are not hashed, and are stored under the name of each definition type. Everything saved is walked for the dense ids it holds. Version 1 is refused. | Design, from building persistence | 02 §11 asked for a save-wide, append-only table and did not say where it lives, what it covers or when an id joins it. Tables that hold only what the save has named keep placeholders to what a save really uses, and naming tables lets another kind be added without a new version. Version 1 held dense ids and was never on disk, so there is nothing to migrate. | 02 §11.1, §11.2. `encode_tract` and `decode_tract` replace `encode_column` and `decode_column`. A state type that holds the id of a definition implements `MapIds`; `WorldProcess` is matched without a wildcard so that a new kind of process must say. Decal, conduit, site, tract, devastation kind, map, region kind and path kind ids are written as numbers until their types have tables, and a named state as its position. A store is committed only after `save_world`, which writes the tables. |
| D-481 | A definition a save names and the loaded mods do not supply becomes a placeholder when the save is loaded: a definition of its type added after the real ones in a copy of `WorldDefs`, keeping the id the save wrote and not found by name. A placeholder floor has no thickness and can be stood on; a block has no volume and is `load_bearing`, `anchor` and `passable`; a fixture has no volume; a material has no density or toughness, default resistances, no bearing and an unlimited span; an area kind is empty; a realm keeps the extent it was saved with and has no strata and no generator. Elements show as `?` and carry the tag `placeholder`. Each is reported by `Cosmos::take_save_warnings` with where it was met. | Design, from building persistence | 04 §12.3 said a placeholder is inert, visibly marked and remembers the id, and not what it does in a world that stands on it. Dense ids index tables everywhere, so something must be at the id; a definition of its own for each missing id keeps the id without a side table for each cell. A wall that vanished or fell when its mod was left out could not be restored when the mod came back, so a placeholder block holds up what stood on it. It has no volume so that it weighs nothing, blocks nothing and breaks at the first blow that takes effect. | 02 §11.2; 04 §12.3, which no longer says the placeholder is in the `engine` namespace; invariant W9. After `load_world` the definitions to use are `Cosmos::defs`: every definition passed in keeps its dense id and placeholders follow. A realm whose definition has gone loads its stored tracts and generates none. Migration entries (04 §12.1) will be consulted in `SaveIds::bind` (P7). |
| D-482 | `TractStore` also keeps named sections: `section`, `put_section`, `delete_section`, `section_names`. Names that begin with `world.` are the world's. `RedbStore` is one `redb` database file for each save, with a table of tract blobs keyed by realm, row and column and a table of sections. What is put and deleted is held in memory until `commit`, which writes it in one transaction. The file is opened with redb's integrity check. | Design, from building persistence | A save is more than tracts, and all of it must become durable together or not at all. Holding changes until the commit makes a game that is not saved leave its file alone, and makes `put` visible at once as 02 §11 requires. A plain open of a damaged redb file may panic; the checked open returns an error (01 R6). | 02 §11, §11.5. This is the use of `redb` that 01 §13 lists; nothing else is added. Our code has no `unsafe`. The store is read and written only at safe points (01 R5). The simulation writes its own sections through the same trait before the one commit. |
| D-483 | The entity section of a tract is bytes the simulation hands in with `Cosmos::set_tract_entities` while the tract is loaded. The world writes them with the tract when it is unloaded or the world is saved and hands them back in `LoadedTract::entities` at the next load, taking them out of the store as it does. A pristine tract with an entity section is stored as the section alone. | Design, from building persistence | 02 §11 named the section and gave no way in or out, and the world never calls upward (01 R4). Entities handed back and still in the store would be there twice after the next save. Storing cells because someone slept in a tract would end the pristine policy for every tract a creature was ever unloaded in. | 02 §11.1, §9.4, §9.5. The simulation hands the section in when it answers `TractUnloading`. A loaded tract's section waits in the cosmos, is saved with the tract and comes back still waiting. |
| D-484 | The generator fingerprint is a hash of the definitions' content fingerprint, the generator a realm's definition names with its parameters, and the realm's seed. A tract's record keeps the one its cells were last generated with, in `TractExtra::generated`. A pristine tract generated again under another fingerprint reports `LoadedTract::regenerated` and takes the new one; only its cells are made again. `save.store_pristine` stores every tract at unload and at save and leaves its record pristine. Cells that are in a tract's blob are what the tract loads with, whatever the setting and the fingerprint. | Design, from building persistence | A pristine tract is generated on every load, so a changed fingerprint cannot be what makes it regenerate: it is how the world, and the simulation that kept entities there, learn that the cells are new content. Acting on the generator's areas and spawns again would double what the first generation left. With everything stored the point is to keep the world as it was seen, so a stored tract is not thrown away because a mod changed. | 02 §11.3, §9.1, §9.4. `WorldDefs::fingerprint` is the loader's (04 §9), so a change to any definition of any type counts. `Cosmos::set_store_pristine` stands for the setting until settings are bound (P6). `TractExtra` is hashed with its new field. |
| D-485 | `Cosmos::save_world(store)` writes the world as sections, each a blob with its own kind and version: `world.cosmos` (realms with their records, the grid table, global variables), `world.areas`, `world.sites`, `world.tickets`, `world.stability`, `world.activity`, `world.tracts` and `world.ids`. The sections are a list in the code, `WORLD_SECTIONS`; a subsystem adds an entry. A loaded tract that has changed is written as its tract blob and one that has not is generated again by `Cosmos::load_world(defs, io)`. Saving changes nothing hashed. A section of the world's prefix that a build does not know is refused at load; one a save lacks leaves its subsystem new. What reconciliation last worked out is saved beside the tickets, and whether a loaded column had changed beside the list of tracts. | Design, from building persistence | 02 §11 said only that realm and cosmos state go in the main save. Other packages are adding world state at the same time and need one obvious place to add theirs. Writing unchanged loaded tracts would make every save cost what is loaded, not what changed. Without the reconciliation's results and the changed marks, a loaded world would hash equal and then behave differently: a tract's activity would be unknown until the next reconciliation, and a changed tract would be unloaded without being written. | 02 §11.4; invariant W7, tested with a collapse in progress, tickets, areas, a site, checks waiting, and loaded and unloaded tracts of both sorts. `load_world` needs the generators. The world does not touch the timeline when it loads; the simulation restores the timeline from its own section, then places its entities again with `WorldCtx::place` into a sink it discards. `Cosmos::restore_activity` remains for tests. |
| D-486 | Left out of persistence for now: migration entries (P7); region records as blobs of their own, so `world.cosmos` is written whole at every save; save-id tables for the definition types that are not built; the columns of grids that are not a realm's base grid (W20a); a test script (W11) and a benchmark (W23). | Design, from building persistence | Each waits for a package that is not built, or costs little until worlds are large: a surveyed region's records are a few hundred bytes compressed. | plans/world-model.md W10, marked `beta`. Fields join the chunk's stored form and raise the tract blob's version (W16); bodies add a section (W14). |
| D-510 | A placement's `origin` is where the map's anchor goes. Each tract a map covers resolves the map again and writes its own window; within a tract placements are written by `order`, then position, map and seed. A map's spawns are handed over by the tract their cell is in, as one request for each creature with `kind` and `map` as parameters; an area is made once, by the tract that holds its lowest corner or first cell; marks follow from the map and the placement alone; the rest is carried in `PlacedMap`. | Design, from building placement | 10 §4.1 said where the north-west corner goes, and D-396 gave maps an anchor. A realiser may not read a neighbour's cells, and resolution is a pure function of the placement, so resolving again is the window. An area or a spawn must come into being once however many tracts its map covers and in whatever order they load. | 10 §4.1; plans/worldgen.md WG2. `clash_gen::place`. A spawn that names an area is given cells of open floor by the placement's seed. Area ids are given out in the order tracts first load: the cells of a world are the same in every order of exploration, the ids of its areas are not. |
| D-511 | A realiser finds the placements that overlap its tract from what it is shown: the placements a realm definition makes by hand, which the generator holds, and those of tract definitions, which are a pure function of the seed, the tract's position and its definition and lie wholly inside their tract. It works its eight neighbours' out too, from their records. A survey writes the same placements into the region's record. An overlapping placement of a tract definition is dropped, not moved, and the generator lists what a region dropped. | Design, from building placement | The generation interfaces show a realiser tract records and no region record (D-370), and 02 §9.1 keeps placements in region records. A levelled lot beside a tract decides where a ramp goes inside it, so the neighbours' placements matter; chains of overlap across tracts and regions would make what is dropped depend on the order of a survey. | 10 §4.1, §6, §6.2; 02 §9.1. `StandardGenerator::tract_def` and `placements_of` are the seam for region kinds and settlements (WG7, WG8): what replaces them must give a realiser every placement within 12 cells of its tract, by position alone or from a plan the generator keeps. |
| D-512 | A map that sits by `level` flattens the surface under its footprint before the ground is filled, so the ground's own steps lay strata, a surface and ramps there; `foundation` fills and cuts under the columns the map draws after the ground is filled; `follow` puts each column at its own surface; nothing is scattered under a footprint that levels or founds, and a map scattered by a ground never shapes the ground. `base` sets the storey of a placement's origin when a survey writes it. Applied to the world as it is, the ground of a column is the storey nearest the placement's, within 8, that stands on a block with a solid top; `level` then cuts and fills without ramps, and only `replace` cuts. | Design, from building placement | 10 §4.2 gave four words and a line for each. Flattening the height the ground reads gives "ramps at the edges" by the rule that already places ramps, and keeps the result a function of position. The world as it is has no height field to ask. | 10 §4.2, §6.1. Step 2 of 10 §6.1 is done in two parts, one before the ground and one after; the precedence of invariant X5 is unchanged. |
| D-513 | `apply_map` takes a placement, a mode and a cause, and returns what it changed, made and marked. `replace` writes every layer the map states; `only_empty` writes a cell of the map only where the world's cell holds nothing at all; `add` puts each element only where its layer is empty and never clears. A pending map is `PendingEdit::Map(PendingMap { placement, mode })`, it waits in every unloaded tract the map covers, and it stays in the record when the tract loads until `Cosmos::take_pending_maps` hands it to whoever holds the maps; `clash_gen::apply_pending_maps` writes it, clipped to the tract, with the coarse cause. | Design, from building placement | 10 §4.3 named three modes and defined one; 02 §17.3 gave the pending map no seed, without which two tracts would draw different maps. The world cannot depend on the crate that resolves maps (01 §3), and a pending edit that was dropped because nobody held the maps would be a silent loss. | 10 §4.3; 02 §9.4, §17.3. Added to `clash-world`, each at the end of its list: `MapMode`, `PendingMap`, `PendingEdit::Map`, `Cosmos::take_pending_maps`. Whoever loads tracts calls `apply_pending_maps` for each tract loaded; pending maps are written after the pending cell changes of the same load. The test script `aftermath_edit` is an integration test until scripts have a world to run in. |
| D-514 | A generator may be registered as a closure that owns what it builds from: `GeneratorRegistry::register_maker`, beside the plain factory. `clash_gen::GenDefs` is the build stage of maps, grounds and tract definitions together, `generators_with(defs)` binds every generator of the crate, and `check_defs` binds them too, so that a wrong `generator_params` is a lint error. `serde_json` becomes a dependency of `clash-gen`. | Design, from building tract realisation | A factory was a bare function given the world's tables alone (D-371), and the `standard` generator is made of maps, grounds and tract definitions, which are built from the loaded `Defs` above the world. A realm's parameters are JSON values that the generator reads. | 02 §9.3; 10 §6.2. `clash_gen::generators(world)` still binds the test generators and reports a realm of the `standard` generator as needing `generators_with`. `serde_json` was already a dependency of the workspace and of `clash-world`. |
| D-515 | A `ground` is a list of steps, each an object with one key. It declares its parameters with defaults, and its expressions see them as `$name`; a tract definition sets them. `depth` bounds how deep it is worked. A stratum's `anchor` selects the state of its block that has the flag or lacks it. `surface` takes `floor`, `block`, `ramp` and `where`; a ramp goes in the surface cell of a column beside one a storey higher, by its four sides. `liquid` fills cells with no block below its level. The surface height comes from the realm generator's `height` field until realm plans exist, and the realm definition's `sea_level` is the lowest storey that is not sea. `paths` draws nothing until surveys record paths; `float` is not built and fails to load. | Design, from building ground | 10 §5.1 named the steps and gave one example of each. A ground is shared by tract definitions with different parameters, as a palette is by maps. 02 §4.1 already gave realms a `sea_level` and did not say which storey it is; the `flat` generator's `surface_z` is the lowest storey that is not ground, and the sea follows it. | 10 §5.1, §13. Every field of every step is tabled in 10 §5.1. Lakes (`to: plan`) wait for realm plans (WG6). |
| D-516 | What keeps generated ground standing: every block of a stratum, a vein or a pocket is `load_bearing`; a cave cell is carved only where the stratum laid there anchors, the block there anchors and the cell above holds an anchoring block or nothing; a vein or pocket that does not anchor is laid one storey thick between ground that anchors; and a realm whose gravity would crush a run of loose strata of a ground it uses refuses the ground when its generator is bound. | Design, from building ground | Tests over many seeds found three ways generated ground fell at its first check: a cave cut through a vein of rock that crossed loose ground, a pocket of loose ground under more loose ground whose lowest storey was crushed by the run, and loose strata too thick to bear themselves. Anchors are never crushed and pass no load down (D-383), so only runs of loose ground can fail, and their weight depends on the realm's gravity. | 10 §5.2; invariant X9. `Ground::crushed_by`. Loose veins and pockets are thin lenses, never lumps: a known limit. |
| D-517 | Veins, pockets and caves are features started in the cells of a coarse lattice, so many to a cell as the density asks, each drawn from the positional hash of its lattice cell; a tract works out every feature that could reach it. A vein is a straight run one cell thick, a pocket a ball, a cave a run of 36 cells that turns every 6. A scatter step makes each cell a candidate by its own hash and keeps one when no candidate within `apart` has a lower hash; the chance of being a candidate is `1 − (1 − d·n)^(1/n)` for density `d` and `n = (2·apart + 1)²`, which leaves exactly `d` of the cells while `d·n` is under one. | Design, from building ground | Whatever crosses a tract's edge must be agreed without reading the neighbour (X1), and noise thresholds give no control of density. Thinning by rank needs only the hashes of cells within `apart`, and its yield has a closed form. | 10 §5.1. Density with `apart` is capped at `1/n`, below the closest packing: a known limit, in 10 §13. |
| D-518 | A `tract` definition has `ground`, `params`, `place`, `ruin`, `bands`, `glyph` and `tags`. Its `params` are plain values or expressions in the generation context with nothing bound, worked out once for each tract. Its `place` entries put maps on every tract of the definition, each with `at`, `turn` or `facing`, `chance`, `params` and `order`; `facing` rotates a map so that its `front` faces a direction. The engine supplies `engine:none` as dense id 0. `ruin` is checked to be an object and carried. | Design, from building tract realisation | 10 §6 gave an example and said that what stands on a tract is in placements, which only settlements, two packages on, would write. A street of houses needs to be writable now, and a lot's map must face its street. The world's records already mean "no definition" by id 0. | 10 §6. `region.*` in a tract's `params` waits for surveys (WG7); `ruin` for transforms (WG5); a record's `rot` and `variant` are not yet used. |
| D-519 | The generator `standard` reads `tracts`, `patch`, `layout`, `height`, `surface_z` and `place` from a realm definition's `generator_params`. Its survey gives each tract the layout's definition, or else one of `tracts` by weight from the positional hash of its patch, and writes the placements. `mods/core` gains a ground, three tract definitions, a lane map and the realm `core:countryside`; `cargo run -p clash-gen --example tract_ascii` prints realised tracts along the ground. | Design, from building tract realisation | Document 10's `worldgen` and `region_kind` types are later packages, and D-371 already lets a realm give its generator parameters. An authored grid of tract definitions and maps placed by hand are 10 §9.3 in their smallest form, and make a realm whose streets line up. | 10 §6.2, §12; 02 §9.3. Golden pictures of two tracts are in `crates/clash-gen/tests/golden/countryside.txt`, and the test fails if anything in them is unsupported. When `worldgen` definitions land, `standard`'s parameters move into them. |
| D-500 | A field's rules are a list of objects, each with `rule` and that rule's parameters, read as one struct of optional parameters and checked rule by rule when the tables are built: `decay` takes `amount`; `diffuse` `rate` and `rise` or `sink`; `flow` `rate`; `spread` `needs`, `chance`, `amount`, `from`; `emit` `field`, `amount`, `from`; `convert` `with`, `uses`, `gives`, `max`; `damage` `kind`, `energy_per_level`, `intensity_per_level`, `from`; `drift` `share`, `spread`; `expose` `pool`, `per_level`, `route`, `from`. Rules run in the order written. One that moves the field moves whole levels, rounded down, and only what the rules before it left; where the neighbours want more than is left, each gets its share. A level stops at 255. | Design, from building fields | 02 §16 named the rules and gave parameters for a few. An internal tag would make `serde` buffer the rule and lose its positions (04 §7.1). Rounding down needs no randomness, conserves the total and lets a field come to rest; sharing out what is left keeps a puff of gas symmetrical, which taking in order of direction did not. | 02 §16. Differences smaller than one over the rate never even out: a thin haze stays where it is until it decays. The order of `drift` and `diffuse` in a definition matters and is the author's. |
| D-501 | Fuel is a material tag. `spread` ignites a neighbour that holds a floor, block or fixture of a material carrying the tag its `needs` names, and `flammable: true` counts as the tag `flammable`. Such a `spread` also reaches a neighbour it could not pass into when what stops it, the covering block or the floor between, is itself of a material it needs. A fire grows where it has fuel by a reaction with the tag, and consumes the fuel by its own `damage` rule, which strikes the floor, block and fixture of the cell each step. | Design, from building fields | The engine may not know what fire is (01 R10): `flammable` as an engine notion of burning would be one. Read literally, a field cannot enter a covered cell, so a wooden wall or a closed wooden door could never burn, and a fire could not climb through a wooden ceiling. 02 gave fire no way to grow or to starve. | 02 §16, §5.4. `core:burning` and the tag `fuel` on wood. Items are not fuel to the world; a burning item is the simulation's to add. |
| D-502 | A field cell is active while it is awake. A cell of a field whose rules only move it (`flow`, `diffuse`) and which reacts with no material sleeps at a step that moves nothing into or out of it, and wakes when its field's level changes beside, above or below it, when any field's level changes in its cell, or when an element changes in or next to its cell. Every other field's cells are awake while they last. Whether a cell is awake is state, stored with its level and with who is responsible for it. The cosmos keeps the set of loaded tracts that hold any field. | Design, from building fields | 02 §16 said only active cells are stepped and did not say what makes a cell active. Water that has found its level must cost nothing, and must run again when the floor under it is shot away. A field that harms or doses must be stepped while anyone could walk into it, and the world is not told when they do. | 02 §16, §7.2. `WorldEdit` wakes fields at every structural change, at no cost while no field exists. `Cosmos::active_field_cells`. |
| D-503 | Fields are stepped by one process of the world for each clock and step interval, not for each realm. It wakes at whole multiples of the interval in the clock's local time, and the number of a step is that local time in intervals. A step reads everything, then writes each cell's summed change in order of cell and field, then strikes elements and tells occupants. Whether a field spreads is the positional hash of the realm's seed, the domain `world.field.spread`, the cell, and the step number with the field, the rule and the direction. | Design, from building fields | A field inside an area of stopped or slowed time must stop or slow with it (01 §5.2), which a process on the realm's clock cannot do. Steps counted from when a process happened to begin would make the outcome depend on the order fires were lit in and on whether an earlier fire had gone out. | 02 §16. `WorldProcess::FieldStep`. The same fire stepped in one advance, in a hundred, or saved and restored half way gives the same hash, tested. Processes are numbered in the order they begin, so two worlds lit in different orders agree in their cells and events and not in their process ids. |
| D-504 | A reaction between two fields happens, each step, as many times as the levels in the cell allow, at most `max`, and once if it uses nothing. It runs at the steps of the field named first where that one is awake and at the other's where it is not. A reaction with a material tag runs at each step of the field. A reaction to damage happens when `WorldEdit::conduct(loc, packet)` is called for a packet that has arrived in a cell: every cell joined to it through the field within `reach` steps, at most 4096, gets a packet with `share` of the energy and of the intensity, and the cell itself nothing more. `convert` is a reaction with the field itself named first. `leave` is not built. | Design, from building fields | 02 §16 gave the amounts of one reaction and not how many happen in a step: once a step would make water put a fire out over a minute. Two fields may have different steps, so which one's steps a reaction runs at had to be said. The world has no one place where a packet arrives in a cell, since `damage` strikes one element. | 02 §16, §7.2. Whatever delivers damage to a cell (releases, attacks) calls `conduct` once for it. `leave` comes with change of state (W25). |
| D-505 | Whoever instigated the edit that first puts a field in a cell is responsible for it there, and what the field brings elsewhere carries that along; everything a field does has the cause kind `Field` and that instigator. `FieldAppeared` and `FieldVanished` report a cell gaining a field it had none of and losing the last of it. `FieldContact` reports a field and an occupant coming to share a cell, once. `OccupantDosed` (from `expose`) and `OccupantExposed` (from `damage`) are emitted once a step for each occupant and rule, for the strongest cell of the field the occupant fills, to occupants of every kind and plane. `OccupantDosed` carries the pool as written, the amount `level × per_level` and the route. | Design, from building fields | 02 §7.3 wants every change traced to who is responsible, and a fire outlives the action that lit it by minutes. An occupant four cells wide would otherwise breathe four doses. The world knows no pools, routes or guards (01 R4), so it reports and the simulation decides. | 02 §7.4, §16. A field cell holds an instigator; of several arriving at once, the lowest id. The simulation applies routes, guards and `exposure_incoming`, and adds to the pool. |
| D-506 | The budget counts the active field cells of the whole cosmos at every step. With `n` too many, every active cell at or below the level of the `n`th weakest loses 16 levels in that step on top of its own rules. `FieldBudgetExceeded` is emitted when a step first finds the count over, and again only after it has been back under. | Design, from building fields | 02 §16 said the weakest decay faster and did not say which are the weakest or how much faster. A level found from a count of cells by level is the same whatever order the cells are in. | 02 §16, §23. The budget bounds cost over a few steps, not at once. `Cosmos::set_field_max_active_cells` until settings are bound. |
| D-507 | Drift for fields is what an area sets, read with `Cosmos::field_drift`: a flow as given, a vortex as its speed round the middle of its area from east toward south, less its inflow, with its lift. A field with `drift` moves, along each axis, the share of its level that the medium carries across the cell in a step, at most all of it, where it can pass and there is room. The chance of its `spread` to a neighbour is multiplied by `1 + spread × v`, `v` being the speed of the medium toward that neighbour, and never below nothing. | Design, from building fields | The region's wind and current come with the weather (09 §14.1) and the medium with vehicles (11 §4.7); 02 §8.3 left what velocity a vortex gives at a cell to them. "Likelier that way" needed a number. | 02 §16, §8.3; 11 §4.7. A field is carried at most one cell a step. Until there is weather, wind is an area with a flow. |
| D-508 | A field gives light by `light`: the intensity of a cell at level 255, a radius, and the least level that gives any; a lower level gives in proportion. `Cosmos::field_lights(grid, box)` lists it as moving sources, and the world keeps none. A field hides sight from its `opaque_from` level up, and `Cosmos::field_hides_sight(loc)` says so for a cell; rays, lines of sight and fields of view are not changed. | Design, from building fields | 15 §3.3 hands fields to light as moving sources and 02 gave a field no brightness. The field of view is shared with light, whose cache is keyed on structure revisions: smoke that stopped it would leave lit rooms lit by lamps the smoke hides, until something structural changed. | 02 §16, §22; 15 §3.3. Perception asks `field_hides_sight` for the cells on a line until the queries take a field of view of their own. A large fire is thousands of sources: the caller thins them. |
| D-509 | A chunk's fields are part of what is stored for its tract: the tract blob is version 2. A change to a field marks its tract as one to store, and a chunk that holds a field is never taken for one that holds only the fill. On reading a tract back each field catches up by its `catch_up`: `keep`, `none` (gone), `burn_out` (gone once a step has passed, burning nothing), `disperse` (its decay for each step, halved every 16 steps) or `settle` (kept and woken). A field definition's `warms` and `changes` are stored and do nothing yet. Space that is not loaded and the edges of a grid let no field through. An edit may put a field into any loaded cell, a covered one included. | Design, from building fields | A fire must survive a save, and 02 §9.4 and §16 named catch-up rules without saying what each does. Temperature and change of state are later packages (V1, W25). A field that leaked into unloaded space would be lost or would have to load it. | 02 §9.4, §11, §16. W10 stores fields by save id with the states. A closed-form settling of liquids and the waking of still liquid blocks are not built. |
| D-460 | The simulation holds one `Components` beside its entity table, saves it in its blob and hashes it as a part of its own. The kernel implements `Containment` over places. A `Physical` is given and changed only through `Sim::set_shape` and `Sim::set_own_mass`; those, and `relocate` for the container an entity left and the one it entered, work the masses out again and write each to the kernel's record of the entity (its presence) and, for one that stands in the world, to the world's occupant record. Removing an entity removes its components after `relocate` has taken it out of what held it. `Sim::load` refuses a blob holding a component of an entity that does not exist. | Design, from joining the component store to the kernel | The store was built apart from the kernel and listed what the kernel must hold and call (D-420 to D-423). Two records of an entity's mass, the component's and the presence the world is told, would drift unless one function writes both. A component whose owner is gone is damage that would otherwise be handed to whoever is given the slot next. | 03 §4.1, §4.2; 06 §3. `SimHashes` gains `components`, so the total hash is composed of one more part. An entity with no `Physical` keeps the mass its presence was given. The save layout is version 2. |
| D-461 | The simulation owns the generators and a tract store, neither of which is state, and reconciles tickets at every safe point: after each wake-up and after each accepted command. On `tract_unloading` it takes every entity with a cell in the tract out of the world and keeps where each stood on a shelf under that tract; on `tract_loaded` it puts them back, and one whose footprint needs a tract that is not loaded waits under that tract. A shelved entity keeps its id, its components and its contents. A loaded tract's spawn requests are reported as `spawn_requested`; a tract that could not be loaded or unloaded is a fault. One safe point reconciles up to four times. `Sim::start_session` holds a fixed ticket, reconciles, places the entity, holds `Ticket::player` and releases the fixed ticket. The store is a `SimStore`: a `TractStore` that can be copied and printed. | Design, from joining tickets and loading to the kernel | 02 §9.5 has the simulation empty a tract between two steps of the world's (D-373), and an entity's ticket covers nothing until the entity has been placed, which needs a loaded tract. A ticket that followed a command only at the next wake-up would leave the world behind the player for as long as nothing was scheduled. Keeping the id of a stored entity spares its components a round trip through a bundle and keeps every reference to it valid. `Sim` is cloned by tests of determinism, which a bare `Box<dyn TractStore>` cannot be. | 02 §9.4, §9.5, §10.2; 03 §4.3. The shelf is saved and hashed with the kernel's queues. Wake-ups of a shelved entity are cancelled (T9) until package T7 takes timers off the timeline first. `StartSession` is refused only for what can be told without loading; a later failure is a fault. A store that stands for a file is cloned as another handle on it. |
| D-462 | The handler of the `World` wake-up domain calls `WorldCtx::handle_wake` inside `Sim::world`. The simulation's blob carries what the world has under way (`Cosmos::processes`), and `Sim::load` puts it back into the cosmos it is given. For `occupant_unsupported` and `occupant_exposed` the kernel emits, after the world's event, `entity_unsupported` and `entity_exposed` with the entity as `self`, and does nothing to the entity. The world's occupant events all bind the occupant as `target`. The kernel's placeholder for an entity's channel slots is `ChannelSlots`; `ChannelSet` is the action module's set of channel ids. | Design, from joining damage and collapse to the kernel | D-385 leaves the process table to whoever saves the timeline, which is the simulation. The world says what happened to an occupant and leaves the consequence to the simulation (02 §14.3); falling and being hurt belong to packages that are not built, and a rule attached to an entity listens for events in which it is `self`. Two types named `ChannelSet` met when the kernel and the action definitions were merged. | 03 §4.1, §4.3, §13; 02 §14.3. Once the cosmos has a store of its own (W10) it may save its activity too: restoring the same value twice is harmless. Every world event is now a registered event type of the simulation. |
| D-463 | `clash_core::Value`, the value a variable stores, holds everything the expression language can compute: beside the four plain kinds, a list, a map, an id (the number of its definition type and its number within it), a handle (the number of its kind and 64 bits) and null, which occurs only inside a list or a map. It converts to and from the expression crate's `Value` without loss (`Value::to_stored`, `From<&clash_core::Value>`), and the two hash the same. A stored value nests at most 32 deep. | Design, from building variable storage | 05 §3 says variables are stored as `Value`, which can hold any type, and a variable may be declared `list<int>` or `map<real>` with a default of that shape; the foundation's value held four kinds and was marked to grow. A second store beside the world's for the wider values would split one variable over two places and leave the world's own events and saves blind to half of them. The codec writes 128 levels at most, and a value sits inside the records that hold it. | 05 §3, §6. Additive changes to `clash-core` and `clash-expr`: the enum was `non_exhaustive`, the new variants come last, and what a blob already holds reads as before. The two ids are runtime numbers until saves go through the id table (K7). |
| D-464 | The simulation serves the kinds `entity`, `loc`, `element`, `area`, `site`, `tract`, `realm` and `global`. In Rust a subject is a `Subject`, which gained the last five; in an expression it is a handle whose 64 bits are: an entity's id; a location packed as grid (10 bits), `x` and `y` (21 each), `z` (10) and two bits that are zero for a cell and the layer plus one for an element; an area's, a site's or a realm's id; a realm's id (16 bits) with a tract's `x` and `y` (24 each); zero for the simulation. What lies beyond a packing has no handle and reads as null. A handle whose subject is gone reads as null and never as another thing. | Design, from building subjects | 05 §3 gives a handle 64 bits and leaves their meaning to the simulation. A location is four numbers of 32 bits; the packing covers every grid, realm and storey the engine can plausibly hold and refuses the rest outright instead of wrapping. Entity ids carry a generation and the world's ids are never reused, so staleness needs no table. An element shares its cell's packing so that `here.block` costs nothing to make. | 05 §5.1; `clash_sim::subject`. 1024 grids, 1,048,576 cells each way from the origin, 512 storeys; 65,536 realms and 8,388,608 tracts each way for a tract's handle. The other kinds of 05 §5.1 are registered by their packages, which add a variant to `Subject` and a row to the kinds. |
| D-465 | The simulation's half of the registry is tables of `PropertyDef` and `FunctionDef`: names and types as the language writes them, documentation, flags, and a plain function to read, set or compute. `register_engine` enters in any registry whatever of them it lacks, with the kinds, the definition types the property types name, and the kernel's and the world's events; `engine_registry()` is the built-ins plus all of it. The loader's registry step of this crate calls it and then enters the events mods define. A simulation joins the registry of its definitions to the tables by name (`Bindings`). Every event's subjects are followed by `actor` and `instigator`; a subject bound only sometimes is registered as bound and is null when it is not. | Design, from building properties | 05 §5.3 splits the registry in two and leaves the simulation's half open. Ids depend on the order of registration, which a test's own registry or a later package changes; names do not.
