A destroyed part leaves a broken item. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §7.5 hands the part on and leaves making the item to items. Keeping the part whole lets a later graft (06 §6.5) put back what came off. | 06 §7.5, §9.2; package E8. `item::part_item` takes the `Detached` value: the body's events are not yet raised with it, so nothing listens for them. |
| D-566 | The verbs of holding take the names 06 §9.2 gives (`take`, `drop`, `give`, `wear`, `remove`, `stow`, `put_in`, `take_out`), with `create_item`, `split`, `destroy_item`, `set_color`, `repair_item` and `materialise_contents` beside them, each returning the entity that now holds what it moved. An argument missing or of the wrong kind is refused with `item.bad_argument`. Beyond the events 06 §13 listed, `item_repaired`, `item_split` and `item_merged` are raised. The hooks `item_taking` and `item_leaving` carry `holder` and `hold`, and are not raised when a part that can no longer hold lets go or when a holder is removed. A debug command `CreateItem` makes items by qualified id. | Design, from building the beta | A rule that moves a stack needs to know which entity it ended up in, and a presentation needs to see stacks change. Distance and strength are an action's `requires`, not the verbs'. | 06 §9.2, §13; package E8. The item events are entered into the simulation's bus with its own and into the engine's registry. |
| D-567 | Every property of items reads truth and none is safe for belief. `held(hold)`, `carried` and `worn_on(part)` yield what belongs to the same owner, so that a character may read what it holds, wears and carries and what its own pack holds, and not what is inside another's. `holder` is `container` under the name 06 §13 gives. Containers' contents are lists until queries are built. `entity.color` is settable through `set_color`. | Design, from building the beta | 05 §7.3: a character reads its own. | 06 §13; package E8. The engine's registry enters the `item` and `channel` definition types before its properties, so that `id<item>` and `id<channel>` parse without a loader. |
| D-568 | A thing let go of lies where its holder is: in the holder's cell, or inside whatever encloses the holder, passing up through whatever carries it (a grasp, worn, stowed, fixed) to whatever does not (a locker, a seat). Its height in the cell is not kept. Removing a holder leaves what it held where it was, whole, as the kernel's `remove_entity` already does. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §9.2 says "where the holder is" without the case of a holder inside something. | 06 §9.2; package E8. |
| D-569 | Left out of items for now: ageing (06 §9.4; `ages` and `keeps` are read, checked and carried), `look`, `round`, `interactions` and `device`, `content_table`, what `grants`, `traits`, `rules`, `guards` and `contains` give, what seats and fixed holds mean (`item::place_in` records them), a check of an item's colour names against `color` definitions, the property `age`, the test script `the_larder` and benchmarks. The simulation's save layout is version 4, for the stores of `Item` and `Holds`. | Design, from building the beta | Each waits for a package not built: temperature, actions, traits, the rule runtime's binding, exposure, substances, devices, vehicles, presentation. | Plan E8 is `beta`. |
| D-590 | The application keeps everything outside the scene in one shared state, which the scene's host and its `AppState` and the frame loop each borrow for the length of one call. The loop alone calls `advance`: while the player's character is busy, in steps of 100 ms of simulation time, until it is free, a decision is asked or 8 ms of a 16 ms frame are spent, draining and showing the events after each step. Headless, a pass advances until the character is free, with a fixed step. Starting a game closes every screen and opens the one of the role `play`; closing the play screen ends the game. | Design, from building the application | The scene owns its host and its application and hands neither back, and the loop must still reach the simulation. A loop that waited for the simulation would freeze the terminal; one that advanced without showing would break 12 V3 and V4. A role that is opened and closed needs no service for "end game", and a pause menu leaves a game with the effects it already has. | 12 §3, §3.2. `clash_app::game::Game::pass`, `run_frontend`, `SIM_STEP`, `SIM_SLICE`, `FRAME_TIME`. |
| D-591 | (Superseded by D-750 and D-758: the `scenario` type exists.) Until game scenarios exist, a game begins from a `start` definition, registered by `clash-scene`: `name`, `doc`, `realm`, `at` (`[x, y]` or `[x, y, z]`) and `glyph`. `app.scenarios` lists the starts; `new_game { scenario }` takes one, the first if none is named. Until character creation exists, `app.creation` is a map of `name` and `seed`, set by a new service, `edit_creation { field, value }`, and a screen variable named `creation_<field>` is set to that field when its screen opens. The bundled starts are in `mods/core/data/starts.json`. | Design, from building the application | The `scenario` type of 13 is not built, and a game needs a list of ways to begin that a mod can extend. A start is the smallest scenario: one world, one place, one ready-made character. The new-game screen needs a name and a seed from the player and a way to start with the seed the command line gave, and a screen's variables are its only state. | 12 §3.4, §10.4; 13 §3. `clash_scene::StartDef`. The type is deleted when `scenario` lands, and `creation` becomes the character spec of 06 §10.3. |
| D-592 | The player's character is made by one function, `make_player`: a bare creature, with no body or attributes, put in the world by `start_session` at the start's place and given direct control. Its glyph is the start's. Its name is what the player typed, which the host answers as `name()` of the character while it has no `Identity`. | Design, from building the application | Templates and spawning are being built by another package. One seam keeps the change to a single function when they land. | `clash_app::session::make_player`. A character with a body comes from a template; the start's `glyph` then goes. |
| D-593 | Until the action runtime exists, a stand-in module carries out the bundled controls: `step { dir }` checked against the world with a walker's movement profile (walls, closed doors, drops and corners refuse, with the reason), done by a debug move, and owing the simulation the step's length at 1.4 m/s; `go_to { to }` over the cells the character has seen; the command `wait { dur }`. Anything else is refused with a reason in the log. A character with nothing to do is asked a free decision. | Design, from building the application | The simulation's `Command` holds debug commands only, and the first walk needs walking. Checking the step as a walker would keeps the stand-in honest about walls and shut doors, and owing time keeps the clock moving as it will. Planning over seen cells keeps `go_to` from reading truth (01 R11). | 12 §3.2. `clash_app::shim`, deleted when `Command::Act` lands; `host::command_for` then builds the command. |
| D-594 | (The feeding superseded by D-656 and D-700, the leaving by D-706.) Until perception exists, a stand-in feeds the view model from sight: every known cell becomes remembered, then the field of view from the character's cell, an eye 1.6 m up and 40 m of reach, is observed with its light, and the occupants in it become sprites; other creatures are drawn as the suspected mark. While a preference whose bare name is `omniscient` holds true the view model is filled from truth instead; switching either way empties the view model, and the preference is never kept between runs. Until the `tools` mod exists, `ui` defines it with a control on F12 that switches it only while the preference `ui:development` holds, which no screen offers and the development profile `profiles/dev.json` sets. | Design, from building the application | The play screen must already behave as belief will: walk into a house and the street behind the wall goes to memory. The omniscient view is a debugging tool (12 §5) and must leave nothing it learnt from truth behind, in memory or in the next run. A `tools` mod is being built with the console, and a second one now would collide with it and with the tests that list the bundled mods. | 12 §3.2; 04 §3.5. `clash_app::perception_shim`, deleted when perception lands; `prefs::kept`. The toggle moves to `tools`, and the dev profile lists that mod, when it exists. |
| D-595 | The command line is `clash [--profile name] [--mods dir] [--seed n] [--data dir] [--headless script]`. Mods are found by `--mods`, else `mods` in the working directory, beside the executable and up to three directories above it. The user's data directory is `--data`, `CLASH_DATA_DIR`, `XDG_DATA_HOME`, `APPDATA` on Windows, the macOS application-support directory, then `~/.local/share`, each with `clash2d`; it holds `preferences.json`, a `mods` directory searched first, and `saves`. A command line that cannot be read leaves with code 2; a profile that cannot be loaded prints located diagnostics and leaves with code 1 having drawn nothing; with no terminal and no script the application says so and leaves with success. | Design, from building the application | No dependency is taken for finding a data directory, so the platforms' variables are read by hand. A person who starts the binary in a pipe by mistake is told what to do instead of seeing an error. | 12 §3.1; 04 §3.3. `clash_app::cli`, `paths`, `prefs`. |
| D-596 | A headless script is a file of lines: a key as a keymap writes it, `type text`, `wait [n]`, `frame`, `frame golden`, `size cols rows`, comments. Each line of input is followed by one pass on the null frontend at 80 × 24. A line that is none of these stops the run with its number. | Design, from building the application | Tests and agents need to go from the title screen into play without a terminal, and to read frames as text. | 12 §3.3. `clash_app::headless`. |
| D-597 | (The backend that saves nothing is superseded by D-703.) Saving goes through one trait, `SaveBackend`: `available`, `list`, `save`, `load`, `delete`. The binary's backend saves nothing and refuses each service with "saving is not built into this binary yet"; `app.can_save` and `app.can_load` are then false, and the pause menu offers Save and Load only while they hold. A failed service opens the screen of the new role `error`, with the reason as `text`. Events are shown by one function, `present_events`, until playback exists: each marks the view to be fed and each with a message gets one log line. | Design, from building the application | The save crate is being written beside the application; one trait makes joining it a few lines. A button that can only fail is worse than none. A reason in the log alone is easily missed when a menu covers the play screen. | 12 §3.2, §10.7. `clash_app::saves`, `present`. The role `error` is optional in a profile. |
| D-598 | `ui:options` moves from `play.json` to the new `app.json` with the title, new-game, pause, load and error screens; Esc in play is the new control `ui:pause`. The default profile boots into `ui:title` and names `ui:error` for `error`. | Design, from building the application | The options screen was an empty placeholder in the play screens' file; it now sets preferences and binds keys, and belongs with the screens the application opens. | 12 §10.7; `profiles/default.json`. |
| D-570 | The one-step verb is `step`, not `move`: `who`, `dir` (one of ten directions written as text: `north`, `north_east`, `east`, `south_east`, `south`, `south_west`, `west`, `north_west`, `up`, `down`) and `drop`. Like every movement verb it takes no time: the action's phase waits `self.step_time(dir)` and the verb is the effect that ends it. Beside the verbs 06 §11 named there are `move_to`, `plan_path`, `follow_path`, `clear_path` and `set_movement`. | Design, from building movement | The play screen's controls already start an action named `step` with a `dir` (D-531), and an action's phases own time (03 §6.4): a verb that waited would take the time twice. | 06 §11. `sweep`, `leap`, `set_altitude` and `teleport` are not built. |
| D-571 | What an entity can do to move is kept in a component, `mover`: the ways of moving it has been given, its posture, the factor that posture put on its height, the path it follows, and where it set off while the world carries it as a body. A creature given none moves by every `movement` flagged `default`, a new field; anything else given none does not move by itself. A way of moving counts only while the posture allows it, the body has the slots of its `uses` less what the posture `occupies`, and its `speed` comes to more than zero. `movement` also gained `name`, `rough` and `climb`. The simulation's save version is 4. | Design, from building movement | 06 §11 did not say how a creature comes to have its ways of moving, and templates (E9) are not built: a default lets the bundled walker walk, and a list set by whoever builds a creature lets a fish not walk. Rough ground and stairs slow movers differently. | 06 §11, §11.1, §13. `set_modes` and the verb `set_movement`. |
| D-572 | Each surface is given its meaning in the world's geometry: which cells a mover may be in and which steps it makes (06 §11). Every surface but `solid` is stopped by what stands taller than `step_up` and does not cut corners. A step takes its length, times `rough` into a rough cell and `climb` between storeys, over the speed of the quickest way of moving that makes it (the first listed on a tie), in the mover's own time. Its cost to a search is that time times the mover's best speed, and never below its length. | Design, from building movement | 06 §11 named the surfaces without saying what each allows, and the world's `MoveRules` asks only which cells and at what cost (D-315). Metres at the best speed keep the cheapest path the quickest and the search's estimate admissible. | 06 §11. `MoverRules` and `ModeRule` in `clash_sim::movement`. |
| D-573 | A mover that blocks movement is refused a cell another such occupant fills on a plane they share (`move.occupied`), unless exactly one does, both fill one cell, and a handler of the hook `passage_requested` sets its field `yields`: then both are relocated in the one step. `move_to` asks nobody to yield. | Design, from building movement | 06 §11 named the hook without its fields or what a handler does. A mutable field is how other hooks answer (D-368); swapping movers wider than a cell would need a rule for which cells each takes. | 06 §11. |
| D-574 | A path is planned from what the mover believes, through the functions of `movement::belief`, which read truth until perception is built; through what can be opened, at 2 m of walking; and round the cells a refused step of a journey to the same goal taught the mover it cannot enter. The path is kept in the `mover` component. `go_to` is two phases, `plan` and `stride`, that loop through `next` on the properties `path_left`, `path_stale` and `path_next`. The properties that read the world beyond the mover (`can_step`, `step_fault`, `step_time`, `can_reach`, `path_cost`) read what it believes; none is safe for belief (D-466). | Design, from building movement | The action definition type has no list-typed local and no repeating phase, so the path lives with the mover, where it is saved already. Each refusal takes a cell away from the next plan, so the loop ends. A door shut since the plan was made is found at the step, which is checked against truth (01 R11). | 06 §11. The perception package replaces the bodies of `believed_rules`, `believed_step` and `believed_path`, and nothing that calls them changes. |
| D-575 | Falling is movement's: the kernel calls `movement::on_event` for every event once its handlers have run, and on `entity_unsupported` an entity that cannot stay where it is falls. `fell` is raised and the entity is launched as a body of its own, at rest from the middle of its cell, with the mass, volume, cross-section and planes it has; the world carries it down. Where it set off is kept in the `mover` component from `body_launched`, and on `body_landed` `landed` is raised with the height below that, the speed `sqrt(2 g h)` and the energy `m g h`; so for any entity the world carries, whoever launched it. While carried, it is refused steps and placements (`move.in_flight`). One the world will not take as a body is dropped to `resting_cell` at once. A step into a cell with no footing is refused unless the mover can be there or the step says `drop`. | Design, from building movement | 03 §13 leaves what falling does to the movement package, and 02 §15 makes whoever falls the world's until it lands. The blows themselves are the world's `body_impact`; `landed` is the measure of the fall that a rule can read without following a flight. | 06 §11. Catching a falling entity and a body that leaves loaded space are not handled. |
| D-576 | A posture is held in the `mover` component; being in none changes nothing. `set_posture` raises the hook `posture_changing`, which may cancel, scales the height of the entity's `Physical` from its share in the old posture to its share in the new, and raises `posture_changed` with the new posture's `look`. A shape given while in a posture is taken to be the height in that posture. | Design, from building movement | 06 §11.1 scales a body's height, and the simulation does not hold bodies yet; `Physical` is what sight and cover read now. | 06 §11.1. A body's part bands, a posture's `traits` and `limp` wait for bodies in the simulation. |
| D-600 | Playback is a queue of batches, each one stretch between two decisions, of events grouped by tick. Between groups simulated time counts for at most two seconds; the rest is scaled so that the stretch plays in `stretch_time` from its first group that shows something to its last, and a group that only changes the picture never makes it longer. A `min_time` holds the clock into the next stretch. The preference `playback_speed` scales real time and 0 plays everything at once with no effects. A backlog of more than a second of real time plays faster in proportion. The key the keymap names `skip` jumps to the end and does nothing else; while no key is so named, any key does, and then does what it does. A decision's prompt opens once playback has caught up. The application calls `push_events`, `advance`, `playback_caught_up` and `skip_playback`. | Design, from building playback | 12 §7.1 named `stretch_time` and `min_time` and said playback is scaled down to instant, and not how a long wait is played, how a stretch of one event is timed, or how playback that falls behind ever catches up. A step that only moves something out of sight should not stretch a fight. | 12 §7, §7.1, §9.5. `clash_scene::playback::Playback`; the two seconds, the second of catching up and how long a marker stays are constants a test can change, not settings. |
| D-601 | A `show` has `on`, `filter`, `if`, `change`, `anim`, `at`, `to`, `log`, `category`, `heard`, `shake`, `flash`, `time`, `min_time`, `importance` and `sound`. Its changes are `cell`, `field`, `sprite` and `gone`, each naming a subject of the event; left out, everything the event is about is looked at again, and `[]` changes nothing. `at` and `to` name subjects bare. `weight` is replaced by `importance` (`minor`, `normal`, `major`), which a `log` widget filters by and the new preference `effects` (`all`, `major`, `none`) uses to thin animations, glides, shake and flash. An `anim` has `time`, `frames` and `travel`. Floating `text`, `particles`, effects with parameters and dropping animations that overrun a stretch are not built. | Design, from building playback | 12 §7.3 gave an example and no fields, said nothing of how a show changes the picture, and wrote `"at": "event.here"`, which is no expression a scope has. Dropping by `weight` needs a measure of overrunning that nothing defines; one importance serves the log, the preference and later the cutscenes. | 12 §7.3, §7.4, §10.4. `mods/ui/data/playback.json` defines `effects`. A show that names an animation that does not exist, or a travelling one without `to`, is a load error found by a cross check. |
| D-602 | A show's `log`, `heard.log` and `if` are compiled in the scope of its event, pure, as a mind context with nobody's own: they read the event's subjects and fields and whatever is safe for belief, and the reader helpers are answered by the host for the viewing character. A finished line gets a capital first letter. A show about an event that is not registered is a load error where any event is; in a load with no events at all, as a tool without the simulation's registry makes, a show is read and checked and shows nothing. | Design, from building playback | A line is read by one character and must speak from what that character knows (01 R11, 12 V9). The gallery and the toolkit's tests load the `ui` mod without the simulation, and must not fail on its shows. | 12 §7.3; 05 §4.6, §7.3. `clash_scene::testing::registry` holds the simulation's kinds and its kernel and world events, so that shows compile in tests as they do in the game. |
| D-603 | The host decides what the party perceived: `SceneHost::perceived(event, picture)` gives `Seen`, `Heard { from }` or `Not`, and `seen_cell` and `seen_sprite` say what is seen of a cell and how a thing is perceived. Each is asked once, when the batch arrives; playing asks nobody anything, so a step is drawn with what the party knows at the end of its batch. The last change a batch makes to a sprite leaves it as the party then perceives it, and one no longer perceived leaves the picture at the last event that moves it. A heard event puts a marker and writes the `heard` line, and changes no cell or sprite. `clash_scene::testing::FakeHost` stands in: seen if a cell the event is about is seen in the picture, or a thing it is about stands in one, the actor not counting; heard within twelve cells of `view`. | Design, from building playback | Perception (15 §13) is not built, and the scene must not read truth (R11). Asking once makes playing pure and repeatable, and makes the picture after a batch what a fresh view would be (12 V4) whatever the events carried. | 12 §5, §7.5. All four calls default to nothing, so a host that does not answer shows nothing. |
| D-604 | A log line has its text with style tags, a category, an importance, the tick of the event and a count. A line the same as the one before in all but tick is folded into it, which takes the newer tick, and is shown with ` (xN)` after it in the theme's dim style. The log keeps its newest 2000 lines. The `log` widget gains `importance`, the least a line must have; it wraps at spaces, a column narrower when its scrollbar is drawn, follows the newest line unless scrolled back, and scrolled back stays on what it shows as lines arrive. | Design, from building the log | 12 §7.4 said the log holds lines with their tick and a category, and nothing of how many, how repeats read or how a long line is shown. A collapse writes the same line a dozen times. | 12 §7.4, §10.3. `clash_scene::playback::MessageLog` replaces the store of package U6a; `Scene::log_mut` is where `message` effects go until the simulation reports them. |
| D-605 | Bindings on the view show the moment playback has reached: before evaluating them the scene calls `SceneHost::as_of` with the tick before the next event to be played, or with none once caught up, and it evaluates them again whenever an event is played. A host that cannot answer as of a moment answers as of the present. | Design, from building playback | 12 §10.4 required it, and the scene holds no history of the simulation; only the host can answer as of a moment. Re-evaluating at each event played is what makes a bar drop with the blow. | 12 §10.4, V4. The simulation implements `as_of`. |
| D-606 | What playback draws is never part of the picture. Each frame, effects in progress are written into the view's `ViewFx` (sprites hidden from their cells, glyphs over cells, frame sprites between cells on layers 1 to 3) and the frame's shake and flash. A glide lasts its show's `time`, since events carry no duration; a move of two cells or more leaves three afterimages; a flash begins at 0.6 of full strength; a heard marker stays one and a half seconds of real time. A sprite of a screen beneath does not show through a screen above it. | Design, from building playback | 12 §7.2 asked for glides and trails and the frame had sprites and effects, with nothing saying how a view gives them. Keeping effects out of the view model keeps the check of V4 exact. | 12 §7.2, §7.3. `clash_scene::view::ViewFx`, `Playback::render`. |
| D-607 | The bundled shows, in `mods/ui/data/playback.json`, cover every event of `KERNEL_EVENTS` and `WORLD_EVENTS`: steps glide and write no line, destruction looks at the cell again, animates and writes a line by layer, by how and by whether there is an actor, collapse and blasts shake, a field's coming and going changes the cell's field, a body is drawn where it is launched, strikes and lands, someone thrown has a line when they go flying and when they land, and what changes nothing seen is said to with `"change": []`. Events registered by other packages, such as hits and wounds, have no bundled show yet, because they are registered only where those packages' types are loaded and a show about an unregistered event is a load error. | Design, from building playback | The `ui` mod must load wherever the engine's own events are registered, including tests over the fake host. A line about a block cannot name what it was, since nothing safe for belief says. | 12 §7.3, §13. A content mod or the combat packages add shows for their events. |
| D-608 | The check that caught-up playback equals a fresh view, `clash_scene::playback::check_caught_up(played, fresh)` and `Scene::check_caught_up(fresh)`, compares cells, sprites and grid shapes, not heard markers, and reports the first eight differences as a `ViewDrift`. The application makes the fresh view model and calls it in debug builds. | Design, from building playback | 12 §5 asked debug builds to check and the scene cannot make a fresh view itself: that is perception's. A marker of something heard is shown for a while and is not knowledge. | 12 §5. A property test plays random batches over a small map and checks after each. |
| D-550 | Stats and bodies are wired into the simulation beside their modules (`stats::bind`, `body::bind`, `life`). Their tables (`StatDefs`, `BodyDefs`, the vital and template tables) are not state: they are made with the simulation and after a load. A function of the stats that evaluates expressions is lent the store of stats and the timeline while the host reads the simulation through an empty store and a copy of the timeline's clocks (`Timeline::clocks_only`); functions that evaluate nothing run over the stores as they stand. Every change a function returns is raised as its event, in order; `pool_add` raises `pool_changing` first; a `MassChanged` is written to the kernel's and the world's records; a `FunctionChanged`, a change of mass and a change of a variable a stat reads directly work the stats out again. The stats' and the bodies' events bind the cause's `actor` and `instigator`. The verbs of 06 §4.4, §6.5 and §7 are in the engine's verb table; a `key` names a source of kind `rule` by its FNV-1a hash; a part and a wound name their owner, so body verbs take no `who`. | Design, from building the beta | The modules were built as functions over stores (D-449, D-520); the kernel had to hold them without either module knowing the simulation, and an expression of a stat must be able to read the simulation while the stats are being changed. | 06 §4.4, §6.5, §7.6; plans/first-playable.md E2, E4, E5. `cargo test -p clash-sim --test life`. |
| D-551 | Pools and bodies keep their wake-ups in the `Timer` domain, told apart by tag bits of `arg`; the simulation's default handler of the domain runs them (`life::handle_wake` for a package with timers of its own) and reports any other timer as an unhandled wake-up. Whenever the kernel puts an entity on the clock that now applies, it calls the wiring, which moves the pools and the body to that clock, suspends them when the entity leaves the world (shelved, stored, removed) and resumes them when it returns. | Design, from building the beta | The kernel does not know these wake-ups as the entity's; following the kernel's one place where clocks are settled catches relocation, areas and personal clocks alike. | 06 §4.2, §7.3; 03 §9. |
| D-552 | Pools and wounds are `Pause` timers (03 §9): an entity that leaves the simulation and returns resumes where it stood, and no time passes for its pools and wounds while it is away. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §4.2 allowed either ("none if they were paused"); time away for a creature that is not simulated is the cold simulation's (09), which does not exist yet, and a wound that kept bleeding while nobody looked would kill people behind the player's back. | 06 §4.4; `shelving_and_restoring_a_wounded_person_changes_nothing`. |
| D-553 | Harm from the world: the simulation answers `occupant_exposed` after its own `entity_exposed` with a spread hit through `hit_incoming`; `occupant_dosed` raises the hook `exposure_incoming` (pool, amount, route, field) and adds what is left to the pool without `pool_changing`; the world's `body_impact` of an entity and its `body_hit_occupant` become a hit through `Sim::body_impact` (spread, or with the element's contact surface). A part that came off is the kernel event `part_detached`, which carries the `Detached`, and is then made an item by `item::part_item`; a hit goes through `item::worn_layers_of` and hands back `item::apply_worn_hits`; every body change is passed to `item::on_body_changes` after its events. Energy that exits with a direction strikes the block of the next cell along it and is reported as `hit_exited`; `slots_lost`, `slots_gained` and `body_destroyed` are raised from the body's changes. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §7.4 said what becomes a hit; it did not say who raises what. The route and guards belong to whoever holds the mask, so the hook is the seam. | 06 §7.4, §7.6. |
| D-554 | A mind is the component `Mind` (a behaviour profile as text and a vital state: awake, unconscious, dead); a driver is the component `Driver`. What is seated in a mind stays in the entity's stats and is taken out and put back by `stats::take_seated` and `give_seated` when a mind moves; control goes with the mind, and a mind's vital state is asked afresh of the body it arrives in. `Sim::can_act` is what a command for an entity is checked against: a mind that is awake, or a driver. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §8.1 sketched `MindStats` inside the mind; keeping seated stats in one store keeps one set of functions, events and properties for them. Unconsciousness is the body's, not the mind's. | 06 §8.4; plans/first-playable.md E7. |
| D-555 | When a creature stops is data: `needs` on a body plan (a function at zero capacity makes its owner unconscious or dead) and `stops` on a pool (its level on one side of one of its thresholds does). The worst holds, asked whenever a pool crosses a threshold, a body changes, a mind arrives or a creature is made. `dead` is `die`, after the cancellable `entity_dying`: control released, decisions forgotten, `entity_died` then `mind_released`; the entity remains. Unconsciousness lasts while something says so. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §8.3 left "when" to content and gave it no field; a rule could do it once rules run, but every creature needs it now and a field is checked at load. Nothing in the engine names blood or brains. | 06 §4.4, §8.4. |
| D-556 | The `template` type has the fields of 06 §10.4. Its contributions are fields of the template and of each option, written by one macro, because the loader cannot read flattened structs (`clash_data::de`). Traits, items and remains are carried as written ids, for the packages that give them, and named by `entity_spawned`. A glyph is read from the last template that gives one (`Sim::glyph`) and is not copied onto the entity. | Design, from building the beta | Traits (E3) and items (E8) are not built; carrying ids keeps templates loadable now. A glyph is presentation and belongs to the definition. | 06 §10.4; plans/first-playable.md E9. |
| D-557 | A character spec names templates by id and choices by dotted keys (template, choice, and option and choice for nested ones); selections are option or part ids, a number or a text. Choices are visited in the order written; random selections come from a stream seeded by the spec's `seed` alone, so the same spec and seed make the same entity (B4). Spawning is `Sim::spawn_template`, the verb `spawn`, the debug command `spawn` and the answer to a `spawn_requested` that names a template (anonymous, seeded by position). The entity's `Identity` names the first template as its definition. The creation session holds a spec and checks every change. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §10.3 described specs and creation as data without saying how a selection names its choice or what a screen edits. | 06 §10.4. |
| D-558 | `Identity` says whether an individual is anonymous (06 §12.2); anything else, and anything without an identity, is notable. Wounds, variables and `make_notable` make it notable and raise `became_notable`. The save layout version is 5 for `Mind`, `Driver` and this flag. | Design, from building the beta | Populations are not built; spawning needs only to mark what it makes from a tract's request as anonymous. | 06 §12.2. |
| D-559 | Left out of living things for now: the `creature` and `mind` types, populations, `apply_template` on an existing entity, adding `assemble` parts (waits for `add_part`), option conditions and a range's `apply`, a track's `on_advance`, beliefs moving with a mind, ending an entity's actions at death (the action runtime's answer to `entity_died`), and test scripts, since the simulation does not yet implement the console's host. | Design, from building the beta | Each waits for a package that is not built. | plans/first-playable.md E7, E9, `beta`. |
| D-660 | Character creation is one screen, `ui:create`, the role `creation`, reached from the new-game screen with F2 for the start selected there, and one popup, `ui:create_choice`, that shows the widget a choice's kind needs. Both read `app.creation`, the session as `CreationSession::to_value` gives it with `name`, `seed` and, where the application can say, `attributes`, and change it only through the `app` service `creation` (`begin`, `select`, `clear`, `name`, `seed`, `finish`), which the scene adds to its services. With no session the screen lists `app.templates`, a list the application gives; Begin is shown only while the session is complete, and finishes the character and asks for `new_game` at the start. A pick puts a new option in place of the first chosen once it has its count; an assemble choice adds a part with Enter and takes every one of it back with Backspace; a seed is rolled by a fixed rule from the last, since a binding may not be random. | Design, from building the beta | The contract the application implements, read whole, with no choice kind needing a screen of its own; effects cannot branch, so the popup branches by what it shows. | 12 §10.4, §10.5, §10.7; plans/first-playable.md U9. |
| D-661 | `open` and `replace` give a screen's variables their starting values as well as its parameters, by name in `with`, a variable taking only a value of its own kind; a name that is neither is an error. | Design, from building the beta | A screen that edits something must start from what it edits, and a variable's starting value is otherwise a literal. | 12 §10.5; `Scene::open_screen`. |
| D-662 | `app.keys(context)` lists what the keymap in force binds in a context: for each name, what its control is called, its `doc` and every key given it, in order of what it is called. `ui:help`, opened by the control `help` on ? and F1, lists the play context from it. | Design, from building the beta | A help screen written out would not follow the player's own bindings. | 12 §10.4, §10.7. |
| D-663 | A screen is told of the cell under the cursor also `cursor_things`, what the reader calls every perceived thing there, written out, and `cursor_ground`, the fixture, block and floor the view model holds there, top first, each by its definition's id without the namespace and its state's name; look mode shows both in a side panel with the slot `ui:look.side`. | Design, from building the beta | Look shows what the character perceives of a place and no more; element definitions have no name a reader uses. | 12 §6.3, §10.7. |
| D-664 | The inventory lists `view.carried`, each container before its contents, indented by container, with how each is held and how full each container is, beside the body as a tree of outer parts with what is worn over each; a thing's verbs are buttons in the popup `ui:item`, shown by how it is held, each starting the action of the item verb's name bare (`drop`, `wear`, `remove`, `stow`, `put_in`, `take_out`, `give`) with `what`, `container` and `to`. | Design, from building the beta | A list can be acted on row by row, where a tree's branches only open; actions are content, and an action no mod defines is refused by the simulation with its reason. | 12 §10.7. |
| D-665 | The simulation registers no property for a character's vital state, the pools and attributes it has, or a pool's thresholds, and this work could not add them to the simulation: the condition screen and the status column show none themselves, and content puts its reserves and attributes into the slots `ui:play.bars`, `ui:condition.pools` and `ui:condition.attributes`, as the new mod `core_ui` (`mods/core_ui/data/bars.json`) does for `core`, in the default profiles. | Design, from building the beta | A screen in the `ui` mod cannot name content's pools; slots are how content adds to a screen. | 12 §10.7, §13 gaps. |
| D-666 | The play screen's status column shows who the character is, the slot `ui:play.bars`, what it holds in a grasp written out by the reader, the slot `ui:play.side` and two lines naming the keys of play; the inventory and condition placeholders are replaced by their screens. | Design, from building the beta | The status column is the first thing a player reads, and every screen it opens is reached by a key it names. | 12 §10.7. |
| D-667 | A list written out of the character's own things, and an `if` whose branches both are, are the character's own. Of a value of type `any`, a property with arguments is read as `.name` is, as a map's entry, and then by each argument in turn, checked when it runs. | Design, from building the beta | A body's tree grows from one of its own parts; the bundled screens must load where no simulation registered the kinds, as 12 §10.4 promises, and calling a property of `any` was a load error. | 05 §3, §7.3. |
| D-668 | A `tree` whose rows come from an expression may say `"open": true` for its branches to start open. | Design, from building the beta | A body is read whole; a closed tree hides it. | 12 §10.3. |
| D-669 | The fake host of `clash_scene::testing` can stand over the registry of a real load (`FakeHost::over`), answering a property with a definition's id as argument by its qualified id and `list` by writing the names out, and its own registry holds the engine's properties of the kernel, items, movement and bodies beside its fakes; the fake application makes characters through a real creation session over the loaded templates (`FakeApp::making`), as the game binary answers it (12 §3.4), with `app.templates` and `attributes` beside. | Design, from building the beta | The bundled screens are tested as the game compiles them; the application is another package's. | `clash-scene` tests `screens.rs`; clash-app. |
| D-620 | The world's verbs are one table, `world_verbs()`, each a call of one world function inside `Sim::world` with the cause it is handed: `set_floor`, `set_block`, `set_fixture` (a state as data writes it, or null), `set_material`, `set_state`, `damage`, `destroy`, `check_stability`, `release`, `add_field`, `set_field`, `launch`, `impulse`, `arrest`, `create_area`, `remove_area` and `enclose`. A place argument takes a cell, an element (which names its layer) or, where a point is meant, an entity where it stands; a direction is three numbers or something to aim at. A release's shape is flat arguments (`shape`, `dir`, `half_angle`, `radius`, `range`, `burst`). `launch`, `impulse` and `arrest` take the entity that is the body. The world gains `WorldEdit::set_state_bits`, which changes an element's state and keeps its damage, and `WorldEdit::destroy`, which destroys by a kind of damage with nothing absorbed. Refusals beyond the world's own are `world.bad_argument`, `world.unknown_state`, `world.unknown_def`, `world.no_element` and `world.not_in_world`. | Design, from building the world's verbs | Setting an element clears its damage (02 §7.2), so a door opened by `set_element` would be mended by opening it. A rule must be able to destroy what has no energy behind it (a spell of unmaking, a demolition order) without guessing a packet big enough for a material it cannot read. A nested shape object would be an `any` the loader cannot check; a body is not a kind of subject. | 02 §7.2, §23 lists what is bound and what is not, each with why: tremors, devastation, subgrids, portals, realms, maps at a place, tickets, pending edits, planes, liquid levels, decals, steering and an area's clock rate. Two small additive functions in `clash-world`. |
| D-621 | A device is an entity of the occupant kind `device` with a `Device` component (its `device` definition and the element it is bound to), found again from its element by the occupants of the element's cell; no index is kept. An element is promoted when its state is not its first or when the verb `promote` asks, and demoted when it is back in its first state with nothing inside it, no variables, no attached rules and no tags of its own: tested on `element_changed`, when something leaves it, and when an interaction ends. `element_changed` is answered from what the cell holds when it is delivered, not from the event's old and new states. A destroyed or replaced element spills what its device held where something dropped there comes to rest (`device_spilled`) and the device goes. A device is a container by its definition's `holds`: items are stowed, anything else enclosed. The `device` type has only `name`, `holds` and `tags` for now; an element naming a device that does not exist is a warning at load. | Design, from building minimal devices | 02 §5.5 lets the device be dropped "when it is back in its default state" without saying what else must be true; a locker that held a coat or a rule's variable must stay. Events are delivered after the change that raised them, and a locker placed and then promoted before the pump would otherwise be unbound by its own placement. A second index of devices by element would be state to save and keep true; the world's occupant index already answers. 06 §9.2 has a person in a locker held `Inside`; items in it are within its room as in a pack. | 16 §4.3 as built; 06 §9.2. `Sim::relocate`'s callers do not tell devices: the kernel's pump calls `device::on_event` beside `movement::on_event`. The store of `Device` raises the simulation's save layout: version 8, after the stores of minds and perception. `item::hold::fits` asks `device::holds_of` for a container that is not an item. G2 adds modes, ports, stores, config, processes, slots and passive loads. |
| D-622 | An interaction declares what it is done to, `target: element` or `entity`, so that `target` is bound as a subject of that kind and its expressions are checked; its hooks and `interacted` bind the user as `self` and the target as `target` (an entity) or `element`. `shown_if` compiles in the user's mind context; `if`, `fail_text` and `do` read truth. What is offered is listed by `interactions_for`, which raises nothing and reads through the seam `interaction::belief` (truth until perception); `interaction_reach` is raised only by an attempt. An interaction is done by any action whose parameters are named `interaction` and `target`: a handler of `action_starting`, set up with the rule runtime, checks reach, `if` and the hook `interaction_starting` and refuses the action with the reason; the bundled `core:interact` lasts the interaction's `time` and ends with the verb `complete_interaction`, which checks again and runs `do`. The verb `interact` does both at once. `adjacent` includes the user's own cell and the cells straight above and below where they are joined. `network` is a stub: a device the user holds or that stands, or is named by an element, within a step shares a `network:` tag with the target's device. Devices gain `put_inside`, and entities `has_item`. | Design, from building interactions | 16 §3.1 types `target` by nothing, and the expression language reads no property of `any`. A listing that raised hooks would run rules for every menu drawn. The action runtime knows nothing of interactions and the engine may not name `core:interact`, while 16 §3.4 has the hook run before the action begins and a lock refuse it: the action's own hook is the moment, and parameter names are mechanism. Without networks (G3, G4) a terminal must still reach a door. A locked door needs to ask whether its key is carried, which no property answered. | 16 §3.5.1, §14. The application's shim still refuses a closed door; its play screen is to call `bump_default` on a refused step, `interactions_for` or `offers_near` for a menu, and submit `Act` with `core:interact`. |
| D-623 | The bundled interactions are in `mods/core/data/interactions.json`: `open`, `close` (refused while someone stands there), `switch`, `break_through`, `hide_inside`, `get_out`, `store` and `retrieve`, with the `locker` device, which holds 0.8 m³ and 300 kg. `data/world/usable.json` patches the `window` block with a closed and an open state (open, it lets air and a blast through and is still not passable, so that paths do not run through windows) and three interactions, and adds the `steel_locker` fixture, whose device is `locker`. An element's `interactions` or `device` that names nothing is a warning at load, not an error, and a load that defines no interaction or no device at all is not looked at. A key and the `lockable_door` with `lock` and `unlock` are in `locks.json`, and `core:interact` in `interact.json`. | Design, from writing the content of interactions | Doors, windows and lamps placed by the bundled maps and generators must be usable without changing the files that place them; `data/world/` holds only the world's types, because the crates below the simulation load it alone, and there an element names interactions and devices no definition provides: a warning keeps those loads whole while the linter, which loads everything, still reports a misspelling. | `mods/core`; 16 §3. A window keeps its look and its first state, so maps and golden frames are unchanged. |
| D-640 | A contest multiplies its comparison by `factor`, not `scale`, and adds logistic noise whose scale is its `spread`: a margin is `d + spread × ln(u / (1 − u))` for one uniform draw `u`, and `odds` is the chance it is at or above zero, `1 / (1 + e^(−d / spread))`, or with no spread 1 for `d ≥ 0` and 0 otherwise. In the ratio form a value at or below zero is less than any above zero. Every contest draws one number from the rule stream. A function the simulation registers may draw at random (`FunctionDef::drawing`): it is then refused in a pure context, computed with the evaluating host's stream, and null where nothing may be drawn. | Design, from building contests (C1) | `scale` is the loader's operation for multiplying a field (04 §6.1) and cannot be a field's name. 07 §4.1 named no distribution; the logistic gives `odds` in closed form, so a preview needs no sampling, and needs one draw. Aim lands at a margin of zero, so `odds` counts zero as a win. A function of the simulation was given only the simulation to read, which holds no stream it may draw from while an expression runs. | 07 §4.1. `clash_sim::combat::{ContestDef, contest_fn, odds_fn}`, `registry::DrawingCallee`. |
| D-641 | `strike` is an effect the rule compiler reads (`EffectKind::Strike`), listed in `rule::ACTION_EFFECTS`, not a verb: the host runs it (`RuleHost::strike`) with its arguments, its origin and the locals of the list, and the interpreter writes what came of it into `$landed`, `$absorbed` and `$passed`. It finds its attack through the definition its origin names; it is a load error in a rule, in an attack's own `on_land`, `on_miss` and `on_stopped`, and in an action without the attack it names, and `attack` may be left out of an action with one. An action with `attacks` is given those three locals as variables unless it declares them. `on_land`, `on_miss` and `on_stopped` run once for each target, binding `self` and `target`, on a copy of the locals. A strike resolved inside eight others is refused. The example of 03 §6.1 gains an `attacks` block. | Design, from building the strike (C2) | A verb's result cannot reach an action's locals: the action runtime copies them in and out around a list, and a verb is given only subjects. The attack's expressions read the action's locals, which the frame running the effect holds. An attack's own lists run while the strike is being resolved, on a frame of their own; a strike from one of them could recurse without end. | 07 §3.1, §3.4; 05 §10.7; 03 §6.1; D-401. `clash_sim::combat::{run_strike, check_strikes, Attacks}`, `rule::{Strike, StrikeCall, StrikeOutcome}`. |
| D-642 | Delivery as built. A target is in reach when the distance between the middles of the two cells at their footing, less one cell, is within `reach`; `reach` defaults to the reach of the part that strikes, plus a held item's length. `with` finds the first working part that answers to a name and has the surface (one that grasps nothing first), or the first item held with a tag and the surface; a part's surface scales with the part. What exits a target strikes the block behind it (06 §7.4); where there is none it carries on to the next target of a `through` arc, or, with no arc, to the creature standing behind, up to 16, unaimed, out of reach and unpushed; where there is one, the strike ends. An arc without `through` strikes each target with the whole energy, each a strike of its own. A line takes the cells within half a cell of the line to the target named, in order of distance; a sweep those in reach within half its angle, in order of bearing. Recoil is a packet of the surface's kind at `returned / (area × give)`: a hit on the part, or damage to the item. A hit cancelled by `hit_incoming` is a stopped strike. A strike at a cell strikes its block, else fixture, else floor. In `mods/core`, `cut` severs. | Design, from building delivery (C2) | 07 gave reach in metres and cells are a metre wide: from middle to middle neighbours would be out of reach of a fist. The body wiring already spends what exits on the block behind; carrying the same energy to a creature beyond a wall would count it twice. 07 §6.4 did not say what an arc without `through` does, nor how a line or a sweep orders its targets. No damage type of the bundled content severed, so no cut could take anything off. | 07 §3.1, §3.2, §3.4, §6.1 to §6.4. `combat::strike::{gap, arc_targets, intensity_of, MAX_CARRIED}`, `combat::surface::find`; `mods/core/data/world/severing.json`. Swings meeting the world, contact surfaces and `closing_energy` are not built. |
| D-643 | Aim as built: one margin of the attack's contest, the values evaluated with the target bound, drawn from the rule stream; at or above zero on the mark, within `near` below on a part beside it, else a miss. The parts beside a part are the part it is `on` and those `on` it, there and outside, one chosen by exposed area with a second draw; with none, or no part aimed at, the body picks by exposure. The bundled `melee` contest is a ratio, so that the bundled `evasion`, zero for a target that does not perceive its attacker through `perceives` (15 §6), makes such a target always hit where it was aimed. | Design, from building aim (C3) | 07 §4.2 named "a neighbouring part" without saying which parts neighbour. With logistic noise no finite difference is certain; a ratio against nothing is, so "simply hit" is a property of the content rather than a rule about awareness in the engine. | 07 §4.2. `combat::aim::{aim, beside, mark}`; `mods/core/data/combat/fighting.json`. Reach in height (07 §4.3) and cover by posture are not built. |
| D-644 | Push as built: `sqrt(2 × energy × push × (1 − brace) / mass)` with the energy that reached the target and its `Physical` mass, in the strike's direction on the level raised by `lift` degrees; `knockback_incoming` carries `speed` and `dir`. A target the world carries takes all of it as an impulse, at any speed. Otherwise below `combat.stagger_at` nothing, below `combat.launch_at` `staggered` with a severity from 0 to 1, and at or above it the attack's `knock_down` posture is taken and the target is launched as a body from its cell's middle at half its height, with its mass, volume, cross-section and planes, for movement to take back on landing. Only a strike that landed pushes. Nothing braces until defences exist. | Design, from building push (C5) | 07 §7 named `stagger_at` and `launch_at` outside a reserved namespace (D-236), gave no measure of a stagger's severity, and did not say where a launched body starts or when a posture is taken. A body launched at floor level would scrape along the floor; a posture taken in flight would change the shape of a body the world is carrying. | 07 §7, §7.1, §16.1. `combat::push::{push, push_speed, push_dir}`. A leap and `closing_energy` (07 §3.5) are not built. |
| D-670 | A `region_kind` has `tracts` (weights are numbers), `patch_size`, `sites`, `settlements`, `paths`, `weather`, `population`, `glyph` and `tags`. Until world generators exist, the `standard` generator gives regions kinds with `regions` (weighted, by the positional hash of the region) and places settlements by hand with `settlements` (each `{ settlement, at, seed }`, inside the realm, meeting no other). A tract's definition is the layout's, else its settlement's `tract`, else its region kind's by patch, else the realm's `tracts`. | Design, from building region kinds | 10 §8.1 named `patch`, which the loader keeps for merging (04 §6.3) and refuses as a number; its weights were expressions over fields no realm plan gives yet; and nothing could choose a region's kind or place a settlement before WG6. | 10 §6.2, §8.1, §8.2, §13; plans/worldgen.md WG7. A kind's `settlements`, `weather` and `population` are carried. When `worldgen` definitions land, `regions` and `settlements` move into the realm plan. |
| D-671 | A `path_kind` has a `width` (1 to 15), a `surface` floor and an optional `edge` strip of another floor on each side (1 to 8 cells); `max_grade`, `crossings` and `current` are carried. A path line is kept as straight stretches of its centre line (`clash_gen::region::Seg`); a region's record holds, for each line crossing it, the cells of its centre line inside the region at the height field's surface. The ground step `paths` lays the floor in the surface cell of each column a path crosses, empties the fixture there, keeps a ramp, and lays nothing under a levelled or founded map. | Design, from building surveys | 10 §8.5 gave a path kind one floor and no width rule, and a street is asphalt between pavements. The realiser sees no region record (D-370), so the cells a path lays must follow from position; a ramp kept under the floor keeps the path walkable over rolling ground. | 10 §5.1, §8.5; 02 §9.1 (`PathLine`). Crossings are not built: a path lies on the ground whatever it meets. |
| D-672 | Paths between regions cross an edge at points drawn from the positional hash of the edge (the eastern or southern region and the edge's direction), an eighth of a region from either corner; how many is drawn between the lower of the two kinds' `per_edge` lows and highs, and the path is of the western or northern region's kind. Each region lays its part in two straight stretches from the crossing to its hub: the centre of the first settlement centred in it, or a cell of its middle half drawn from its hash. No path is laid inside a settlement. | Design, from building surveys | 10 §8.3 said minor paths cross at points derived from a hash of the edge and said nothing of where they go or how two different kinds agree. Both regions can work out both kinds and the edge's hash alone, so they meet without reading each other (X3), and a settlement's ring of streets joins the paths that reach it. | 10 §8.3. Tested in `crates/clash-gen/tests/settlements.rs` (`edges_agree`, an integration test until test scripts have a world). |
| D-673 | A region plan (its kind, paths and its kind's sites) and a settlement plan (streets, blocks, lots, sites) are pure functions of the realm's seed, the position and the definitions, which the `standard` generator works out on first need and keeps (`StandardGenerator::region_plan`, `settlement_plan`), forgetting all past 256 of either. A realiser takes every lot, site and path within `GATHER_MARGIN` of its tract from the plans of the settlements and regions near it; a survey writes the same into the region's record. | Design, from building surveys | D-511 asked for every placement within 12 cells of a tract "by position alone or from a plan the generator keeps". A settlement's lots depend on its whole layout (which row a must-have site takes), so a tract cannot work them out from its own position, and laying a town out again for each of its tracts would cost a survey's work per tract. Keeping a pure value changes no result. | 10 §6.2 (the seam), §8.2; invariants X1, X2. The plans are kept in a `Mutex`, filled outside it; two threads that race make the same plan. |
| D-674 | A region kind's sites are placed after its paths (10 §8.2 steps 5 before 4) so that `near: road` can be honoured. A site is tried in the region's tracts in an order drawn from their hashes, takes one tract, never one in or beside a settlement or crossed by a path, keeps `apart` tracts from its entry's other sites by Chebyshev distance, stands in the middle of its tract turned a quarter-turn the hash chooses, and is dropped where it meets a placement of the tract's definition or the realm. Fewer than drawn is a shortfall in `SurveyReport`. `near` is `road`, `centre` or `edge`; `coast` and `river` fail to load. | Design, from building surveys | 10 §8.1 named counts and constraints without rules; one tract holds one site at most (02 §8.4), and placements written by a survey may not overlap (X10). | 10 §8.1, §8.2, §13. Site counts and spacing are tested over many seeds. |
| D-675 | A settlement is laid out by rows: streets east to west cross its whole extent; between two of them a row of blocks is cut by streets north to south. `grid` cuts every row alike at even spacing; `organic` draws a spacing for each row and moves every inner street by up to a fifth of it, keeping blocks at least four cells. The middle street of each direction is a main road (`main` 0 to 2, `main_path`); with `organic` the main road north to south runs straight. A block has a row of lots on its north side fronting north and one on its south side fronting south when it is deep enough for its zone's shallowest map twice, else one fronting north; its zone is the first whose `to` holds its centre's distance as a share of the way to the edge. Along a row a map of the zone that fits is drawn by hash, turned so its `front` faces the street, its front edge on the street, `gap` cells apart. A tract definition places nothing inside a settlement. | Design, from building settlements | 10 §8.4 named patterns, spacing, zones and lots and gave no rules. Rows of blocks with every street meeting one that spans the whole width join every street by construction, for both patterns, and a map that fronts its street has its door on the pavement. | 10 §8.4. `radial`, `linear` and `scatter` fail to load. Every lot fits and fronts a street, every entrance is reachable on foot from every other, and the streets meet across a region's edge: tested. |
| D-676 | A `must_have` site takes the start of a row: nearest the centre for `centre`, farthest for `edge`, by hash for `anywhere`; its map is one of the site's that fits, and is placed only where none of the tracts it covers is taken by an earlier site, where it lies in one region, and where it meets nothing the realm places. The region its box begins in makes it (`SiteSpec` with the site's name and tags, its tracts, the anchor where its map's anchor goes). A site that cannot be placed is reported in `SurveyReport::missing` and the settlement is laid out without it. A placement's `site` is left unset. | Design, from building settlements | A tract holds one site (02 §8.4) and a survey's sites lie inside its region (10 §8.2); the world gives a site its id only after the survey, when it makes it, so the survey cannot name it on the placement. | 10 §8.4, §11.1, §13 (`must_have`). |
| D-677 | The `site` definition type is built for what generation needs: `map` (an id or a tag; a map stands for a site only if it names a `front` and supplies every role as a mark that is the same in every placement, X7), `roles`, `staff`, `name`, `tags` and `founded`; `owner`, `stocks`, `supplies`, `incidents`, `building` and `assembly` are carried for WG9 and WG10. | Design, from building settlements | Region kinds and settlements name sites, and WG9, which builds the type in full, comes after them. Checking roles now keeps X7 true from the first site. | 10 §11.1; plans/worldgen.md WG9. |
| D-678 | People: a zone's `people` and a site's `staff` are entries `{ creature, count, chance, in, at, role }`. The realiser adds them to the placed map's spawns, with how many and whether drawn from the placement's seed, in the areas named, at the mark's first cell, or anywhere in the map; each becomes a spawn request whose `what` is the id as written, handed over by the tract its cell is in, which the simulation answers when it names a template. `role` is carried. | Design, from building settlements | The simulation already answers a request that names a template (06 §10.4), and a map's own spawns already become requests at open floor; a zone saying who lives in its houses needed no new mechanism, only a way to add to them. | 10 §11.2; 06 §12.2. Homes, presence and agents are WG9's. |
| D-679 | The bundled town: `core:town` is two regions of the region kind `town_country` (pasture and copses, farmsteads by the roads, roads across the edge), the market town `town` (a grid with main roads, three zones, a square and a town hall) across the edge between them, and the organic `village`; five maps (`town_house`, `terrace`, `shop_flat`, `office`, `square`), the area kind `office` and the start `wold_market`. A settlement's `wall` fails to load. `cargo run -p clash-gen --example tract_ascii -- town 1 62 30 0 3 2` prints its centre; `tests/golden/town.txt` holds two storeys of a block across the edge, which must stand. | Design, from building settlements | The beta needs a generated town to walk in. Walls round a settlement wait for assemblies (WG10). The start's id sorts after the others so that the new-game list keeps its first two starts where they were. | 10 §8.4, §12, §13; plans/beta.md. The golden frame `new_game` of `clash-app` gains the start's line. |
| D-650 | Life gates acting: every start of an action, by a command (`Act` with `start` or `enqueue`), a rule (`start_action`) or a plan step, is checked against `Sim::life_lets_act`, which is `Sim::can_act` for anything that has a mind or a body, after the actor is found in the world and before its parameters; an entity with neither is not life's to stop and acts as the runtime alone allows. The runtime answers life's events once their handlers have run, as movement does: `slots_lost` with `check_slots`; `entity_died` by abandoning the entity's actions and dropping its wait and plan; `entity_unconscious` by interrupting them with the reason `entity_unconscious` and dropping its wait and plan; neither asks anyone. A free decision is not requested, nor `decision_needed` raised, for an entity whose mind has stopped and that nobody drives; at `entity_woke` and `entity_revived` it is free as one that has just started. A driven entity is not stopped by its own mind. | Design, from joining actions and life | 06 §8.4 made `can_act` what a command is checked against and left ending actions at death to the runtime, and D-559 left both undone. Abandoning asks nobody, which is what a dead entity needs; interrupting runs `on_interrupt`, which is what being knocked out is, and leaving the player to be asked while unconscious would ask a question nobody can answer. Kernel and test entities with no mind and no body, and devices that rules move, act today and must go on doing so (03 §6.2 gives an entity with no body one slot of every channel so that it can); a body with no mind and no driver does not (E7). | 03 §4.4, §5.3, §6.6; 06 §8.4. `Sim::life_lets_act`, `action::run::on_event`. `crates/clash-sim/tests/acting_alive.rs`. |
| D-651 | The `Timer` wake-up domain is shared: the kernel hands every timer to `life::handle_wake` first, and only one that is neither a pool's nor a body's goes to the domain's handler, which by default reports it as unhandled and which a package or a test replaces with `Sim::set_wake_handler`. | Design, from joining actions and life | D-551 asked a package with timers of its own to call `life::handle_wake` first; a handler registered in its place that forgot would have stopped every pool and wound in the simulation, silently. Dispatching in the kernel makes the order hold whoever registers. | 03 §4.3. `Sim::dispatch` in `advance.rs`; `life::timer_wake`. |
| D-652 | The application runs on the simulation's own commands and decisions. `host::command_for` turns the play screen's commands into `Act` (with `start`, the action by qualified id, a bare name meaning the first of that name in order of qualified id, and the parameters as the scene assembled them), `Wait`, `Cancel`, `ClearPlan` and `Choose`, and refuses `react`, `pass` and rules with a reason; a refusal is the simulation's reason. The host's pending decision is the simulation's front request, free or a choice. Each pass advances the simulation while the player waits on it (no decision asked, and busy or unconscious, not free, dead or away) with `advance(Work(256))` until a decision, nothing left, the frame's slice or an hour of simulation time; it drains everything the pass's commands and advances reported and hands it to the scene as one batch, with a line in the log at once for a fault and for a `message` to the player. The stand-ins `shim` and `present` are deleted. `Game::submit` hands a command to the game for tools and tests. | Design, from joining the application to the action runtime and playback | The stand-in answered every free decision with a zero wait and moved the character by debug commands; the runtime now asks and answers as 03 §5 says, and `UntilDecision` in one call could hold a frame for as long as a long wait takes. An hour bounds a pass in which nothing ever asks the player (everyone else busy for ever). A fault is not a happening anyone perceives, and nothing else shows it; messages reach the log by the application until playback can carry them. | 12 §3.2, §3.3. `clash_app::host`, `game::Game::pass`, `SIM_WORK`, `PASS_LIMIT`, `Session::waits_on_simulation`. Supersedes the stand-ins of D-593 and the `present_events` of D-597. |
| D-653 | The creation session is the application's (`clash_app::creation`): `app.creation` is null while none is in progress, else `CreationSession::to_value` with `template`, `name`, `seed` and `finished`. The service `creation` has the ops `begin { template }`, `select { key, value }`, `clear { key }`, `name { value }`, `seed { value }` and `finish`; `finish` refuses with the reason when the character cannot be made and otherwise keeps it; any change drops a finished character; `name` and `seed` are kept without a session; `name` also selects the template's text choice that sets the name. `new_game` makes the finished character, or else the start's `template` with its defaults and the name given, with the seed given for the world and the spec; then the session and the finished character are dropped. `edit_creation` stays as the older form of `name` and `seed`, and the `creation_<field>` variables are filled from the name and seed whether or not a session is in progress. | Design, from joining the application to templates | D-591's map of `name` and `seed` stood in until the creation session existed. The new-game screen must keep working, with the command line's seed, before a creation screen exists; a refusal of `finish` must reach the screen, which a failed service does through the role `error`. | 12 §3.4, §10.4, §10.5; 06 §10.4. Supersedes the `app.creation` of D-591. `AppService::Creation` in `clash-scene`. |
| D-654 | The player's character is spawned from a character spec by the debug command `spawn`, so that it is in the input log; it is drawn with its templates' glyph, or else the start's. A `start` gains `template`, a qualified id or a bare name in its namespace, checked at load where templates are loaded, and its `glyph` becomes optional. The bundled starts name `core:person` and no glyph. | Design, from joining the application to templates | D-592's bare creature had no body, mind or name of its own and could not act through life. A start is the stand-in for a game scenario, which names its pre-made characters (13 §3). | 12 §3.4. `clash_app::session::spawn_player`, `clash_scene::StartDef::template_id`. Supersedes D-592. |
| D-655 | An event that no show gives changes for looks at each thing it is about where the thing is drawn: its look is taken from the host, and it does not move. Only an event that says so moves a sprite, and the last change a batch makes to a sprite still leaves it where the party perceives it. | Design, from joining playback to the action runtime | With the action runtime, a step's batch begins with `action_started` and `phase_entered` about the actor, which have no show; looked at again as of the end of the batch they put the actor in its new cell before the step was played, and the step then drew no glide. In a walk of many steps the actor jumped to the end and glided back. | 12 §7.3. `clash_scene::playback`, the op `Look`. Refines D-603. |
| D-656 | (Superseded by D-700: perception exists.) Until perception exists the application's stand-in answers playback's host: after each pass that moved the simulation it works out the party's sight (field of view, light, occupants) and writes it into a map memory; an event was seen if a place it is about, not counting `actor` and `instigator`, is in sight or seen in the picture; nothing is only heard; `seen_cell` and `seen_sprite` answer from sight, a thing ever perceived and not perceived now as known and unseen. Once playback has caught up the view model is given what sight says that no event did, and a debug build compares it with a fresh model of memory and sight, writing a drift to the log as a fault. Leaving the omniscient view forgets the memory with the picture. `as_of` answers as of the present. | Design, from joining the application to playback | Events change only the cells and things they are about; walking changes what is in view without an event saying so, and showing it before the step is played would draw the step ahead of its glide. The check needs a fresh model built the same way. | 12 §3.2, §5, §7.5. `clash_app::perception_shim::{look, show, fresh, perceived, seen_cell, seen_sprite}`. When perception lands only that module changes. Supersedes the feeding of D-594. |
| D-680 | A query is compiled by the data layer where it is written: its kind, its arguments as expression fields of the declared types, `where` (a condition) and `sort` (pure) with the candidate bound as the subject `it` after everything the field binds, and `limit` once in the field's scope. The data layer answers `list` and `defs` and applies `where`, `sort` (numbers, then texts, then the rest; stable) and `limit`, in that order, for every kind. The simulation registers its kinds as `QuerySpec`s (arguments, result type, shapes that exclude each other, arguments that go together, whether safe for belief, how to work out the extent of constant arguments) in the expression registry and gives each kind's candidates in its default order through `Host::query`. The value sources give `count` an int, `collect` a list, `first` and `pick` a result or null, `sum` a number (an int if all are) and `min` and `max` a number or null; `weight` and `of` read `it`. | Design, from building K6 | The expression crate cannot name the world and the simulation cannot run the machine on its own fields; splitting at the candidates lets one implementation filter, sort and cut every kind, keeps the order of results one rule, and lets the reference and the compiler know every kind and argument. | 05 §7.4, §11, §12. `clash_expr::{QuerySpec, QueryKind, QueryCall, Host::query, Fault::Query}` (`X910`); `clash_data::{compile_query, CompiledQuery, Evaluator::query}`; codes `E0677`, `E0678`, `W0690`; `rule::Items::Compiled`. |
| D-681 | The simulation's kinds are `entities` (one shape of `around` with `radius`, `box`, `in_area`, `in_site`, `at`, or none for every entity in the world; filters `plane`, `kind`, `tag`, `def`, `has`), `cells` (`around` with `radius`, `box`, `in_area`, `at`), `contents` (`of`, `deep`), `areas` and `sites` (`at`, or `around` with `radius`, or neither). Results with a centre come nearest first, an entity by the nearest cell it fills; other spatial results by cell, each entity where the shape first covers it; ties and the rest by id. `list` is `{ "list": expr }` or `{ "list": { "items": expr, … } }`; `defs` takes a constant `type`. | Design, from building K6 | 05 §11 named shapes without arguments or types, and nothing selected by tag, definition or component, which the town's own rules need; an entity that fills several cells needs one distance. `line`, `cone`, `actions`, `windows` and `parts` have nothing behind them yet. | 05 §11; `clash_sim::query`. A property test holds `entities` and `cells` to a brute-force reference. |
| D-682 | `rules.max_query_cells`, 1,048,576 by default, caps the cells a spatial query covers: a radius the box round its centre, a storey three metres; a box its cells; an area its bounds; a site its tracts on one storey; `entities` with no shape the entities in the world. A constant radius over the default is a load error; anything else over the setting is refused when it runs, with the warning `X910`, and has no results. | Design, from building K6 | A cap measured against a setting cannot be checked at load, so the load check uses the default, which a mod that raises the setting has to live with; the cells a radius scans are what it costs. | 05 §11, §13.2, §17. |
| D-683 | No query of the simulation is safe for belief until the perception package answers it from belief: writing one in a mind context is the load error `E0662`, naming the seam (`QuerySpec::safe`, and `QueryCall::observer`, the own subject of the context, which the host must answer from). `list` and `defs` read nothing of the world and are allowed anywhere. | Design, from building K6 before perception landed | 01 R11: an unanswered belief query would read truth. | 05 §7.3, §11. |
| D-684 | A timer is plain state in the rule state: its owner, name, interval and runs left, `while_unloaded`, what it runs (a continuation, or a recurring rule by qualified id), and its wake-up (domain `Rule`, `id` the timer's number) or, while its owner is away, the time it had left and the clock and local time it left on. A continuation is where the effect is written (definition type, qualified id, JSON path), the rule and its owner, the subjects, locals and event fields bound there, and the cause, found again when the timer fires. A timer of an entity is that entity's wake-up, so it follows its clocks. The next run is settled before the effects run. A timer's owner is what `owner` says, else the rule's owner, else `self`, else the realm of `here`, else the simulation; one of the same name and owner is replaced. `cancel_timer` takes a name, or `{ name, owner }`. `owner.timer(name)` reads the time left. The save layout is version 9 (8 with perception before it). | Design, from building K7 | 05 §13.4 said what a continuation holds and not how it is found again; a compiled tree cannot be saved, a place in the definitions can. Settling the next run first is what lets an `every` cancel itself. | 05 §10.4, §13.4, §17; 03 §9. `rule::{TimerRecord, Continuation, TimerSet, Timers}`, `RuleHost::{set_timer, cancel_timer, recurring}`, `RuleSet::resume_timer`, the reasons `rule.timer_refused` and `rule.timer_lost`. |
| D-685 | An entity's timers leave the timeline when it leaves the world and return with it, by `while_unloaded`: `pause` keeps the time left; `elapse` takes off the local time that passed on the clock it left (universal time if that clock is gone) and fires once at once if overdue; `all` fires once for each interval missed, at most 100; `skip` runs nothing missed; `once` as `elapse`. What fires at once fires in the order it fell due. Timers of other owners never leave the simulation. | Design, from building K7 | 03 §9 named `Pause` and `Elapse` and not how much time passed for something that was nowhere; the clock it left is where its time was being kept. | 03 §9; 05 §13.5. `Sim::timers_follow`, called where the kernel puts an entity on the clock that now applies. |
| D-686 | A recurring rule is a timer whose body is the rule, run as a call with the owner as `self` if attached. A global one, owned by the simulation, starts when the simulation is set up (and after a load if new); an attached one starts on `attach_rule` and stops on `detach_rule`. Its first run comes `1 + h mod I` microseconds after it starts, `h` the positional hash of the owner's handle and the rule's qualified id; then every interval. `skip`, `once` and `all` are D-685's. | Design, from building K7 | Staggering by a hash of who and which, never by call order, keeps a thousand owners from one tick and keeps the same world firing the same way. | 05 §9.4. The rules carried by definitions wait for a definition type of the simulation that carries `rules`. |
| D-687 | The console reaches the rule runtime: a verb of the engine is called directly with its arguments by name (`Sim::call_verb`); a rule used as a verb, `do` and `run` are compiled as one effect against the loaded definitions (`clash_data::expr::compile_loose`, `Sim::compile_effects`), binding `self`, `here` and `global`, and run through `Sim::run_effects`. What they change is not in the input log. A cell's field level is the property `loc.field(kind)`, so that a query can ask which cells hold fire. | Design, from binding the console to rules (K10 seam) | The console's language carries values the console resolved, which cannot be written in an effect's JSON: they are passed as locals.
