A `Debug` command that carried an effect would need the loader at replay. 02 §23 left `loc.field` unbound until fields had a definition type; they have one. | 14 §3.3; 02 §23; 05 §11. `clash_save::host::GameHost::run_verb`. Effects typed at the console cannot set timers or keep choices: their origins are not among the definitions. |
| D-688 | `mods/core` has rules of town life in the group `core:town_life`, on unless the setting `core:town_life` is false: `lamp_keeper` (attached to every entity made with the tag `street_lamp`, every ten minutes, sets its `core:lit` by the time of day where it stands), `fire_alarm` (on `field_appeared` of fire, rings each entity tagged `fire_alarm` within 12 m that stands in the same areas, raises `core:fire_alarm_rang` and silences it two minutes after the last fire by a named `after`), `rest_when_tired` (attached to every `person`, every half-hour) and the function `people_near`. The world's test scripts were blessed again: the rule state now hashes its timers. | Design, from writing the content of the stream "queries and timers" | Rules a modder would write show what queries and timers are for; attaching on `entity_created` by tag keeps them off everything else, so no other script's state changes. Lamps keep a variable: switching a lamp fixture waits for a world verb that sets an element's state (K8). | 05 §9.4, §10.4, §11; `mods/core/data/rules/town.json`; `scenarios/rules/`. |
| D-630 | An entity has a trait once for each giver (a rule, an item, a posture, an ability, a template, other), held as instances in a component of its own, `Traits`, the sixteenth component type. `stacking` applies to an instance given again by the same giver: `none` changes nothing and raises nothing, `refresh` starts the duration again, `stack` adds a stack up to `max` (default and cap 1,000) and starts it again. A trait of a `group` removes the group's other traits, from any giver. Modifiers apply once for each stack and rates are multiplied by the stacks; tags, rules and channels are not counted. `remove_trait` removes every giver's instance, or a number of stacks taken in the order given. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §5 named stacking modes and groups and said nothing of where a trait comes from. A sword's blessing and a potion's must not end each other, and an item let go must take back only what it gave. 08 §3.3 needs the same for abilities. | 06 §5, §13; package E3. `traits::Giver`, `Traits`, `add_trait`, `remove_trait`, `withdraw`. The simulation's save layout is version 9. |
| D-631 | What a trait confers is applied when it is given and taken back exactly when it goes: modifiers and pool rates under the stats source `(trait, instance key)`, a tag recorded only where the entity did not carry it of its own, a rule recorded only where it was not attached already (`RuleState::attach`). A tag or rule two instances confer stays until neither does, and a tag an item and a trait both confer stays while either does (`item::confer` asks `traits::confers_tag`). A trait's attached rules are detached when it ends, before `trait_removed` is delivered, so 06 §5's example answers its own ending with `on_remove`. | Design, from building the beta | Removing exactly what was added (E3) needs a record of what was added. A rule attached by the trait would otherwise never hear its own removal, or hear every other trait's. | 06 §5. `traits::conferred` works it out from scratch; a property test compares it with the stats, tags and rules after random giving, removing, stacking, withdrawing, expiring and leaving the world. |
| D-632 | A trait's duration is a wake-up of the domain `Timer` scheduled through `Sim::schedule_for`, so it follows its holder from clock to clock; `id` is the holder's entity bits and `arg` has bit 61 set, bits 62 and 63 clear, and the instance's key below. Traits register the domain's handler, which the kernel calls for what is not a pool's or a body's (D-651), and which hands anything not a trait's to `life::timer_wake` to be reported as unhandled. `Sim::relocate` calls `traits::suspend` before an entity leaves the world (with everything inside it) and `traits::resume` once it is back. A new field `while_unloaded` says `pause` (default) or `elapse`; an elapsing duration has the local time that passed on the clock it left (universal time if that clock is gone) taken off, and those that ran out end at once, the first to run out first. | Design, from building the beta | 03 §9 gives timers `Pause` and `Elapse` and leaves taking them off the timeline to whoever stores an entity; `relocate` is the one way an entity leaves the world. Which one fits is the content's to say, as 05 §10.4 lets a rule's timer say. | 06 §5; 03 §9. Pools and wounds keep their own wake-ups and return as they stood (D-444, D-526, D-552); a trait's `elapse` is the content's choice. |
| D-633 | The events of traits are `trait_added` (with `stacks`, `first`, `duration`, `giver`, `look`), `trait_removed` (with `stacks`, `left`, `how`: `removed`, `expired`, `replaced` or `withdrawn`) and `trait_expired`, which precedes `trait_removed`. The hook `trait_adding` may cancel and may change `duration`; `trait_removing` may cancel and is raised by `remove_trait` alone. The verbs are `add_trait { to, trait, duration? }` and `remove_trait { from, trait, stacks? }`, each returning a count of stacks. The properties are `has_trait(trait)`, `traits`, `trait_stacks(trait)` (for 06's `trait(id).stacks`), `trait_active(trait)` and `trait_left(trait)`; none is safe for belief. | Design, from building the beta | 06 named the events, hooks and verbs without fields or arguments, and `trait(id).stacks` would need a subject kind for every trait. A resistance that shortens a stun is a handler that changes `duration`; letting expiry or a dropped item be cancelled would let a hook keep time from passing. | 06 §5, §13. `TRAIT_EVENTS`, `hook_types`, `traits::verbs`, `traits::properties`, `TRAIT_REASONS`. |
| D-634 | The `traits` an item and a posture list are given to the holder with the item or the posture as their giver while it is held, worn or taken, and withdrawn when it is let go, comes off or is left. A trait is named bare or qualified; a bare name is looked up in sorted namespace order. A name no trait answers to is a load error at the item or the posture. | Design, from building the beta | Both packages carried the field for traits to give. A misspelt trait on a sword would otherwise do nothing in silence. | 06 §9.1, §11.1. `item::confer` and `movement::set_posture` call `traits::give_named`, `withdraw` and `posture_changed`. |
| D-635 | Left out of traits for now: what `grants`, `abilities`, `senses`, `bound`, `goals`, `weights` and `seat` give (read, checked and carried); the slots `channels` adds or disables, which `traits::channel_change` gives the action runtime; keeping `while` current (worked out when a trait of the holder changes and by `traits::reconsider`); and notability (06 §12.2). `on_apply` and `on_remove` run against the simulation (`Sim::run_effects`), and what a trait changes of the stats is raised as `attr_changed` and `pool_crossed`. | Design, from building the beta | Each waits for a package not built: abilities, perception, minds' goals, reactive expressions, the action runtime's use of slots. | Plan E3 is `beta`. |
| D-636 | The edits of 06 §6.5 are functions of the body model in `body::edit`, over a `BodyCx` as the rest of `body` is, each checking everything before it changes anything and ending in the bookkeeping every body function shares, so that functions, slots, mass and size always equal a recomputation; their refusals are an `EditError` with the codes `unknown_part`, `no_such_socket`, `socket_refuses`, `no_free_socket`, `part_name_taken`, `too_many_parts`, `no_such_layer`, `unknown_material` and `not_a_part`. A part on another takes a named socket, or the first by name that would take it; names of parts that are there are not repeated, and a new part of a gone one's name drops the gone listing; `replace_part` keeps what still fits its sockets and drops the rest; `graft_part` takes a `Detached` and gives each part a new key, keeping names, layers, damage and size but not wounds; `damage_part` stops at 254 255ths. | Design, from building the beta | 06 §6.5 named the verbs with a phrase each and its refusals with one line. A limb that grows back must be that limb again for rules and wear to find it, and a damage verb that could take a part away would make destruction something other than a hit. | 06 §6.5; package E6. A property test applies random sequences of every edit and checks B2, B3, the tree, sockets and names, and that a refused edit changes nothing. |
| D-637 | The simulation's side of editing is `crate::reshape`, over the kernel's own body wiring (D-550): each edit raises the hook `body_changing` (`verb`, `part`), runs over `Sim::with_body`, raises the events of what changed with `Sim::raise_body_changes` (masses, stats, vital state follow), reports what came off as `part_detached` and leaves what its definition's `leaves` says, lets go what parts can no longer grasp and what was worn only over parts that are gone, and raises `body_changed` (`verb`, `part`, `parts`). Its verbs are `add_part`, `replace_part`, `graft_part`, `set_layer`, `scale_part`, `scale_body`, `set_body_plan` and `damage_part`; as D-550 has it, a part is a subject and a verb that names one takes no `who`, and the part arguments may be null so that `self.part(name)` is written as it is, a null being refused when the verb runs. `heal_part`, `disable_part`, `restore_part` and `remove_part` stay the body package's verbs, which raise the events of 06 §7.6 and not `body_changing`. | Design, from building the beta | The kernel's wiring of bodies landed beside this package: one way to change a body and raise what follows, and one convention for naming parts. | 06 §6.5, §13. `reshape::remove_part` is the edit that also drops what was gone beneath the part. |
| D-638 | `set_body_plan` maps a part of the new plan to one the body has by name and definition together, keeping its key, layers, damage, wounds and state; everything else of the plan is new, what is not mapped comes off in pieces, and what was gone is forgotten. `remove_part` (and a part coming off by `replace_part`) drops the listings of what was gone already beneath it, which `body::detach` with `Detach::Removed` leaves behind hanging from nothing. | Design, from building the beta | Mapping by name alone would put an arm's layers on a tentacle. The property test found the listings left by `detach`. | 06 §6.5. `edit::remove_part`. Whoever owns `body::take_off` may fold the same into `detach`. |
| D-639 | Left out of body editing for now: a change of footprint with a change of plan; bodies made of cells (06 §6.6); shared parts; and a `.scn` script for `reshaping_touch`, which is an integration test in `crates/clash-sim/tests/reshape.rs` that calls the fixture rule `reshapefix:apply_form` against the simulation. | Design, from building the beta | Each waits for a package not built: subgrids, shared parts, a console command that calls a rule on an entity. | Plan E6 is `beta`. |
| D-700 | The application shows what the simulation's perception says the party perceives (`clash_app::perception`). As a batch ends it takes the frames (`drain_frames`) and works out what is in view: the cells of the party's view at the members' last observations (`party_view`), each with what is in it now (elements, strongest field, most marked decal, open sky, light by the sky and fixed sources, as map memory records them); the party's own members, always; and whatever stands in a cell in view and gives off something light could show, drawn with its templates' or its item definition's glyph, else the theme's suspected mark. `perceived` is `Sim::party_perceived`; `seen_cell` and `seen_sprite` answer from what is in view. Once playback has caught up, each cell a frame recorded or the view left is given as the member's map memory holds it and remembered, each cell in view as it is now, and who is in view; the debug check's fresh model is the map memory (`snapshot`) and what is in view, made the same way, and the model is built so afresh on a game begun or loaded and when frames were dropped. What is due at the present tick is run (`advance(Until(now))`) before the player is asked, so that a sense sweeps as soon as its owner has moved or been put in the world. Heard markers come from tags through playback; the frames' heard lists are not shown again. | Design, from joining the application to perception | Map memory is written only at sweeps, every half second or when the observer moves, and an interaction's effects run as its action ends, after the last sweep: drawn from memory alone, a door seen opening would stay shut until the player moved. Cells in view are seen now, so they are drawn as they are; every other cell must be the simulation's own memory, or a game loaded from a save would draw differently from the one saved (01 R7). A wake-up due now is what the simulation runs before asking anyway; without running it, the character would not look until the player had acted. | 12 §3.2, §3.5, §7.5; 15 §13. `clash_app::perception::{look, show, fresh, perceived, seen_cell, seen_sprite}`, `Session::due_now`. Supersedes D-656 and the feeding of D-594; `perception_shim` is deleted. |
| D-701 | A step refused because the way is closed is a bump: the host notes it when the refusal's code is `move.closed`, or when the step's `requires` refused and movement says the step would meet something closed (`believed_step`, `judge`), and the loop asks `interaction::bump_default` what is there. By the preference whose bare name is `bump` (`at_once`, the default; `ask`; `never`) it runs the control named `bump` or `bump_ask` with the offer as `it` (a map: `target`, `interaction`, `id`, `name`, `default`, `available`, `why`, `where`), or writes the step's refusal. Where nothing can be done by default and a default would be, the refusal of each such default is written instead, joined. | Design, from offering interactions in play | 16 §3.1 has the default used when a thing is bumped into, and walking into a shut door must open it without a menu. What is done is content: the `ui` mod's controls start the `interact` action, and a mod may make them ask, play a sound first or do nothing. A step action's refusal carries its `fail_text`, not movement's code. A locked door's two defaults (`open`, `unlock`) both cannot be done, and only the two reasons together say why. | 12 §3.7; 16 §3.1. `clash_app::interact`, `Game::bump`, `host::command_for`, `Session::bumped`; `mods/ui/data/interact.json`. |
| D-702 | What a character could do within a step is the property `entity.offers_near` (`list<map<any>>`), read of the character itself in a mind context: one row for each interaction a thing within a step offers, with `target`, `interaction`, `id`, `name`, `default`, `available`, `why` and `where`. The `ui` mod's control `interact`, on `e` in play, opens `ui:interact`, which lists it, greys the rows that cannot be done with their reason, and starts the action `interact` with the row's `target` and `interaction`. The scene's fake registry carries the property for tests over the scene. | Design, from offering interactions in play | A screen's rows are an expression, and nothing a screen could read listed what is offered (16 §3.5.1 left `offers_near` reachable only from Rust). A property of the character itself is what a mind context may read in full, and it needs no new kind of host answer. A query (05 §11) would serve as well once queries exist. | 16 §3.5.1, §14; 12 §3.7, §10.7. `clash_sim::interaction::properties`, `offers_near_value`. An additive change to `clash-sim`. |
| D-703 | Saves are files of the user's data directory's `saves/`, one for each (`clash_app::saves::FileSaves` over `clash_save::save_game`, `load_game`, `read_meta`, `list_saves`, `load_warnings`). A save's id is its name's letters and digits in lower case with `-` between, `save` for a name of nothing; saving under an id that is there replaces it. The application writes a summary (who, the start's name, how long the game has run) and the seed into the metadata, and as its own bytes a JSON object of the start, the character's name and the character. `app.saves` lists every save newest first by when its file was written: `name`, `description` (summary and UTC time), `state` `enabled`, `changed` (other mods) or `broken`. The pause menu's Save opens `ui:save_game`; the title offers Continue (the newest) and Load (`ui:load`, which moves to `saving.json`). A load's warnings go to the log and to the screen of the new role `notice` (`ui:notice`, `title`, `text`). A load closes every screen, empties playback and builds the view model afresh, and changes nothing hashed. | Design, from joining the application to the save crate | 13 §9 and 01 §11.2 said what a save holds and not where the application keeps it or what a load screen shows. One file a save is what `clash-save` writes; an id from the name keeps files readable and a path from going elsewhere. Time is the file's, which the application may read (01 R5 binds the simulation). A save made with other mods must be told before it is chosen, and what differs once it is loaded, and an error screen is wrong for what is not a failure. | 12 §3.1, §3.6, §10.4, §10.7; 01 §11.2. Supersedes the backend of D-597. `clash_app::saves`, `app::App::service`, `Game::show_warnings`; `mods/ui/data/saving.json`; the bundled profiles name `notice`. |
| D-704 | (The start superseded by D-750: a scenario names its policy in `saving.policy`.) A `start` names its game's saving policy in `saving` (`free`, `single`, `checkpoints` or `none`; left out, `free`; anything else a load error). `app.can_save` holds only under `free` while a game is played; loading into a game being played is allowed only under `free`, and from the title always; `single` saves the game under the character's name (else the start's) as its play screen is left; a rule's checkpoint is saved under its name under every policy but `none`. | Design, from building saving | 13 §9.2 gives the policies to a scenario, which is not built; the start is its stand-in (D-591). Saving at intervals, restoring checkpoints, endings and `on_party_gone` need the scenario packages (SC1 to SC3, SC7). | 13 §9.2; 12 §3.4, §3.6. `StartDef::saving`, `saving_policy`, `SAVING_POLICIES`; `Session::saving`, `Game::save_on_leaving`. An additive change to `clash-scene`. |
| D-705 | (Superseded by D-751: `BeginScenario` gives them.) The player's character is given senses when it is made, before it is put in the world, if its templates gave it none: those its start names in `senses` (qualified ids or bare names in the start's namespace, checked at load where senses are), or every `sense` the loaded mods define in order of qualified id, through `attach_sense`. This is done outside the input log. | Design, from joining the application to perception | 15 §15 puts `senses` on templates, which do not carry them yet, and a party is the directly controlled characters with senses: without them the player would see nothing. The application names no content, so the default is what the mods define and a start can narrow it. A command for senses would change the kernel's commands; until templates carry senses, a game replayed from its first command lacks them, and replays between two saves are unaffected. | 12 §3.4, §3.5; 15 §15. `clash_app::perception::give_senses`, `StartDef::senses`. Moves to the `spawn` of templates when they carry `senses`. |
| D-706 | The omniscient view also sets the simulation's switch `perception.omniscient`, so that playback is told every event was seen; switching it off clears it and builds the view model afresh from the party's map memory and what is in view, which hold only what was really seen. | Design, from joining the application to perception | 15 §13 gives the switch to a debug profile; D-594 forgot the memory with the picture because the stand-in's memory was filled from what was shown, and the simulation's map memory never is. | 12 §3.2; 15 §13. Supersedes the leaving of D-594. |
| D-710 | Windows as built: plain state in the `WindowTable` (save layout 12, after abilities' 11), each with its kind, source, action and closing phase, those it names, when it opened, a deadline (the end of the opening phase where it leads straight to the closing one, or the end of a window's length, else unknown) and its candidates with why each was offered and where it is (`never`, `pending`, `late`, `noticed`, `asked`, `reacted`, `passed`). Candidates are those named, then by id those within `reach` metres, middle of cell to middle of cell, of the source or someone named who intervene for one of them, then by id the directly controlled within that reach; never the source. Until relations exist the `intervenes` relation is answered by `Sim::set_intervenes`, not state. A window closes as its closing phase is entered, before the phase does anything, or as its action ends by any path, or at the end of its length; closing cancels the delays still running and withdraws a `Reaction` decision being asked. A window is a value of the new kind `window`; `window_opened` is unchanged, and `window_noticed` and `window_closed` carry the window. `open_window` and `close_window` are verbs; `window_open`, `window_source`, `window_kind_of`, `window_time_left` and `away_from` are functions. | Design, from building windows (T5) | 03 §7.1 gave a `Window` struct with a wake-up that closes it, but a phase's end is one wake-up of the action already; a deadline that is a tick is what a prompt and an option's timing need. Reach from where to where was not said. The `window` kind was named by the action's `$window` and by the window kind's scope and registered by no one, so a reaction could not load in the real registry. Re-signing `window_opened` would break its matches; a new event with the window costs nothing. | 03 §7.1, §7.2, §13; 15 §6, §9.1. `clash_sim::action::windows`. The `bodyguard` row is a Rust test with the relation supplied there. |
| D-711 | Reacting as built: nothing asks or answers by itself; rules on `window_noticed` call `react` or `ask_reaction`, and the AI layer answers the same event. `react` checks the window open, the reactor a candidate that noticed, the kind listed, `on_behalf` where the window names someone else, then everything a start checks with `$window` bound before the defaults; actions in phases that allow `cancel` make room as for any start, and busy slots held by one that does not are `engine:reaction_cannot_cancel`. The reactor turns to face the window's source. `ask_reaction` asks a directly controlled candidate with a `Reaction { window, deadline, options }` decision, each option `{ action, label, in_time, refused }` in order of definition, `in_time` from the phases' durations along their default path to `ready`; the `React` and `Pass` commands answer, the names the scene already submits. | Design, from building reactions (T5) | D-035 and D-048 make prompts rules, and the T5 "Done when" asks that nothing happen with no rule listening. A reaction is an ordinary action (03 §7.3), so its refusals are a start's. The scene's prompt shows each option's timing and must be able to say why one is refused. A parry's arc is measured from the facing, and a reactor that did not turn to meet the blow it answers would parry the air. | 03 §5.2, §7.3, §13; 12 §9.5. `clash_sim::action::react`, `Command::React`, `Command::Pass`, `DecisionKind::Reaction`, `ReactionOption`. The application maps the decision to `PendingDecision::Reaction` and the two commands back. |
| D-712 | Defences as built: the `defence` block (`during`, `form`, `with` as one or a list of which the first that answers is used, `protects` with the defender beside whom it protects, `covers` with `arc` about the facing and `parts` by name, id or tag, `handles` counted per phase in force and kept in the window table, `contest`, `deflect`, `holds`, `brace`, `evasion`). At step 5 those in force for the target are tried, others' first, then by when their phase began, entity and action; each tried raises `strike_defended` after the hook `defence_resolving`. An `avoid` that succeeds is a miss (`why` `defence`). An `interpose` turns `deflect × min(energy, holds)`; the push share of what is left pushes, less the largest `brace`; the rest lands on each surface in the way at the energy over that surface's own area times its give, and what passes goes on to the part aimed at with the striking surface; what a surface was not affected by is recoil. A guard that took all of it stops the strike (`combat.defended`), and the push and the recoil still happen. | Design, from building defences (C4) | 07 §5.3 said the rest "lands on the interposed surface as a packet" without saying at what intensity. At the striking edge's, 18 J on a knife's edge is 900 MPa and breaks steel, which contradicts 07 §14; a surface put in the way spreads the blow, which is why a shield is broad. 07 §5.1 did not say what a list of surfaces, a protected neighbour or a failed contest count toward `handles`. | 07 §3.4, §5, §7, §14, §16.1. `clash_sim::combat::defence`, `StrikeTally.turned`, `Push.brace`. |
| D-713 | A defence may carry `evasion`, which its holder's own defences in force add to the aim's `against` for a strike from within their arc. The bundled `evasion` function is unchanged. | Design, from building defences (C4) | A sway or a weave makes someone harder to land on squarely as well as letting a blow slip by; the aim's `against` is content, so the defence says what it adds rather than the engine scaling the target's value. | 07 §4.2, §5.1. `combat::defence::evasion`; `core:dodge`. |
| D-714 | The bundled reactions: the `strike` window kind notices in `2 / wits` seconds of the candidate's own time and reaches 3 m; `parry`, `block`, `dodge`, `step_back` and the guard `guard` in `mods/core/data/combat/reactions.json`, with the contests `control` (the defender's fighting against half the attacker's: a guard set in time is kept nine times in ten between equals) and `slip`; the rule `ask_player_to_react`, off with the setting `core:ask_reactions` false; stances as rules to attach. `mods/core/data/shields.json` gives the knife and the club a `flat`, the arm a `forearm` and adds a `buckler`, sorted after the files they patch. | Design, from building defences (C4) | Wits is the bundled attribute of the mind; a new attribute would change every person. A parry that held half the time would make 07 §14's "control margin favours B" a coin toss. Item patches must load after `items.json`. | 07 §12, §14, §16.1; 03 §7.3. |
| D-715 | Perception gains the verb `attach_sense { who, sense }`. | Design, from building windows (T5) | A window is noticed only by a candidate that perceives its source (D-615). The application gives the player senses directly (D-705), but the bundled templates give nobody any, so a test script could not give a defender a window. The smallest change in perception's package. | 15 §15; 03 §7.2. `perception::props`. Templates giving senses are the application's or E9's. |
| D-716 | A mind answers a window its character noticed by its definition's new field `reactions`: the first listed that may start and would be ready in time, else the first that may start, through `Sim::react`; a directly controlled character is never answered for. The bundled guard lists `block` then `dodge` (by an optional patch in `combat/reactions.json`); townspeople and dogs list nothing. | Design, from joining reactions to the AI (T5) | 09 §6.3 asks for `best_reaction` by tactics and previews, neither of which is built; an ordered list in the mind's data is the smallest thing that lets a guard defend himself and keeps the choice in content. A parry is not listed: nothing yet lets a reaction require a held blade, and a parry with none puts nothing in the way. | 09 §3.1, §6.3; 03 §7.3. `ai::react_to_window`, `MindDef::reactions`. |
| D-717 | The application answers a `Reaction` decision with the reaction prompt (the role `decision.reaction`): the host hands the scene the window as its `window` value, each option's action by qualified id, label and whether it is in time, the time left in the character's own time (`Sim::window_time_left`) and the whole window at the same rate; the prompt's `react` and `pass` become `Command::React` and `Command::Pass`. The frontend's seconds are floats for showing only. | Design, from merging reactions (T5) into the build | Without it the bundled rule `ask_player_to_react` stopped time on a prompt the player could not see. The rate of the character's clock is read from what the simulation already answers (time left over universal time left), not from a new call. | 12 §3.2; `clash_app::host`. |
| D-720 | A reactive condition (05 §7.5) is checked at load from its source and its bytecode: one expression (no `all`/`any`/`not` structure, no `exists`), every property naming the events that change it unless it is constant for its subject (`kind`; an entity's `attr`, `pool` and `track`; an ability's `def`, `id`, `tags`), and every function a built-in that draws nothing or a host function with events; a mod's function and `now()` are refused. At run time a recording host turns each property read into subscriptions by event name and subject (an ability followed by its owner, an entity's stats by the entity), and each variable read into one by subject and name. | Design | The expression crate cannot be changed by this package, and the registry already carried `changed_by` for properties and functions; parsing the source finds the calls the bytecode does not show. Subscribing by subject rather than by argument is coarse but never misses a change. | A trait added makes stale whatever read `has_trait` of that entity, whatever trait it named; a re-evaluation that changes nothing costs one evaluation. Properties with arguments cannot be watched more finely without changing the registry. |
| D-721 | Reactive expressions are evaluated again at the end of the pump, when the event queue is empty, in the order of their owners (abilities' `while`, then traits'), at most `rules.max_reactive_rounds` rounds a pump (default 8), the rest reported as the fault `reactive.rounds` and left for the next pump. The subscriptions are not state: a simulation made or loaded builds them afresh at its first pump, and any owner whose state disagrees with a fresh evaluation is told. Trait `while`s are watched when what they read can be, and otherwise worked out as the traits package always has. | Design | Values follow from saved state (invariant A6), so saving them would only add a way to disagree. Existing trait content reads `has_tag`, which names no events. | No new save data for A1. The kernel calls `reactive::settle` from `pump`, `var_written` from `set_var` and `event_delivered` for every event. |
| D-722 | Abilities are held in a component of their own, `Abilities`, the seventeenth; each instance has a number given once in the simulation's life, kept with the entity that holds it in `AbilityIndex`, saved and hashed with the kernel's state (save layout 11). A handle of the kind `ability` is that number. Sources are templates (a new `abilities` field on templates and their options), trait instances that are not dormant (their `abilities`), and rules; items and body parts confer abilities through the traits they give. One ability is one instance across its sources; `revoke_ability` keeps an instance that traits still give, revoked, until a rule grants it again. | Design | The kernel's `Subject` cannot name an ability, and a handle must never name another thing; a number never reused satisfies both. Items and parts already give traits. | Every test script's hash changed once. The application and AI read instances through `ability::options` and `self.ability('id')`. |
| D-723 | `ability` is bound in what an ability works out about itself (parameters, `while`, grants' `params`, `cost`, `cooldown`, `if`, `value`; modes' `upkeep`, `cost`, `on_start`, `on_end`), not in actions: a grant hands the action the ability's numbers as its parameters (`"params": { "energy": "ability.power" }`), standing in for the command's. Parameters are numbers, registered as one property of the kind `ability` per name, read fresh each time and then modified by the `ability_params` of the owner's traits (a new field of traits) and the `params` of the entries of `always` its abilities apply. A grant's `if` and `value` are in the owner's mind. | Design | An action stays one generic definition that any ability, item or rule may start, and changing the action's scopes would have touched the action runtime. A trait's `modifiers` are attribute modifiers; parameter modifiers need their own list. | `ability.var.x` and `ability` in attached rules are not built. A granted action can still be started by `Act` without its ability. |
| D-724 | A use (`use_ability`, the verb `use_ability`, the command `UseAbility`) checks held, active, `at_rank`, cooldown, `if` and every cost, refusing before anything changes; raises `ability_using` (mutable `params` and `cost`) if anything listens and checks the cost again; prepares the action through the action runtime; then takes every cost in full, starts the cooldown, starts the action and raises `ability_used`. A refusal after the hook is raised as `ability_refused`, and an `action_starting` refusal refunds the pools. The verb reports every refusal as `ability_refused` and returns false. | Design | Invariant A4, and the action runtime's own rule that a refusal after a hook is raised, not returned. | `ability_refused` carries the reason's code and sentence. |
| D-725 | Costs are keyed by a pool, or by an item with `"item": true`, each an amount or `{ amount, overdraw, from, item, count }`. Overdraw always comes from the owner's pool at its ratio. `from` is an expression giving the entity that pays in the owner's place; with nobody there, or nobody with the pool, the use is unavailable with `ability.cost_source_gone`. A shortfall is refused with `ability.cost_short` naming who pays, the pool, what is wanted, what is there and what is short. Costs from stores (16 §5) are not built. | Design | The review addition (D-233) needs a cost drawn from something else; bindings (A7) are not built, so `from` takes any expression, and `ability.bound(name)` will be one. | Content can draw from a companion held in a variable today. |
| D-726 | A cooldown is the owner's own time (03 §3.4) at which the grant is ready, kept in the instance; no wake-up is scheduled. It stands still while the owner is out of the world and runs with the owner's clock. | Design | Nothing need happen when a cooldown ends; reading it is a subtraction, and a save carries it as a number. | No event says a grant is ready; `cooldown(action)` and `cannot_use` read it. |
| D-727 | A mode's upkeep is a pool rate under the mode's own stats source; the moment the first upkeep pool comes to nothing is worked out from the pool's line and scheduled once on the owner's clock, in the `Timer` domain with bit 60 of `arg` set (bits 61 to 63 clear; the low byte the mode, bits 8 to 59 the instance), handed to abilities before pools, bodies and traits. It is worked out again when the owner's pools are heard to move: `pool_crossed`, `attr_changed`, a trait added or removed, a cost paid, a move into the world. A mode ends at that wake-up if a pool is dry (`mode_ended` `how` `empty`), and is rescheduled otherwise. Switching is a toggle (verb or command); a windup is a grant whose action calls `start_mode`. | Design | A pool schedules crossings only at its thresholds, and the stats package was not to be changed; a wake-up of the mode's own ends it at the analytic tick with nothing scheduled in between. | A pool level changed by a verb without crossing a threshold is noticed at the next crossing or wake-up; until then the mode can outlast an emptied pool. |
| D-728 | Givers and stats sources of abilities are `(instance number << 16) \| slot`: a mode's index, or 256 plus an entry of `always`, each applied and taken back by itself, so that a rank that crosses an `at_rank` changes only that entry. When a mind moves, the mind-seated abilities held only from rules and templates go with it, keeping their number, rank and cooldowns; for a swap, both ways. | Design | Traits can only be withdrawn by giver, and an entry must come and go without disturbing the others. | A body keeps what its traits give, whatever their ability's seat, until traits have seats. |
| D-729 | The bundled powers of `mods/core/data/powers` are content only: `dash` (a timed trait that makes a way of moving count, from stamina, with a cooldown), `force_push` (a cone `release` of impact one cell in front of its user), `firebolt` (a charged held phase, a heat beam and fire laid with `add_field`; its rank is the track `pyromancy`, fed by a rule on `ability_used`), `shield` (a mode whose trait turns three fifths of each hit aside, with upkeep from `reserve`) and `heal` (closes open wounds and heals parts of someone in reach). Nobody has them by default; the template and the trait `power_sampler` give them. No `start` offers them yet: a start would change the application's new-game frame, which is the application's to change when it offers abilities. | Design | The beta needs powers to try, and they prove the system without the engine naming any. | Their actions are tagged `power`; adding them shifted the dense ids of later definitions in `core`. |
| D-770 | Suppression (08 §6.2) is kept in each instance as the list of what suppresses it and the list of what it resisted, each a suppressor: `effect` (a suppression `suppress_abilities` imposed, kept in the owner's `Abilities` with its tags and the owner's own time at which it lifts), `trait` (a trait instance whose definition lists `suppresses`, a new field of traits) or `area` (an area the owner is inside whose kind lists `suppresses`, a new field of area kinds the world carries). A suppressor reaches an ability that has any of its tags; an imposed one with no tags reaches every ability. Whenever what applies to the owner may have changed (a suppression imposed, lifted or run out, a trait given or taken, an area entered or left) each held instance is brought into line: what no longer applies is forgotten, and each new suppressor runs `ability_suppressing` once for that instance, a cancel making it resist while the suppressor lasts. An instance suppressed confers nothing, its modes end (`suppressed`) and bindings that end on suppression end; lifted, it confers again and its modes stay off. A state that is active and suppressed reads `suppressed`. An imposed suppression's end is a `Timer` wake-up of abilities whose low byte is `0xFF`, found by its id, scheduled again when the owner comes back into the world. The reactive rounds of a pump are counted afresh when the pump ends. | Design, from building suppression | The specification named the three ways to suppress and the hook, and not where suppression is kept, how a resistance lasts, what reaches an ability, how a mode ends, or how a timed one survives unloading. Keeping it in the instance saves it and needs nothing derived; asking once per suppressor keeps a resisting handler from being asked at every step; the free byte of the mode wake-up adds no domain. The rounds counter, reset only by an empty round, carried over between pumps and faulted a sequence of ordinary changes. | Save layout 16. `restore_abilities` lifts only imposed suppressions: a trait's or an area's lasts as long as they do. Trait `while`s that read what names no events are still worked out when a trait of the holder changes (D-721). |
| D-771 | Anchoring, the events of a moving area and radius shapes were already in the world (D-303); package A6 adds what areas change. An `area_kind` takes `gravity` (a number, a scale of the stratum's; or three numbers, a vector), `light` (as an element's) and `suppresses`, each the default of its areas: a kind's gravity applies unless the area's own override sets gravity, a kind's light unless the area carries one. An area may carry a light (`Area::light`), a source at the middle of its shape that the world counts as a moving source wherever light is read, before those handed in, the sum never below zero; the area table lists the areas that carry one. `create_area` takes `anchor`, `gravity`, `light` and `light_radius`. | Design, from building anchored areas | 08 §7.2 asked for negative light that an area or an entity can carry, and gravity by area kind. A light kept with the area moves with it at no cost to the light cache, which knows nothing that moves; perception reads light through the world and needs no change. A darkness on an entity is an area anchored to it. | The world's `areas` section is version 2. `LightSource` is saved and has `LightSource::shining`. Light entities carry themselves (lamps in hand) are still handed in by whoever asks. |
| D-772 | Bindings (08 §7.3) are kept by the simulation, in `AbilityIndex`, by a number given once: the instance and its owner, what is held (`trait` on someone with the binding as its giver, `entity`, `edit` with each cell's state before and placed, `area`, `rule`), a name, the mode whose `on_start` made it, the bits of `ends_on`, `on_end`, and its end as the owner's own time with a wake-up (the timer of abilities, `0xFF`). The terms are five arguments of every verb that binds (`ability`, `for`, `ends_on`, `on_end`, `as`), and `create_area` takes `bound` beside them; the ability is the argument or the `ability` bound where the effect is written, and an action is handed one by its grant as a parameter of type `ability`. A binding always ends with its ability's loss and its owner's removal; `ends_on` says the rest (default all). `binding_ending` may change `on_end`, `prolong` (seconds) or cancel, except when the ability is gone. Bound traits are given by `bind_trait` and rules attached by `bind_rule`, not by `add_trait` and `attach_rule` with `bound`. `conjure` restores a cell only while it is still the state placed; a cell not loaded is left. `release` is an `on_end` (and `dismiss`'s `how`), since `release` is the verb of energy releases. Portals are not built and nothing binds to them. | Design, from building bindings | A binding must outlive its owner's component to be ended when its owner is removed, and be found by its thing for `dispel` and `bound_by`. An argument written as a JSON object is a value source (05 §6), so `"bound": { "for": … }` cannot be an argument; flat terms read the same in every verb. `add_trait` and `attach_rule` belong to other packages (traits, the rule compiler). | Save layout 16. `rules.max_bindings` (default 64) refuses with `ability.too_many_bindings`; no ability with `ability.no_binder`; a bad term with `ability.bad_binding`. |
| D-773 | `summon { template, at, mind?, obey?, … }` makes the entity through the template service and binds it. `mind` sets the `mind` it thinks by. With `obey` (default true) it takes the stances its summoner's mind holds and none toward its summoner, and is given a track of its summoner as told (15 §7); it is given its mind's senses, which a mind otherwise gives only at its first thought, and is prompted to think. The entity property `bound_by` names whose ability it is bound to, so that a mind can keep at its summoner's heel. `create_area` takes its `at` from its `anchor` when `at` is left out. `MindState::stances` lists a mind's stances. | Design, from building summoning | The AI keeps stances and no orders (relations and orders are the social layer's); taking the summoner's stances is the obedience the AI has. A mind's state made before its first thought (by `set_stance`) left it without senses, and a servant that never saw its summoner could not follow it. An anchored area made in a mode's `on_start` has no cell to name but its owner's, which may be null. | When the social layer lands, `obey` should give a `commands` relation (15 §10) as well. |
| D-774 | The bundled powers of `mods/core/data/powers/areas.json` and `bound.json` are content only: `time_stop` (a mode: the user's clock anchored to the realm's and an area round them, anchored, of the kind `still_time` whose clock runs at a thousandth, bound; upkeep from `reserve`), `null_field` (a mode: an anchored area of the kind `null_field`, which suppresses `power`; the maker's abilities resist their own field by a rule on `ability_suppressing`), `gravity_well` (a grant: an area of six times gravity at a cell for a while), `float` (a mode: an anchored area of no gravity over the user's own cell), `summon_hound` (a grant: a `dog` that thinks as a `hound`, which goes for those held hostile and otherwise keeps at its summoner's heel), `stone_wall` (a grant: a line of wall conjured square to the user's way, restored when its time runs out) and `shadow` (a mode: an anchored area carrying a light of negative intensity). All twelve bundled abilities carry the tag `power`; the template and trait `power_sampler` give all twelve. | Design | The brief's superpower sandbox needs them, and they show each mechanism composed from data. | The tests of `crates/clash-sim/tests/abilities.rs` load the minds and perception content the hound names. |
| D-690 | Minds think in batches: a mind is prompted (by `decision_needed`, a percept as clear as its mind's `wake_clarity`, `detected`, `lost_track` and `memory_added` of its own, an event a goal names in `wake_on`, coming into the world or out of direct control, the end of a step's action, or `think`) and thinks when the event queue is next empty, with the other minds prompted, in order of entity. With nothing to prompt it a mind thinks at its interval: its `think`, doubled after each thought that changed nothing up to `stretch` times, a wait of up to a quarter more by a hash of its entity when it is idle. A mind thinks at most `ai.max_thoughts_per_tick` times a tick, a batch holds at most `ai.max_minds_per_batch`, a thought takes at most `ai.max_steps_per_thought` steps that take no time. Each mind draws only from its own stream, seeded from the simulation's seed and its entity. | Design, from building minds (I3) | 09 §3.3 named the triggers and said minds due together are served by tick and entity, without saying when in a tick or how bounded. Thinking where the queue empties sees every event of the moment, and what one mind does reaches another only through events delivered after the batch, so order does not change a decision (invariant N8). A stream of its own keeps a character's chance its own: another mind thinking does not change it. | 09 §3.3, §3.5; invariant N8. `ai::think_due`, `prompt`, `think_in_order`. The kernel's pump calls `ai::on_event` and `think_due`. |
| D-691 | A goal's `about` is chosen by the engine from the mind's beliefs: one of its tracks (`track`), its memories of some events (`memory`) or a place (`place`), each candidate tested by `where` and the greatest `best` taken, the goal held keeping what it is about while that is still a candidate. `$about` is typed by the kind: `entity`, `map<any>` (event, loc, age, salience, confidence, actor, how) or `loc`. A `settle` step marks a track settled until it is perceived again and a memory for good (at most 64 kept). | Design, from building minds (I3) | 09 §3.2 wrote `about` as a query over memories, and queries (K6) are not built. A choice made in Rust from the mind's own beliefs reads nothing it has not perceived, and the typed `$about` lets the compiler refuse `$about.loc` of an entity (N1). Without `settle` a guard who searched a place would search it again for as long as he remembered it. | 09 §3.2, §3.5. `ai::defs::About`, `AboutHeld`. Queries can replace the selector later without changing goals that use it. |
| D-692 | `weight` is a property of an entity read of the character itself, `self.weight(name)`, giving its mind's weight with what the mind's `adjust` entries whose conditions hold add, or 0. `adjust` (`if`, `weights`, `goals`) is how traits colour a mind until traits are joined to it. | Design, from building minds (I3) | 09 §3.2 wrote `weight('duty')`, a function; a function is given only its arguments and cannot tell whose mind asks. Conditions on the character are what traits will set. | 09 §3.1, §3.5, §17. |
| D-693 | The steps of the beta are `go`, `do`, `choose`, `wait`, `look`, `say`, `set`, `settle`, `stance`, `keep_near` and `keep_from`, each with an optional `if`. `go`, `keep_near` and `keep_from` start the action the mind names in `go_action` with its parameter `to`; `choose` starts the first action carrying its purpose as a tag that can start, with `target`; `say` raises `said`, which content gives a signal. An interaction is a `do` of the action that does interactions; `use`, `job`, `goal`, `follow` and `run` are not built. | Design, from building methods (I4) | The engine names no action: the mind says which action walks. Options and tactics (I5) are not built, and a tag is what an attack definition can already carry, so a guard can fight with whatever attacks content adds. Speech (S8) is not built; an event lets others hear a call for help now. | 09 §4.1. Invariant N2 holds: every act is `start_action`, `wait`, a turn or speech. |
| D-694 | Until relations (S7), a mind holds others hostile or feared by stances: from tags they present once perceived (`hostile_to`, `fear_of`), from being made out as the actor of an event it perceived (`hostile_after`, `fear_after`), or by the `stance` step and the `set_stance` verb. Stances are kept by persona in the mind's state. `self.hostile(x)`, `self.fears(x)` and `self.stance(x)` read them. | Design, from building reactions (I5 beta) | 09 §3.4 reads enemies from relations, which are not built. A stance kept by persona follows a disguise as beliefs do (15 invariant P2), and becomes a relation whose kind opposes when S7 lands. | 09 §6.6. |
| D-695 | Paths of a character whose mind has thought cost more for cells where it saw someone in the last five seconds (`crowd_cost`) and within a remembered hazard (`hazards`), and a cell counts only if its whole footprint fits; `movement::believed_path` applies this for every mover. Routes in use are checked again at the next batch after an element changes or is destroyed within a cell of them; a blocked one is marked stale (`movement::mark_journey_stale`) and `route_blocked` raised. | Design, from building navigation (I1 beta) | 09 §5.3 made doors, hazards and creatures cost by what is believed; fields seen are not noted by perception yet, so a hazard is what the mind remembers. Checking at the batch bounds the work of a collapse that changes thousands of cells to one check of each route. | 09 §5.4a. Regions, tract graphs and flow fields (I2) are not built. Additive in `movement` (`mark_journey_stale`, the call in `believed_path`). |
| D-696 | A template gives senses by id as written (`senses`), attached when the entity is made where perception's definitions are loaded; a template whose senses name none of the loaded senses fails to load where any are loaded. A `mind` names senses too, given to its character when it first thinks if it has none: the bundled minds give sight and hearing that way, and the `person` and `dog` templates give none. | Design, from building the bundled minds | 06 §10.1 has templates contribute senses, and a spawned person who cannot perceive cannot think about anything. Given by the template, a player made from `person` would perceive with the species' senses whatever its start names, which the application lets a start decide; given by the mind, only a character that thinks for itself is given them. Written ids keep the living-things content loadable without perception's. | 06 §10.1; 09 §3.5. |
| D-697 | A method is written in a goal's `methods` as `{ if?, steps }`, or on its own as a `method` definition that says what `$about` is (`about`: `track`, `memory`, `place` or none), named by `{ if?, method }`; both conditions must hold, and a goal names only methods written for its kind of `about`. | Design, from building methods (I4) | 09 §3.2 wrote methods in place; a definition written in place is compiled on its own, before the goal that holds it is known, so `$about` would have no type. | 09 §4.1. |
| D-698 | What minds keep is the component `mind_state` (the goal held, its method and step, the step under way, its stream, home, stances, settled things, penalties, interval) and `AiState` on the simulation (minds due, the schedule of thoughts, cells changed under routes). Both are saved and hashed; the save layout is version 12 (after abilities' 11), and every state hash changes. | Design, from building minds | A mind's state goes with its entity when it is stored or removed, as every component does; what is due belongs to no one mind. | 09 §3; 01 R7. |
| D-730 | The benchmarks are a subcommand of the tools, `clash-tools bench`, which `cargo xtask bench` builds in release and runs against `budgets.toml` at the root of the repository; `--check` fails on a measurement over its limit by more than its margin. Each benchmark builds its scene from the repository's mods and keeps the best of several rounds of plain wall-clock timing. The file holds one `[[budget]]` for each row of a specification's budget table that has a benchmark (`bench`, `spec`, `row` word for word, `limit` with a unit, `margin` as a share), and is read without a TOML library. It is not a step of `cargo xtask check`. | Design, from building the benchmarks | `criterion` is allowed (01 §13) but brings a large tree of dependencies and statistics the gate does not need: on a shared machine noise only adds time, so the best round is the figure. The tools already reach every crate (01 §3) and may read the wall clock. A run takes minutes and its figures belong to the machine. | 14 §6.3; plans W23, T8, Z1, TL5 `beta`. A test checks every row of the file against its specification's table. Rows with no benchmark are not in the file yet; benchmarks with no row (field of view, a line of sight, a party member's sweep) print a figure with no budget. |
| D-731 | The cosmos counts every borrow that could change the cells of a grid (`grid_mut`, a grid's slot, a column), but not the occupant index. The light cache keeps the count with each chunk's light when it last found it standing, and answers at once while the count has not moved, without comparing the revisions of the 27 chunks round it. The count is not state: not saved, hashed or cloned into meaning. | Design, from the performance pass | Every read of light at one cell began a new pass that compared 27 shapes, about 0.6 µs, and sight reads light for every target it sweeps. Equal counts mean no cell anywhere changed, so the answer kept is the answer. | 02 §22 (caches); 15 §3.3, §17. `Cosmos::light`, `static_light` and `fill_light` all gain. Field steps move the count, so a fire burning costs light its old comparison and no more. |
| D-732 | `Cosmos::sight_to_heights(eye, eye_alt, target, alts)` says which of up to 32 heights over one cell an eye sees, each exactly as `line_of_sight_between` would. Where the eye and every height are on one storey the lines of each direction of walking pass the same cells, differing in height alone, so the cells are walked once for each direction and only the heights are measured for each line. A line of sight keeps the column of the cell before. Perception's `exposure` asks it; a sweep reads the light at a target once for the bound and the strength, and works out what belongs to the observer once for all targets. | Design, from the performance pass | Four lines of sight for each target were most of the 3 µs one evaluation of sight cost (15 §17). Between storeys the lines cross floors at different points, so there each line is walked alone, as before. | 02 §22; 15 §3.1, §17. Tested against the first forms, kept as references: random scenes in `clash-world`, random crowds in `clash-sim`. |
| D-733 | Delayed signals on their way are kept by id in a map, and saved and hashed as the list in order of id they were. | Design, from the performance pass | The arrival of the oldest took it out of the front of a list of hundreds and moved the rest: a quarter of perception's time in a crowd. | 15 §3.1. The save layout and every hash are unchanged. |
| D-734 | A field layer that holds more than `FIELD_DENSE_FROM` (512) cells of its chunk keeps a table of all 8192, and goes back to a list under `FIELD_SPARSE_BELOW` (128). Either is read, iterated in cell order, compared, saved and hashed as the list. | Design, from the performance pass | A fire fills most of a chunk, and adding or taking away a cell of a list in cell order moved thousands: a fifth of the time of a step. | 02 §16. The tract blob is unchanged. A table is 128 KB for as long as a chunk is mostly alight. |
| D-735 | A field step sums the changes of level it asks for in a table for each chunk and field, remembering the last two it used, and puts them in order once: by one 64-bit number for each slot, its grid, storey, row, column and field packed by their spans, with its index below, where they fit; compared as they are where they do not. The tables are emptied and kept for the next step. A change is written reading the level it adds to once; drift is looked up only where an area touches the tract. | Design, from the performance pass | A hash map keyed by grid, cell and field took a hash for every share moved, and its sorted list cost a tenth of a step. The order written in is the one 02 §16 and D-503 fix, and the sums do not depend on the order they are made in. | 02 §16, §25. Tested against the hash map on random changes near one another and far apart. |
| D-736 | The path search finds its nodes through a table of each chunk it meets, a cell's entry its node, instead of a hash map of cells; it keeps the column of the cell before when it reads a cell's geometry. The tables are emptied and kept for the next search. | Design, from the performance pass | Every step tried looked up three nodes; a third of a search was hashing cells. | 02 §22 (D-315); 09 §19. Paths, their costs and their order of search are unchanged; the index is tested against a map of cells. |
| D-737 | `FovSet::offsets_between(low, high)` walks a view's cells on those storeys alone, in the order `offsets` gives them. A party member's sweep, which keeps from one storey up to three down (D-618), walks them instead of every storey its view holds. | Design, from the performance pass | Under open sky a view of 60 m holds twenty storeys of air above, and the sweep passed over every cell of them to keep none. | 02 §22; 15 §13, §17. Tested against the view's cells filtered. |
| D-740 | Projectiles as built. `projectile` is a definition type of the simulation: a mass, a cross-section (default the surface's area), drag, restitution (default 0), a striking surface (`kind`, `area`, `give`, `span`), a field it `leaves`, `on_impact` effects, a `thrust`; `guidance` and `fuse` are read and carried. `shoot` is a verb, not an effect of the rule compiler: it needs no locals of an action. A shot leaves from the middle of the shooter's cell at 0.8 of its body's height unless told; it is aimed along `dir`, at an entity (a part's band middle, a height, or its body's middle), a cell (a height above its floor, or level) or a point; its speed is given or `√(2E/m)`; dispersion turns it across and up by two angles drawn from a normal distribution of standard deviation `spread`, four numbers from the stream `shots` for each projectile whatever the spread; `lob` sends it along the lower ballistic arc (45 degrees where none reaches). Each projectile is a world body with a `Projectile` owner whose tag keys a `Shot` kept by the simulation (`RangedState`, saved; the save layout is version 11; hashed only once anything has been fired). | Design, from building C9 | 07 §11.1 left open where a shot leaves from, how dispersion is drawn and how the simulation knows a body's projectile again. | 07 §11.7; package C9. |
| D-741 | A shot strikes a creature through the delivery of a strike: the world's `body_hit_occupant` for a shot is answered in place of the blunt blow of 06 §7.4 with a hit of the body's whole energy and the projectile's surface at the height above the creature's footing where the body met it (`HitAt::Height`), through worn layers and the body's. The hit is given no direction, so that the body wiring does not also carry its exits to the block behind; the projectile carries them on, the world body given the speed of what exits along its way by `impulse`. A creature with no part at that height is passed by at full speed; one a projectile leaves nothing of lodges it, and it ends. Things made from a template that gives a body collide with bodies in flight (`COLLIDES`), which the world requires before it strikes an occupant. | Design, from building C9 | 02 §15.2 strikes only occupants flagged `COLLIDES`, and no template set it; 07 §11.1 asks for the strike's delivery. | 07 §11.7; 06 §10.1; every test script's hash changed. |
| D-742 | A projectile that strikes an element is the world's to resolve (02 §15.2); the simulation raises `projectile_hit` at the cell. One that did not go through and has no restitution is lodged there and ends: a bullet in a wall, an arrow in the ground, so that it does not slide on along the floor. `on_impact` runs once, at the first thing a projectile strikes, and it ends there. Where a projectile ends it leaves the field it names, by `add_field`. | Design, from building C9 | A body the world turns back keeps its other motion and slides; a shot that does not penetrate should stop. | 07 §11.7. |
| D-743 | Near misses as built: at each event of a shot's flight (it struck something, landed, left), the stretch of its path from where it was last known (where it was fired from, or last struck) is looked along, and everyone with a body whose middle lies within `projectile.near_miss` (2 m) of it, other than the shooter and whoever it struck, is told once by `projectile_passed`, with the distance. A creature passed by at a height where nothing of it is is told with distance zero. | Design, from building C9 | The world reports no wake of a body between impacts; a projectile flies nearly straight between them. | 07 §11.7. |
| D-744 | An instant shot casts one ray (02 §22) through cells and every creature with a body but the shooter, up to `range` (`shoot.instant_range`, 1000 m). An element that closes the whole path, or the share it closes on a draw from `shots`, takes a packet of what is left at `E / (area × give)` by `damage_along`; what it destroys passes `passed` on and anything else ends the ray; a creature is struck as in flight. | Design, from building C9 | 07 §11.3 named the ray without its rules. | 07 §11.7. |
| D-745 | Ammunition is items: an item's `round` block names a projectile, an energy, a count of projectiles and a casing. A container whose `holds` has an `order` (`first_in`, `last_in`) is a store: its `Holds` keeps what is stowed in it in the order it was put in (hashed only where there is one); an equal round put in joins only the stack put in just before it; a member split off keeps its stack's place. Firing spends one member (`item::spend`). An item's feed is itself if it is a store, else the first store fixed in it. `load` puts rounds in one member at a time by `put_in`, from a container, a stack, or what the loader carries. | Design, from building C10 | 06 §9.1 left `round` for this package; a store must keep the order loaded (the review row "Ammunition as items"), which stacks rejoining any equal stack would lose. | 07 §11.7; 06 §9.1; package C10. |
| D-746 | A weapon's numbers are an item's `shot` block (`spread`, `efficiency`, `noise`, `flash`, `energy`, `fits`), and what is fitted to it multiplies them: a shot through an item takes the product of its own number and those of everything held `fixed` in it that states it. Fitting is the verb `fit`, one item for each tag in `fits`; taking a fitted item into a grasp takes it off. A module acts through the weapon it is fitted to, not as a modifier conferred on the bearer (16 §11.2): a scope on a slung rifle does not steady a pistol in the other hand. | Design, from building C10 | The brief suggested modules as modifiers given through the items package; a modifier on the bearer cannot tell which weapon a shot is fired with. | 07 §11.7; 16 §11.2 is unchanged for devices. |
| D-747 | Throwing as built: `throw` puts an item `who` holds down where it stands and launches it as a body that is the item, with its own mass and cross-section, from the height a shot leaves; it strikes with the surface named, or bluntly with none, through the same delivery as a projectile, and raises `thrown`. A timed charge is content: the bundled `throw` starts an `after` timer owned by the charge that lets energy go where it is and unmakes it. | Design, from building C10 | 07 §11.1 had `throw` launch the item with its own surfaces; a timer is what rules already have. | 07 §11.7; C7's `throw` of a gripped creature extends the same verb. |
| D-748 | Left out of ranged combat for now: the `shot` window is offered to nobody until reaction windows exist (T5), so `bullet_time` and `caught_arrow` wait, as do defences against shots (C4); `guidance`, `fuse`, `projectile_lost_lock` and `heat_seeker`; `lead_point`; costs drawn from power stores (G9); the subject kind `body` (events carry the body's number); a bundled recoil rule; fighting at lower detail. The `volley` timing is an ignored test. | Design, from building C9 and C10 | Each waits for a package not built. | 07 §11.7; plans/combat.md. |
| D-749 | The bundled ranged content is in `mods/core`: projectiles, the contest `marksmanship` and the actions `shoot`, `aim_shot`, `reload`, `draw_and_loose` and `throw` in `data/combat/ranged.json`; the pistol, rifle, magazines, rounds, casing, scope, suppressor, bow, arrows, quiver, throwing knife and charge in `data/combat/arms.json`; the report, flash and impact of a shot as signals in `data/perception/shots.json`. An action aims at where its actor believes the target is (`believed_loc`), at a height its parameter gives, and is unavailable, saying why, when its weapon is empty. | Design, from building C10 | 01 R11: a shooter aims at what it believes. | 07 §11.7. |
| D-750 | The `scenario` definition type replaces `start`, which is deleted; the bundled starts became scenarios (`core:town`, `core:wayside`) and `core:wold_market` became `core:town_life`. As built it has `name`, `doc`, `intro`, `tags`, `worlds` (exactly one: `realm`, `as`, `seed` a whole number, `random` or `ask`), `options`, `settings`, `locked`, `start` (`characters`, exactly one: `template`, `create`, `senses`; `at`, one or more places, each `name` and exactly one of `cell` and `site` with an optional `role`; `time`, `day` and `at` as `HH:MM`; `do`), `rules`, `objectives`, `ends`, `saving` (`policy`, `on_party_gone`) and `screens`. It lives in `clash-sim`, where its state is; a start's `glyph` is gone, since templates give glyphs. | Design, from building SC1 | 13 §3 said the start type is deleted when this one lands, and keeping two ways to begin would double every path through the application. The game's state, its rules and its objectives are the simulation's; the interface needs only names and screens. One world and one character are what the rest of the engine can begin with until charts and parties exist (SC2). | 13 §3, §4; 12 §3.4. `clash_sim::scenario::ScenarioDef`; supersedes the start of D-591, D-654, D-704 and D-705. |
| D-751 | A game begins in two steps. `scenario::make_world` makes the scenario's realm from the seed its `seed` chooses (`SeedSpec::choose`: fixed, the one drawn by the application for `random`, the one asked for `ask`) and moves universal time on, before anything is in the world, so that the realm's clock reads `start.time` by its sky's day and `starts_at`. `Command::BeginScenario { scenario, party, place, options }` then gives each character of the party senses if its templates gave none (its entry's `senses`, else every sense in order of qualified id), places the first at the place chosen (a cell; or the anchor of a site of the realm answering `def`, `tag` and `name`, drawn from the rule stream after every region of a realm of at most 64 regions is surveyed, with footing looked for ring by ring up to 8 cells round a column that has none), applies `settings` and the answers to `options`, switches the scenario rule groups, gives the party direct control, records the game, shows the objectives that wait for nothing, runs `start.do` and raises `scenario_started`. The game's time played is counted from when it began. | Design, from building SC1 | Moving time before anything exists is the only way to set a calendar the clocks do not have (09 §14.2 is not built) without anything happening in the time moved. A command keeps the beginning in the input log and replayable, and the same answers give the same state hash. Senses given by the command replace the application's (D-705), which were outside the input log. A site's roles need the world to record where its marks went, which it does not. | 13 §4.1, §4.3, §4.4; 12 §3.4, §3.5. `clash_sim::scenario::{make_world, begin, BeginScenario}`, `FOOTING_REACH`, `MAX_SURVEYED_REGIONS`. |
| D-752 | A scenario's `rules` names rule groups. When a game begins, every group a scenario names is switched (`set_rule_group`): on if the scenario begun names it, off otherwise. A group meant only for scenarios is written `"enabled": false`, so that it is off where no game is being played. | Design, from building SC1 | 05 §9.6 already makes a scenario's special rules groups; switching them at the beginning costs nothing while they are off and needs no change to the rule runtime. | 13 §3; 05 §9.6. `clash_sim::scenario::run::switch_groups`. |
| D-753 | Settings as far as scenarios need them (P6, beta): the `setting` type (`name`, `kind` `flag`, `choice` or `number`, `options`, `range`, `default`, `doc`) and `Command::Setting { key, value }`, refused with `setting.locked` for a setting the scenario being played names in `locked` and with `setting.bad_value` for a value its definition does not take; a change raises `setting_changed` (`key`, `old`, `new`). A scenario's `options` name setting definitions; the answers are given to `BeginScenario` and checked the same way (`setting.not_an_option` for one it does not ask about). A key with no definition (an engine setting) takes any value. | Design, from building SC1 | SC1 depends on P6 and its "Done when" needs the `Setting` command; the options screen needs to know what a setting takes. | 04 §10; 03 §4.4; 13 §3. `clash_sim::scenario::SettingDef`, `Command::Setting`. P6 is beta. |
| D-754 | A scenario's own variables are global variables (`global.var.*`): there is no `scenario` kind of subject. What 13 §13 calls `party`, `party.count`, `start.site` and `scenario.id` are, as built, the bound names `party` (`list<entity>`), `start_site` (`site?`) and `start_cell` (`loc`) in a scenario's expressions, and the functions `party()`, `fallen()`, `scenario_id()`, `objective_state(id, mission?)`, `mission_state(mission)` and `played_for()`, all safe for belief. | Design, from building SC1 and SC3 | A game is one scenario, so the simulation's own variables serve, and a new kind of subject would change the kernel's subject table that every package shares. Functions read the same anywhere, a screen included. | 13 §13; 05 §6. `clash_sim::scenario::journal::functions`. |
| D-755 | An objective's `done` and `failed`, and an ending's `if`, are reactive conditions (05 §7.5) that read truth, with `self` and `taker` the objective's taker, `party`, `start_site`, `start_cell` and `global` bound, and a mission's parameters as locals. When mods load each is looked through: the events each property and function it reads is registered as changed by, and `var_changed` for a variable; something with none registered, or a query, makes it watch every event. A handler for each watched event notes it while a game is played; at every safe point (the end of `safe_point`, and the start of `advance`) the objectives that watch something noted are evaluated again (`failed` first), in rounds while what that did makes more to look at, at most `rules.max_reactive_rounds`. What waits to be looked at is saved and hashed with the game. The objective's `text` and `marker` read in the taker's mind context. | Design, from building SC3 | 13 §5.1 lets conditions read truth and §13 says objectives read belief; the conditions of the bundled content (where a parcel is) are truth, and "what the character knows" is belief functions such as `knows` read of the taker, which a truth context may call too. What the player is shown must be belief. Watching by event type is exact enough for invariant Z4, and costs nothing while nothing a condition reads changes. A noted event lost by a save between two calls of `advance` would have broken the save-and-load mode of the test scripts. | 13 §5.1, §13; 05 §7.5. `clash_sim::scenario::{Tables, Watch, settle}`. |
| D-756 | A mission is started with `start_mission { mission, taker?, with? }`, numbered from 1 in its game, its parameters kept as values in the order its definition writes them. Its objectives are shown in order of `after`; it is done when every objective is, failed when one fails or its `failed` holds, expired when `expires` runs out. Expiry, of a mission or an objective, is a wake-up on universal time in the `Timer` domain with `arg` bit 59 set (`SCENARIO_WAKE_TAG`; abilities' modes use bit 60), taken by the scenario's handler, which is in front of the traits' for that domain. The journal reads `entity.objectives`: rows of the scenario's own objectives for every character of the party, then each mission the character took on and its objectives, with markers worked out from belief (`believed_loc`) and `where` in words from the character's own place. `entity.site` gives the site of where an entity is, kept current by `entity_relocated`. | Design, from building SC3 | Numbers name missions in verbs and events without a new kind of subject. The `Timer` domain is the one for timers of things that are not rules; a tag of its own keeps it apart from pools, bodies and traits. A list property of the character itself is what a mind context may read in full (D-702). | 13 §5.2; 14 §13. `clash_sim::scenario::{start_mission, journal}`. |
| D-757 | `AppRequest` gains `RestoreCheckpoint { name }`, `EndScenario { outcome }` and `TakeControl { entity }`. The application carries every request out between two calls of `advance` (invariant Z6): a checkpoint is saved under its name unless the policy is `none`; a restore loads that save and carries it on; `TakeControl` makes the character the player's, the camera following it; `EndScenario` stops advancing the game, removes its save under `single`, writes a line in the log and opens the summary (the scenario's `screens.summary`, else the role `summary`) with `title`, `outcome` and `text`. An ended game cannot be saved, and leaving it saves nothing. | Design, from building SC7 | 13 §9.1 gives the requests and §9.4 what an ending does; a game of one save that has ended has nothing left to carry on. | 13 §9; 12 §3.2, §3.6. `clash_app::game::Game::{end_scenario, take_control, restore_checkpoint}`. |
| D-758 | The new-game flow is data: `app.scenarios` lists the scenarios by qualified id; activating one asks for `new_game { scenario }`; a scenario with more than one place or with options, asked for without `place`, is set up instead: `app.setup` (a new read of `app`, `AppState::setup`) holds its name, doc, seed, places and options, the screen of the role `setup` is opened with `scenario`, the `creation` op `option` answers an option, and choosing a place asks for `new_game { scenario, place }`. A scenario with an `intro` has it shown on the role `intro` (`title`, `text`) over the play screen. The application draws a `random` seed from the clock. | Design, from building SC1 | 13 §12 makes every step a screen; one service with an optional place keeps the screens simple, and a game with one place needs no extra step. | 13 §12; 12 §3.4, §10.7. `clash_app::app::Setup`, `clash_scene::AppState::setup`; `mods/ui/data/scenario.json`. |
| D-759 | The party is gone when no character of it is directly controlled and not dead (13 §9.3's default `when`; `when` itself is not built). `party_gone` is raised once, the scenario's `on_party_gone.do` runs with the last of the party as `taker`, and then: `end` ends the game as `as` (default `lost`); `restore` asks for the last checkpoint, or with none ends the game with the reason in `scenario_ended.reason`; `continue` tries the ways of `by` in order (`own`: another of the party still alive; `create`: a new character from the scenario's character template at the place the party began), takes control of the first found, counts the others fallen, and ends the game when there is none. `become` waits for SC2. | Design, from building SC7 | The defaults of 13 §9.3, and a refusal that still ends the game as the specification asks. | 13 §9.3. `clash_sim::scenario::run::party_gone`. |
| D-790 | Walking into someone the party sees standing that way is a bump: the host does not submit the step (`Session::bump_creatures`, `bumped_into`, from the view of the last batch) and the loop runs, by the preference whose bare name is `bump_attack` (`hostile`, the default; `always`; `ask`; `never`), the control named `attack_bump` or `attack_bump_ask` with `it` a map of `target`, `where` and `hostile`, true where the character holds them hostile or fears them (`hostile`, `fears` of its own mind). Under `never` the step is submitted and refused as before. | Design, from the application's third pass | Movement asks nobody who is in a cell before the stride (`can_step`), so a step into someone plays and then fails; the player must be offered the blow first. The character's own stances are what it believes; whether the other holds the character hostile is the other's mind, which the player may not read. | 12 §3.7. `clash_app::interact::creature_value`; the `ui` mod's `fighting.json`. |
| D-791 | `clash_sim::offer` (an additive module) answers what a character could start: `could_start(sim, actor, action, given)`, changing nothing and drawing nothing (in the world, life lets it act, parameters given are the action's, the rest from their defaults in its mind with no random stream, `requires` with its `fail_text` once every parameter without a default is given, every attack's `with` finds a surface, else a sentence of what the blow needs, enough working slots per channel); and the entity properties `actions_with(tag, target?)`, `ability_uses(ability?)`, `ability_modes` and `known_sites`, each a list of maps for a screen, read of the character itself. `actions_with` orders those that could start first, then those that strike with something held, then fewer slots, then qualified id. | Design, from the application's third pass | The attack key must offer what the character can make with what it holds, through the actions' own availability and reasons, and the action runtime's `prepare` takes `&mut Sim` because a default may draw. Footing, slots held by other actions and hooks belong to the moment of starting and stay the runtime's. The order makes the quick blow the lightest that can be made without the engine naming one. | 12 §9.3; 07 §3.1; 08 §5.1. Registered in `engine_properties`.
