The tags `strike` and `shot` are the `ui` mod's choice, not the engine's. |
| D-792 | The `act` effect gains `{ "first": "…" }` beside `{ "pick": "…" }`, and `with`, values for parameters by name settled before anything is asked (a null is as if not given; a name the action lacks is passed over). An entry of the list may be a map that says `available` and `why` (an unavailable one is greyed by the list picker and refused with its reason, `first` passes it over), `ability` (the command becomes `use_ability { ability, action, params }`) and `given` (the parameters the ability's grant supplies, neither asked nor sent). The list picker is opened with every field of each entry. | Design, from the application's third pass | Quick and precise attacks (D-037, D-079) need the first blow that can be made at the target selected, and a use of an ability must collect the action's parameters as any action's are while leaving those its grant supplies to it. All is additive to the effect and to `Collecting`; actions stay free of presentation. | 12 §9.3, §10.5. `clash_scene::control::ask`, `COMMAND_USE_ABILITY`, `FIELD_*`. |
| D-793 | The scene keeps a selected target (`Scene::target`), set by the `camera` effect's new `target` (`1`, `-1`: the next or the previous creature the party perceives on the followed character's storey, nearest first, never the followed one; failing, with a reason, when none is in sight) and dropped by `untarget`; read as `app.target`, which is null once the party no longer perceives it; drawn on map views in the theme style `target` (`WorldView::set_marked`). | Design, from the application's third pass | A target cycle must run over what the party perceives, never truth: the view model's creature sprites are exactly that. `app` is the place for interface state a screen reads; an entity read through it is held to what may be read of anyone perceived. | 12 §9.2, §10.4, §10.5, §6.5. |
| D-794 | `ParamAsk` gains `settled`, the roles and parameters settled before the one asked for. The application answers `candidates` for a parameter of the kind `part`: the parts on the outside of the last entity settled (those that are there and inside no other, each a map of `value` and its name in the body as `label`), while the character perceives it, after a first row `wherever it lands` whose value is null. | Design, from the application's third pass | Combat aims by part (07 §4.2), and a part is a subject the scene cannot list; the parts seen on someone perceived are what the character could aim at. Previews (07 §13) are not built, so no odds are shown. | 12 §9.3. `clash_app::host::PART_ANYWHERE`. |
| D-795 | The abilities screen (`ui:abilities`) lists `view.ability_uses` and `view.ability_modes`; a use is made with `act` and `{ "first": "[it]" }`, a mode switched with the command `set_mode { ability, mode, on }`, which the host makes `SetMode`. Quick slots are the preferences `slot_1` to `slot_9` (text, an ability's qualified id), set in the screen's context `abilities` by the number keys (`bind_1` to `bind_9`) and used in play by the controls `slot_1` to `slot_9`; they are kept between runs with the player's other choices, not in saves. An action is put on a key by rebinding. | Design, from the application's third pass | 12 did not say where quick slots live; a slot is a display and input choice, which is what preferences are, and keeping it out of the save keeps saves free of interface state. | 12 §10.7; 08 §5. |
| D-796 | The map screen (`ui:map`) is a `map_view` zoomed out by three over the view model, with the journal's markers pinned: the application gives the view, as each batch ends, the `marker` of each row of the character's journal (`WorldView::set_pins`, drawn with the theme mark `marker`), and the screen lists `view.known_sites` (sites whose anchor the character's map memory holds) and `view.objectives`. | Design, from the application's third pass | The region map and the chart (12 §6.2) are not built; a zoomed map view over remembered cells is what the character knows of the place, and the markers already follow what the character perceived. | 12 §6.5, §10.7. |
| D-797 | `app.controls(context?)` lists every control the loaded mods define, bound or not, with its keys in a context; the keys screen (`ui:help`) lists it, so that a mod's controls appear. The context menu (`ui:context_menu`) offers, for the place clicked, Attack, Shoot and Talk for a creature and Go there, Use what is near and Look around, each the control or action a key reaches. | Design, from the application's third pass | `app.keys` lists only what a keymap binds, so a control a mod adds without a key was invisible to the player. | 12 §9.4, §10.4, §10.7. |
| D-798 | The reaction prompt is given `what`, what is happening as the character perceives it (the window's source, named for the character, and the name of the action that opened it); and the `core_ui` mod carries the shows of combat and powers (`strike_landed`, `strike_missed`, `strike_stopped`, `staggered`, `ability_used`, `ability_refused`), since the `ui` mod loads without the simulation's events. | Design, from the application's third pass | A prompt that says only that the character can react does not let the player choose; a candidate noticed the window only by perceiving its source (D-615), so naming it reveals nothing. Without shows a blow was played back in silence. | 12 §3.2, §9.5, §7.3. |
| D-799 | Talking: the control `talk` (shift+t) and the context menu ask the application for a new service, `talk { to }`, then open `ui:talk`. The application raises the hook `talk_options` (`social::talk_options`) on a copy of the simulation, which it then drops, and keeps the options for `app.talk` (`to`, `options` each with `id`, `text`, `kind`, `lines` and `says`, and `why`, the reason nothing can be said: someone not perceived now, or the hook's refusal). The screen lists them and says the one chosen with a new action of the `core` mod, `say { to, kind, lines? }`, whose phase runs the verb `say`. The `core` mod offers a greeting and a warning to anyone (`offer_greeting`, `offer_warning`) and greets back a greeting addressed to someone a player does not decide for, at most every half minute (`greet_back`, in the group `social_life`). The `core_ui` mod logs speech: `heard_said`, the line a character of `party()` heard as it was worded for it, and `said_to`, that one of them spoke to someone, without the words. | Design, from the application's third pass, after the social layer | `talk_options` takes `&mut Sim` because raising a hook stamps an event; raised on the game itself outside a command, it would change state the input log cannot replay, and a property cannot raise it. A copy costs one clone of the simulation per screen opened. A command goes through an action so that speaking takes time and refusals come back as any action's. The party sees every hearing with one of its own among its subjects, and a show's condition has no reader (05 §4.6), so `party()`, safe for belief, tells whose hearing it is; the words a character said were worded for the one who heard them, and showing them would name the speaker as the hearer knows them (invariant V9). | 12 §10.4, §10.5, §10.7; 15 §11. `clash_app::app::gather_talk`; `AppService::Talk`, `AppState::talk`; `mods/core/data/talk.json`. |
| D-820 | A device keeps a mode (one of its definition's `modes`, the first to start with), the settings its user set (`config`, by `set_config`, which raises `device_configured`) and the levels of its `stores` (filled by `add_resource`). `when` is a list of modes on `speed`, `auto` and each entry of `provides`. Until networks exist (G3) a station draws what its recipes' `while` wants from its own stores, and a store with a `rate` gives no more than that a second, so a machine that wants more works at the share it gets. A device is as made, and may be demoted, only with its first mode, nothing set, every store at its start and no recipe under way. Passive `load` and `when_supplied` are not read: the promotion rule already gives "no entity until one diverges". | Design, from building the device model | 16 §4.2 says "fields that say `when` apply in those modes" without naming the fields, and stations need a cold oven that does not bake, a still that runs only lit, and fuel without a power grid. Stores are what G3 balances; drawing straight on them is the network of one device and is replaced, not undone, by G3. | 16 §4.3 as built; package G2 is `beta`. The `Device` component gained three fields; the save layout is version 16 (15 was combat 2's, merged beside this). |
| D-821 | A recipe under way is a `Process` component of the station's device, or of the worker for work by hand. Its work done is recorded at a moment of the own time of the worker while one works, else of the station, and it has one wake-up in the `Stepper` domain: the earliest of its completion at its present rate, a store it draws on running dry, and a step (`check_every`, else the setting `recipe.step`) for a recipe whose rate reads what changes unannounced (a `rate` expression, a condition on the place) or that `emits`. Changes of the station's mode, settings and stores, and the worker stopping, bring it up to date at the old rate and schedule it afresh. | Design, from building recipes | 16 §8.2 has one scheduled wake-up moved whenever the rate changes, and work kept when it is interrupted. A rate that reads skill or light cannot announce its changes until reactive expressions serve more than abilities and traits; stepping only those keeps invariant G7 for the rest. Own time follows clocks that change rate, as pools' do. | 16 §8.6. The `Stepper` domain had no handler and is recipes'. |
| D-822 | An action whose parameters are named `recipe` and `station` is a work action, as one named `target` and `interaction` is an interaction's (D-622): a handler of `action_starting` refuses it with the reason the recipe cannot be started for. Its held phase calls the verb `work`; the recipe's completion lets that phase go (`cancel_action` without abandoning), and the end of the action by any other way stops the work and keeps what was done. `recipe` takes an id or a qualified id as a text, because a test script cannot write an id literal in an expression. | Design, from building work | 16 §8.2 has work done "for as long as their work action continues", and a held phase is what lasts until let go. The engine may not name `core:make`. | `core:make` in `mods/core/data/making/actions.json`. |
| D-823 | Inputs and tools are looked for in what the worker holds at any depth, in the station's device, and in the contents of devices and items that hold things within `recipe.supply_range` cells (default 6) on the same storey of where the work is done; a latent container is passed by and nothing is opened. A machine alone looks in its own holds. What a character is told is worked out through the seam `recipe::supply::belief`, which reads truth until perception keeps what characters know of containers. Outputs go into the station's holds where they fit, by hand into the worker's grasp where it can take them, else where the work is done; substances into containers at hand with room, which must be there when it starts. | Design, from building supply | 16 §8.3 lists the order and the range but not the storey, the shape of the range or where outputs go. A worker that knows what is in its site's stores needs belief of contents, which perception does not keep yet. | 16 §8.6. `recipe_options` and `can_make` read through the seam. |
| D-824 | A recipe's `while` is a map of resource rates or a condition on the place; its know-how is a list, `know_how`, beside the condition `needs` with its `fail_text`; `repairs` is `{ tag, share }` and mends the most damaged item at hand with the tag; `check_every` sets its step. A station is an element or a device whose definition carries a tag of `at`, within a step of the worker. | Design, from building recipes | 16 §8.1 writes `needs: knows_how('smelting')`, which cannot say which know-how is missing, nor be met by a station; a list can, with the reason `recipe.unknown_how`. 16 §8.4 has `while` a condition for growing. | 16 §8.1, §8.6. |
| D-825 | What a mind knows how to do is the `KnowHow` component of the entity it is in, moved by `transfer_mind` with the mind (the body left keeps none) and dropped with it by `remove_mind`. A template's `know_how` is given when `entity_spawned` is raised, without asking what each needs; `teach` asks (`know_how.needs`), `learn_from` teaches an item's `teaches`, and `unlearn` takes one away. At a station, what its definition `provides` in its mode and what the items in it `provide` count as known; a machine alone has only those. | Design, from building know-how | 16 §8.5 says the set is held by a mind and goes where it goes, and the `Mind` component holds no more than its profile and state; a component of its own changes no other hash. A template's list is the author's to keep in order. | 16 §8.6; 06 §10.4. One line in `Sim::transfer_mind`. |
| D-826 | What an item holds of substances, and what is coated on it, is its `Substances` component, by member, in cubic metres; equal items stack only when they hold the same, and `split` copies it. `filled` fills an item when it is made; one filled with no `contains` holds what it was filled with, so a loaf is bread. A dose adds per millilitre that gets in, the route's factor times the volume; each pool goes through `exposure_incoming` with the substance's id as `field`; `traits` are given for a time. Eating whole is the verb `take_in`, because `consume` is a reserved effect. | Design, from building substances | 16 §9.5 names `contains` and `filled` but not where amounts live, how a stack holds them or how food that is not a container is eaten. The hook 16 §9.5 names for every route is the world's, whose fields fit a dose by a route. | 16 §9.5 as built. The person template is given three pools: `nourishment`, `toxin` and `sedation`. |
| D-827 | A coating is delivered where a hit an item lands on a body is resolved (07 §3.4, step 6): "the outermost living layer" is the outermost layer of the body's own part it struck, so that what is worn does not count; `share` goes in by `inject` when the blow got through it, and every hit, breaching or not, then wipes `wipes` of what is left. | Design, from building coatings | 06 §7.2 has no notion of a living layer; a body's own layers are its living ones and a worn layer is not. Wiping on every hit keeps a blade from poisoning without end. | One call in `combat::strike`. Shots and thrown items deliver no coating yet. |
| D-828 | The bundled making is in `mods/core/data/making/` and `substances/`: stations (workbench, oven, hearth, still, a rack, a herb bed), recipes (bandage, torch, club, arrows, bread, healing salve, nightshade extract, sleeping draught, mending a blade, distilling, growing herbs), the know-how `herbalism` and a herbal that teaches and provides it, drinks and food, and the actions `make`, `eat`, `drink` and `apply`. The town's maps are not changed: maps do not place items yet, and a new map tagged `shop` would change every town. The make and know-how screens are `ui:make` and `ui:know_how`, opened by controls the default keymap does not bind, because a patch to it from `making.json` would load before `theme.json`. A new fixture `rack` holds things instead of a patch to `shelf`, for the same reason. | Design, from writing the content | Data only, and nothing changed that other packages own; what cannot be done additively is said. | Where stations belong: an oven and racks in a bakery, a still and a hearth in a tavern, a workbench in a house's yard; the application's keymap should bind `make` and `know_how`. |
| D-829 | Every golden hash of the test scripts changed: three component stores were added (`process`, `know_how`, `substances`), the `Device` component gained three fields, and the person template three pools. The save layout is version 16 (combat 2 took 15). | Design, from building making | A new store hashes its length even when empty, and the save layout follows the components. | Scripts re-blessed; four new ones under `scenarios/making/`. |
| D-800 | What a definition type's fields are is read from the type's own `Deserialize` by a probing deserialiser, `clash_data::shape::describe`: it answers every request with a harmless value and records what it was asked (the fields of each struct, the values of each enum, the reserved names of `Ref`, `Inline`, `Tag`, the quantities, `Located`, `Gated`, `by_kind` and `by_site`), reading the type again with each field left out to see which are required, with each value of each enum, and with each kind of JSON a field of several shapes may take. `DefTypes::shapes`, `defaults` and `builtins` give it per registered type. | Design, from building the reference (P5) | 04 §13 and M6 require the schemas and the reference to come from the registries the loader uses; a hand-written field list would drift, and a type's `Deserialize` is exactly what the loader reads. No new dependency, no change to any definition type. | 04 §9, §13. Additive to `clash-data`. A type whose `Deserialize` is written by hand is described by the kinds of JSON it takes and what it says it expects; a value the probe's made-up input cannot satisfy is described as far as the reading got. The Rust type's path keys documentation and is never printed. |
| D-801 | What a field means is its Rust doc comment. `clash-tools/build.rs` reads the sources of every crate of the workspace and writes, for each struct and enum read from JSON (derived, written by hand, or given its reading by a macro), its documentation and that of each field and value under its JSON name (`rename`, `rename_all`, `alias` and `skip` applied, fields a macro adds to a struct written inside it included). The reference joins it to the shapes of D-800 by the type's path. Every field and value a definition type reaches, every object and every field type of several shapes must be documented, as must every kind, property, function, event, hook, verb, query, effect, setting and reason; a test fails, listing what is not. | Design, from building the reference (P5) | Doc comments do not reach a running program, and copying them into tables would duplicate every field's documentation. The sources are part of the workspace the tools are built from. | 04 §13. A field documented only in its specification needs a doc comment too; private reading structs (`…Object`) are documented like public ones. A type written by a macro in another form is reported as undocumented. |
| D-802 | The reference is committed in `docs/reference/`, generated by `cargo run -p clash-tools -- docs --out docs/reference`: an index, one page per definition type, and pages of values, units, kinds and properties, functions, events, hooks, verbs, effects, queries, settings, reasons and diagnostic codes. A test of `clash-tools` fails when the committed pages differ from what the command writes, so a package that registers something runs the command and commits the pages. `--format json` writes the same as one document, which is not committed. | Design, from building the reference (P5) | The reference must not drift from the engine (04 §13: "a feature that is not in the generated reference does not exist"), and a reader of the repository should not have to build it. Markdown keeps it small and readable; content of the bundled mods is not dumped. | 04 §13; 14 §9.2. The in-game reference browser (TL9) is not built. |
| D-803 | The schemas are JSON Schema 2020-12, using only `type`, `enum`, `const`, `properties`, `additionalProperties`, `required`, `items`, `prefixItems`, `minItems`, `maxItems`, `minimum`, `pattern`, `propertyNames`, `anyOf`, `allOf`, `if`/`then`/`else`, `not` and `$ref`. A definition that patches, copies from another or is abstract is checked against a variant with nothing required; a quantity is a number or a string matching its dimension's units; with `--with-ids` a reference lists every qualified id of its type and each bare name. The tests check them with a validator of that subset written in the tests, without a new dependency: the bundled mods pass, and every mistake within one definition of the loader's broken fixture is refused. | Design, from building schemas (P5) | Editors need standard schemas; the subset is what every common validator implements. Whole-set mistakes (duplicate ids, inheritance cycles), expressions and the world's and the interface's references, which are plain strings resolved when their tables are built, are beyond a schema and stay the loader's. | 04 §13. `clash-tools schema [--type T] [--out DIR] [--with-ids]`. |
| D-804 | The simulation lists its settings and reasons for the reference in two functions, `clash_sim::engine_settings` (name, default, documentation) and `clash_sim::engine_reasons` (code, documentation, and for the action system's the values and the sentence), beside `engine_verbs` and `engine_properties`; a package that reads a setting or adds reasons adds its table there. | Design, from building the reference (P5) | Settings were constants read with a default where they are used, and reasons were tables per package with no list of them all; the reference needs one place to read each, without listing them in the tools. | 04 §10; 14 §5. Additive to `clash-sim`. |
| D-805 | `init-mod <id> [--dir <mods dir>]` makes a mod that lints clean as it is: a `mod.json` requiring `core` and `ui`, a small commented example under `data/` of an item, a creature template, an action, a variable with a rule, and a part put into a slot of the interface; a profile in the mod's `profiles/` that is the repository's default profile with the mod added; and `.vscode/settings.json` attaching `.schemas/data.schema.json`, written beside it. `new <type> <id>` prints a definition of a type with every required field holding a value to replace, and names the optional ones. | Design, from building scaffolding (P5) | 14 §9.1 begins writing a mod with `init-mod`; a mod that does not load as made teaches nothing. Files and directories starting with a dot are not read (04 §3.1), so the editor settings and the schema do not disturb the load. Taking the profile from the repository's own keeps the interface's screen roles current. | 04 §13; 14 §9.1. The examples name content of `core` and `ui`, which a total conversion replaces. |
| D-806 | `refs` reads a finished definition beside what its type reads (D-800): a string a field reads as a reference is resolved as the loader resolves it (the writing mod's namespace, its imports, `engine`), `copy_from` and patches are read from the files, which keep them. A string read as plain text that holds a qualified id, or is a name that would resolve, is listed apart as *named in plain text*: the world's and the interface's references are such strings, resolved by their own tables (D-803), and so are names in expressions and effects; some are something else of the same name. `find <type> [where=<condition>]... [<text>]...` tests the same walk: `path`, `!path` or `path<op>value` with `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, `~`, lists and maps looked through, quantities compared in SI units, references by the id they resolve to; `*` is any type; words are looked for in ids, `name`, `description` and `doc`. | Design, from building the mod authoring tools (TL9) | 14 §9.2 names the commands and not their reading. What a type reads is already known exactly, so a reference needs no list of fields; what is a plain string to the loader cannot be told apart from text, so it is shown, not hidden or claimed. | 14 §9.2. Values a later patch replaced are not seen. When the world reads references through the loader, its strings move from the second list to the first. |
| D-807 | The modding guide, `docs/modding-guide.md`, is run by a test of `clash-tools`. Read in order, a fenced block whose info string names a path after its language (```` ```json mods/hearth/data/kitchen.json ````) is a file, written there; each line of a `bash` block is a command. Each `cargo run -p clash-tools --` line is run as the tools run it, against a copy of the bundled mods in a directory of its own, with `mods` in its arguments meaning that directory and `--mods` (`--dir` for `init-mod`) added where the command takes it, and must exit 0. The game (`cargo run -p clash-app`) and the world viewer fed through a pipe wait for a person, so only what they would be given is checked: the profile named, the file piped. Any other line fails the test. Every relative link of the guide must lead to a file, and to an anchor in it where it names one. The guide's test script ends in `expect hash` with no value and the guide runs it with `--bless` first, so no golden hash of the guide has to be kept in step with the engine. | Design, from writing the guide (P5) | A walkthrough that is not run goes stale as soon as a field is renamed; running it keeps every file and command in it working, and the links keep it tied to the generated reference. Marking files in the info string keeps the guide plain Markdown that renders as usual. | 04 §13; 14 §9.1. The game's own screens are not driven by the test. |
| D-830 | The window frontend draws on the processor: the grid, sprites and frame effects are drawn into a pixel buffer from a glyph atlas (`fontdue`), and `softbuffer` shows it in a `winit` window. `softbuffer` joins the allowed dependencies for the frontend crate; `wgpu` stays allowed only if drawing ever moves to the graphics card. | Owner | A tile grid of glyphs needs nothing a graphics card offers that the processor cannot do within a frame, and `wgpu` is a large general layer over every graphics interface, with its own shader compiler, that is slow to build and heavy on disk. Drawn on the processor, a frame can be written to an image, so the window is tested headless, where no screen or graphics card exists. | 01 §13; 12 §11, §15; U3. Supersedes the `wgpu` choice of 01 §13 for the window frontend. |
| D-831 | Debug and test builds strip symbols (`strip = "symbols"` in `[profile.dev]` and `[profile.test]`), as they already leave out debug information. | Design, from running many agents' builds on one disk | Symbols are about forty per cent of each test binary (136 MB to 80 MB for the largest), and every copy of the workspace holds some eighty test binaries; the disk filling was stopping builds. A panic still names its file and line; only a backtrace loses function names, and `CARGO_PROFILE_DEV_STRIP=none CARGO_PROFILE_TEST_STRIP=none` brings them back for one build. | The root `Cargo.toml`. Release builds are unchanged. |
| D-810 | Shots meet reactions: `shoot` and `throw` take `window`, the id of a `window_kind`, and open one as the shot leaves (`Sim::open_window`), named for the entity aimed at or, for a shot at a cell, for those with bodies standing there; none for an instant shot or one along a direction. It stays open for the shot's speed over the distance to the aim point and closes then, or once nothing it was opened for is in flight. `RangedState` keeps each shot's window (hashed only when there is one). `mods/core` gives the kind `shot` (`2 / wits` seconds, reach 3 m) and has its `shoot`, `aim_shot`, `draw_and_loose` and `throw` open one. | Design, from building combat 2 | 07 §11.2 said the window closes when the shot arrives; the simulation knows only its estimate until the world reports a strike. The bundled actions aim at a cell the shooter believes in (D-749): who is offered a window is the simulation's matter, not the shooter's belief. `window` as `any` read a bare `core:shot` as a name. | 07 §11.6, §11.7; `combat::ranged::shoot`. The scripts `bullet_time` and `caught_arrow`. |
| D-811 | Defences meet shots: before a shot strikes a creature, in flight or by ray, the defences in force for it are tried as at a strike's step 5, the shooter as attacker, `action` the projectile's id and `attack` `shot`. One that avoids lets it fly by at full speed; one that interposes turns its share aside and each surface it puts in the way takes a packet at the projectile's own intensity over its contact; what passes goes into the body. | Design, from building combat 2 | A blow is spread by what is put in its way (D-712) because a face meets a face; a projectile's point meets the shield as it meets a wall, and spreading a bullet over a buckler's face would make wood bulletproof. | 07 §5.5, §11.6; `combat::strike::guard_takes` (`spread`), `ranged::flight::strike_guarded`. |
| D-812 | Grips are plain state (`GripTable`, saved, hashed once anything is gripped; save layout 14). `seize` is a verb that belongs to the action whose list runs it: the runtime marks the running action (`Sim::acting`, not state) while it runs a list. A grab is an attack with no energy whose `on_land` seizes, so it is resolved like a strike; a strike with no energy is now resolved against the target named instead of ending before it. A grip ends with its action, for any reason; one ended otherwise (broken, released from outside the action, thrown, lost) interrupts the action. The hook `seize_resolving` may change the part and the pin, or cancel. | Design, from building C7 | 07 §9.1 tied a grip to "the holder's gripping action" without saying how a verb knows it; making `seize` an effect of the rule compiler as `strike` is would be a change to the compiler for one verb. C7 says "exactly as long": a grip that ends early must end its action too, or the action would hold nothing. | 07 §9.4, §17 (C7); `combat::grip`, `action::run` (`acting`, `release`). |
| D-813 | While a grip lasts: `pins` disables the channel slots of the part held and the parts on it, and gives them back; a step of the held to a cell not beside its holder is refused (`combat.gripped`), and one of the holder dragging more than `drags` (`combat.too_heavy`), movement asking `grip::may_step`; when the holder steps the held is moved into the cell left. An attack `with` `{ "gripped": true }` strikes with a surface of a part of the first creature gripped, which takes the recoil; a defence's puts the creature in the way whole, struck with the blow's own surface. | Design, from building C7 | 07 §9.1 named pinning, dragging and the shield without saying which slots a part pins, where the dragged go, or what surface a body presents to a blow it shields. The smallest change to movement is one call in a step. | 07 §3.1, §5.5, §9.4; `movement::step`. Carrying (a creature taken into a grasp) and holding the world are not built. |
| D-814 | `throw` of a creature the thrower grips ends the grip and launches the held as a body (the launch a push uses, `push::launch_body`) from the middle of its body, at `speed` or `√(2 E / m)`, toward the aim, or away from the thrower on the level when aimed at itself. `ThrowSpec` keeps its fields; `throw_opening` takes the window. | Design, from building C7 | D-747 said C7's throw extends the same verb; adding a field to `ThrowSpec` would break its literals. A person thrown is a body the world carries as a push's is. | 07 §9.4, §11.7. A thrown person breaks a window only with the energy the glass's threshold asks: the script `thrown_through` uses strength 1500. |
| D-815 | `strike_preview(who, action, target, part?, effort?, attack?)` gives a map: `in_reach`, `odds` (the aim contest's `odds`, 1 without aim), `odds_near` (within `near`), `energy`, `intensity`, `push_speed`, `staggers`, `launches`. It evaluates the attack's numbers through a host with no stream, so it draws nothing and changes nothing (C5). It reads the target as it is and is not safe for belief. `depth`, `disables`, `severs`, `passes`, `recoil` and dry-run handlers are not built. | Design, from building C11 | What a body would do needs a dry run of the body model over a copy of the target, which the function interface (a `&Sim`) does not allow cheaply; the odds and the push are what an application shows first. | 07 §13; `combat::preview`. |
| D-816 | Joint actions as built (T6 beta): roles after the first must be `forced` (`action.role_not_offered` otherwise; offering is not built); each claims slots on its participant, `all` meaning every enabled slot, possibly none; the participant's own actions holding them are interrupted (`action.taken_over`); the action is entered among the participant's actions, its slots kept in `ActionTable::joined` (hashed only while one runs); ending by any path frees everyone; a forced participant whose mind stops does not end it. An action's `cutscene` raises `cutscene_cued` as it begins, once `requires_at_start` held. `entity.helpless` is whether a mind is not awake or a pin holds it. | Design, from building C8 and T6 | A takedown needs the victim locked in and defenceless; asking a victim's consent is not part of it. A victim knocked out by the takedown's own blow would otherwise end it before it settles. | 03 §6.1, §8; 07 §10; `action::run`, `action::state`, `combat::takedown`. |
| D-817 | Stances, default part and effort, stagger and exertion are content: the traits `aggressive` and `defensive` of the group `fighting_stance`, the action `take_stance`, `auto_part()` (null) and `auto_effort(guarded)` as the bundled blows' defaults, the actor passing `self.has_tag('defensive')`; the rule `stagger_breaks_blow`; `exertion` and `winded` in `data/exertion.json` beside the `stamina` pool. | Design, from building C11 and C12 | A content function reading another entity's tags is not safe for belief, so a default may not call `auto_effort(self, …)` that reads them; the actor's own tag is. Tests that load `combat/` without `traits.json` could not resolve `stamina`. | 07 §12. The bundled defaults are unchanged for anyone in no stance. |
| D-818 | Catching is the verb `catch_shot { who?, window, reach? }`: the first of what the window was opened for still in flight within `reach` (1.5 m) of the catcher's middle is arrested where it is; an item thrown goes into the hand, a projectile becomes the item its new field `item` names. The bundled `catch` reaction tries at each 5 ms tick, its contest drawn once. The `arrow` projectile names the `arrow` item, which is given a one-handed grip. | Design, from building combat 2 | 07 §11.6 named `arrest` on a contest; a reaction that arrested at once would snatch a shot metres away. A projectile is not an item: the definition says what one becomes. | 07 §11.6, §11.7. `intercept` is not built. |
| D-819 | The bundled `parry` requires `self.wielding_surface('flat') != null`, saying "There is nothing in hand to parry with.", and puts in the way the flat of anything held (`held: ""`), a club as well as a knife; `entity.wielding_surface(surface)` is the smallest property `requires` needed. The guard mind gains the goal `apprehend` (a grab within two metres) by an optional patch. | Design, from building combat 2 | D-716 left `parry` listed for the unarmed and doing nothing, because nothing let a reaction require a held blade. | 07 §16.1, §12; `combat::defence::wielding_surface`; `mods/core/data/combat/reactions.json`, `grips.json`. |
| D-840 | The window frontend draws as built (U3): a cell is the theme font's `size` in logical pixels high, times the screen's scale; the font is scaled so that the top of its ascent, rounded up to a whole pixel, is the top of the cell and its line runs past the bottom by less than a pixel, which is cut off; a cell is as wide as the advance of `M` at that scale, rounded down. Bold, italic and underline are made from the one font file (strokes thickened sideways, rows slanted, a line under the baseline); the line-drawing characters of one weight (light, heavy, double: lines, corners, tees, crosses) and the half and full blocks are drawn in whole pixels by the frontend, not taken from the font; a character the font lacks is a hollow box; a control character is nothing. Each cell's background and glyph are written in one pass; sprites are centred on their position in cells, at their scale, at their opacity, by layer; the tint and the flash wash every colour toward theirs; a shake of 1 moves the picture half a cell, in a pattern of eight directions taken in turn with each frame drawn; the zoom magnifies by nearest pixel about the grid's centre, between a quarter and eight times; the bars are black, half at each edge. The grid is centred, with the theme's background round it. | Design | A ceiled ascent puts the baseline on a pixel's edge, where the rasteriser puts it, so the font's own lines fill the cell and meet the next one without a seam; the line-drawing characters drawn in whole pixels are crisp where the font's would be anti-aliased across two rows, as terminals draw them. One font file per theme keeps the theme's `font` as 12 §4.3 has it. Washing colours is the same arithmetic as washing pixels and costs nothing per pixel. A frame does not say where its camera is, and the views centre it, so the grid's centre is where a zoom is about. | 12 §4.3, §11, §15; U3. A theme cannot name a bold or italic font file. A shake's direction turns with frames drawn, not with time. |
| D-841 | `clash-tools` reaches `clash-front-window` for the window frontend's drawing benchmark, built without the crate's `window` feature, so that the renderer is measured with no window system compiled. The feature is on by default and the application has it. | Design | The budget table's rows are measured by `clash-tools bench` (D-730), and a release build of the window system's crates for a benchmark that never opens a window would cost minutes and disk for nothing. | 01 §3 gains the arrow; 12 §15; `budgets.toml`. |
| D-842 | Input in the window: keys get the terminal's names and modifiers; Control-C is an ordinary key, since a window is left by closing it, which is `Close`; key releases and the window system's synthetic presses on gaining focus are dropped; gaining or losing focus is no input event, and losing it forgets the modifiers held; text arrives only from an input method's commit or a key that types more than one character, and there is no paste, which would need a clipboard dependency; the mouse is reported with the cell under the pointer as the last picture laid it out, zoom included, a move only when the cell changes and nothing off the grid; the wheel is counted in notches, and a touchpad's smooth scrolling is added up a cell's height to the notch. | Design | 12 §9.1 names the events a frontend delivers and has none for focus. A player must always be able to leave, which the close button gives a window. Reporting every pixel the pointer moves would flood the scene with moves to the same cell. | 12 §9.1, §11; U3. |
| D-843 | The frontend is chosen at start: `--window`, `--terminal`, or with neither a window when a display is available (on Linux and the BSDs, `WAYLAND_DISPLAY`, `WAYLAND_SOCKET` or `DISPLAY` set; elsewhere always). A window that cannot be opened is said on standard error with the reason, a missing or unreadable font named by its path, and the game runs in the terminal. A panic inside the window system's libraries while the window is opened (on X11, when `libxkbcommon-x11` is not installed, `winit` panics) is caught, kept quiet and taken as such a reason. | Owner | D-002 makes the window the primary frontend, and a game started from a desktop must open one; a game started where there is no display, or no working window system, must still run. The window system's libraries are loaded when the program runs, so their absence shows only then. | 12 §3.1, §11; U3, U11. |
| D-844 | A theme's `font.file` resolves inside the directory of the mod whose file wrote the theme's `font`, by the source of that value in the finished definition (04 §15); with no such source, the mod of the theme's namespace. The bundled themes name DejaVu Sans Mono, bundled as `mods/ui/assets/font.ttf` with its licence beside it, at a size of 16. The window keeps the font of the theme in force when it opened: a theme chosen during play changes the colours and not the font. | Design | A mod that patches a theme with a font of its own ships the file in its own directory. DejaVu Sans Mono's licence (Bitstream Vera, with the DejaVu changes in the public domain) allows it to be bundled unchanged, and it covers every character the bundled screens draw. Changing the font during play needs the application to tell the frontend, which a frontend has no call for yet. | 12 §4.3, §13; U3. |
| D-760 | A persona is `PersonaId`, an owner entity and a number (0 the true persona), written as text `7v1/0`; an entity presents its true persona, one of its own it made, or a copy of what another presents. A mind takes an entity for what it decided at its last detection while the entity presents the same, else for what it presents followed through the links it holds; a mind takes itself for its true persona. `identity_check` runs only at a detection of an entity presenting a persona not its true one and not linked by the observer; with no handler the disguise holds. Seen through, an own made persona is linked to the true one in that observer for good; a copy of another's persona is never linked, and is checked again at the next detection. Whoever perceives an entity when it changes persona keeps taking it for whom it took it for. Rules keep naming entities: belief properties, relations and `perceived` translate them. | Design, from building personas (S6) | 15 §8.2 said the observer "learns that the two are the same", which for a stolen face would make the observer take the real owner for the thief ever after. Keeping `about` and the properties on entities kept every rule and property of D-615 and D-616 as it was, and kept invariant P2 in the storage. | 15 §6, §7, §8; `clash_sim::social::persona`. `perception::belief::persona_of` asks the social layer. |
| D-761 | Persona, track, memory and group are not subject kinds yet: a persona is text, a group a key (a definition's qualified id, or `def#n` for one made in play), and what a mind holds is read through properties of the entity read of itself (`persona_of`, `rel`, `remembers_actor`, `memory_source`), none marked safe for belief, as D-616's. | Design, from building the social layer (S6 to S8) | A new subject kind changes the kernel's `Subject`, which every crate matches; the beta needs the behaviour, not the kinds. Text keys compare and print, and a definition's group is its own key. | 15 §15; 05 §5.1. Adding the four kinds later replaces the text with handles. |
| D-762 | A relation is held by a mind toward a persona or a group, of a kind, with the value it was set to and when in the holder's own time (a group's in universal time); fade is worked out when read; at its default it is not held. Read: the mind's own toward the persona; else the strongest of its own toward the groups the persona claims; else the strongest of what its groups hold (each group's toward the persona, else the strongest of its toward the groups claimed, its own before its definition's); else the default. The strongest is furthest from the default, first in key order of equals. Group members are entities; a true persona claims its owner's open memberships. `stance_to` compares the kinds flagged `opposes` and `sides_with`. | Design, from building relations and groups (S7) | 15 §9 said a member with nothing of its own uses "its group's" without saying which of several, and kept members by persona, which a secret identity would have split. The first group by key let a watchman's townsfolk friendship toward the watch hide the watch's hostility to the gang. | 15 §9; 09 §3.4, §6.6. The AI (D-694) reads relations once a mind has perceived someone: hostile where `stance_to` is `opposes`, feared where it holds a kind flagged `avoids`, an addition to 15's four flags; the tags it reads are those of the persona it takes them for. Its own stances and its definition's tags stay beside them. |
| D-763 | An order is obeyed when the one it is given to, having heard it and made out who spoke, holds toward the persona it takes the giver for a relation of a kind flagged `orders` above its default; `order_received` may change that. Kept orders raise `order_given` on the receiver; the bundled rule `core:do_as_ordered` plans the action until the AI's goal does. `until` ends an order without a wake-up: it no longer stands, and is dropped when the orders next change. A kind flagged `orders` falling to its default withdraws the orders kept from that giver. | Design, from building orders (S7) | 15 §10 had the master hold `commands` toward the servant, which the servant cannot perceive: obedience read from the hearer's own relation is belief, not truth, and lets a forged voice command. A wake-up per order with a time limit would cost timeline entries for what nothing reads until the orders change. | 15 §10; 09 §7.4, §8.1. Test script `minion_orders`. |
| D-764 | Speech is a message carried by a signal (`Signal::message`): `say` gives off the message kind's signal kind (the `noise` kind if none) from the speaker's eye height, about the speaker; whoever perceives it, if `understands` lets it, gets `message_heard`. A report carries the speaker's memories and track as held when spoken; whom it is addressed to gains them as told at `belief` (times the `trust` share) of the speaker's confidence, marked with the persona it took the speaker for (`Memory::source`, `Track::source`). Lines are picked when said, from the stream `social`, and written per hearer. | Design, from building speech (S8) | A message that travels with its signal is delayed, muffled and overheard by the same physics as any sound, and a save in mid-air keeps it. A report taken when it is heard would read the speaker's beliefs at a later time. | 15 §11; 05 §4.6. `Memory` gained the public field `source`. |
| D-765 | Group knowledge: `report_to_group` adds a member's memories (of an event type, or naming whom it takes `about` for) and its track of `about` to the group and tells them at once to every other member, as told by the reporter as each takes them, at the reporter's confidence; a member who joins later is told what the group knows. | Design, from building group knowledge (S7, S8) | D-240 made group knowledge count as told to every member when added; who joins later was left open, and a new guard who knows nothing of the wanted man is the surprise nobody wrote. | 15 §9.2, §11. |
| D-766 | The reader lens every simulation is bound with is the social one: someone (with a mind, or presenting another's persona) is called by the name of the persona the reader takes them for if the reader knows it (`learn_name`, or its own), else described by the name of the template that persona looks like (`the person`), else `someone`; anything else by its own name. Names are learnt only by `learn_name` for now. | Design, from building personas (S6) | 05 §4.6 asked for "a known name, or a description of what they made out"; with no features yet (15 §8.4) the template is what is made out. | 05 §4.6; 15 §13. |
| D-767 | Templates name the groups a thing made from them joins (`groups`: group to role), joined when `entity_spawned` is delivered; an unknown group is a load warning and skipped, so content loaded without its groups still loads. `core:person` joins `core:townsfolk`. Perception's `attach_sense` and `detach_sense` are verbs, so that rules can give and take senses in play (a blinding, a gift of second sight) beside what templates and minds give (D-696). | Design, from the bundled content (S7) | The brief asked for the town's people to have groups; settlements spawn `person`. Nothing let a rule take a sense away. | 15 §4, §9.2, §15; 06 §10.1. |
| D-768 | The social state is part of the kernel's hash and the simulation's blob (save layout 18, after making's 17); an empty one hashes as nothing, and a track, a memory or a signal with no source or message hashes as before. Every golden hash was blessed again, because the bundled content changed (the `social_life` rule group, `person`'s group). | Design | Hash churn in every branch is the cost of content; keeping what is unused out of the hash keeps the cost to content. | 01 §4; `scenarios/`. |
| D-769 | Left out of the social layer for now: features and descriptions (15 §8.4: `appearance_feature`, `suspects`, test script `a_tall_figure_in_red`), pronoun sets, exchanges (joint actions whose phases say lines), the `talk` interaction, collectives (09 §8.1), group variables, the role permission `orders`, and the queries `members` and `suspects`. Windows ask `social::intervenes`, which every simulation is bound with (`Sim::set_intervenes`). | Design | A working subset with tests over a complete design without them (plans/beta.md). | 15 §8.4, §9, §11; plans/first-playable.md S6 to S8. |
| D-850 | What a placed map furnishes its cells with is handed on as spawn requests beside its creatures: one for each `items` of a cell spec, of the kind `item` or `table`, with `count` (the items or the draws the map drew at resolution, after its `chance`) and `seed`, a positional hash of the placement's seed, the domain `gen.place.items`, the cell and the request's place among those of its cell; and one of the kind `device` for each device a cell spec gives settings, with `config`. Every request carries `map`. The world hands them on once, by the tract their cell is in, the first time it loads, as it does creatures; the simulation answers them without `spawn_requested`. A device a map only names is not handed on. | Design, from building furnishing | 10 §4.1 carried items and devices in the placed map for the packages that own them. A spawn request is already the one way generation asks the simulation to put something somewhere once, by the tract the cell is in, so it needs no new type in the world (01 R4) and nothing is made twice whatever order tracts load in. A seed from the placement and the cell makes the same seed give the same things without a stream of the simulation (01 §4). | 10 §3.2, §4.1; `clash_gen::place::PlacedMap::furnishings`; `clash_sim::furnish`. Names of devices wait for links (16 §6). |
| D-851 | Items a map puts in a cell whose element is a container (its device `holds` things) are that element's **latent contents**, kept by the simulation by element (cell and layer) with the definition of the element they belong to, saved and hashed with it, and made the first time the element becomes a device for whatever reason (opened, something put in, someone hiding, a recipe started, `promote`), when `materialise_contents` is called on it, or when a packet damages it; they are then stowed in the device and `contents_materialised` is raised. If another element, or none, stands there first, they spill where something dropped into the cell comes to rest, as a device's contents do. Anywhere else a map's items are made lying in the cell when the tract first loads. | Design, from building furnishing | 06 §9.3 asks that a city of unopened drawers cost nothing and that the same drawer always hold the same things; an element is not an entity until it is promoted (16 §4.3), so its latent contents cannot be kept on one. Keeping them by element, out of the tract's entities, keeps them whatever loads and unloads. | 06 §9.3; `element.latent`; `materialise_contents` takes an element. Supersedes the part of D-564 that said `content_table` is not built. |
| D-852 | The `content_table` type: `entries`, each exactly one of `item`, `table` and `none`, with `weight` (above zero), `count` (a number or `[least, most]`: items, or draws from a nested table) and `chance`; `each` takes every entry on a draw instead of picking one by weight. A draw makes every random number it could use whether or not it matters, from a generator seeded by the seed alone; nesting is cut at eight. A table that nests itself is a load error, and a map whose `items` names neither an item nor a table is a load error at the cell spec, with its file, line and path. | Design, from building furnishing | Shared tables of contents were waiting for generation (D-564). Weighted picks, independent rolls and nesting cover a drawer, a rack and a street's litter without an engine notion of any of them. | 06 §9.3; `clash_sim::item::table`. An item's own `contents` still names items only. |
| D-853 | `store` and `retrieve` work on a container without states (a rack, a bench, a stall) as on an open one, and `retrieve` first makes latent contents. Container furniture is content: the fixtures `chest`, `cupboard`, `wardrobe` (which hides a person) and `stall` in `world/stores.json`, their devices in `containers.json`, beside the locker's, so that a load of `world/` alone names no device it lacks. | Design, from furnishing the town | The making's rack and bench offered `store` and `retrieve`, whose conditions asked for the state `open` they never have. | 16 §3.5.1; `mods/core/data/interactions.json`. |
| D-854 | Every market town has its trades: a bakery, a tavern and a market near the centre, a guardhouse, a workshop and a healer's house anywhere, as `must_have` sites added to `core:town` by a patch in `gen/trades.json`, on maps of `maps/trades.json` whose tags no zone fills a lot with, with their named places as roles and no staff. The town's houses, terraces, shops and offices are furnished through `town_common`: a kitchen table with food, a pantry, a wardrobe, a chest; paving sometimes has litter. The guardhouse's armoury door is locked and its key lies on the desk. | Design, from furnishing the town | A site is the only way a settlement guarantees a building; a map with a zone's tag would change every lot of that zone. Who works there is the town's life, built by another package. | `crates/clash-gen/tests/golden/town.txt` re-blessed; every test script's hash moved for the definitions added. Templates cannot carry items, so the guards do not carry the key yet. |
| D-860 | Who a player may be is data. A `template` marked `playable` (with a `description` for the screens) may be a player's character. A scenario's character entry says how the player comes by it: `create` (default true) lets the player make it on the creation screen with every choice of the template; `pick`, `true` or a list of templates, says what is offered, every playable template by default where `create` holds; with `create` false and `pick` given the player chooses one ready-made and may only name it; with neither the player is given the character of `template`. The application offers `app.templates` for the game named to the creation session (`creation` with the op `scenario`, which the new-game screen's F2 sends), refuses to begin from a template the game does not offer and to change a ready-made character's choices but its name, and makes a finished character only for the game it was made for. Creation stays optional: a game begun without one gives the scenario's `template`. | Design, from the first player's report | No bundled game let the player choose who they are, so nothing the creation screen offered could be made and the abilities screen was always empty; 13 §4.2 named `create`, `spec` and `pick` but the beta built only a flag. A field on the template keeps the list with the content, and a scenario that wants fewer names them. | 06 §10.4; 13 §3, §4.2, §12; 12 §3.4, §10.7. The bundled `ordinary_person`, `brawler`, `marksman`, `adept` and `power_sampler` are playable; the sandbox and the town offer creation, the errand and the last stand a choice, the lane gives an ordinary person. |
| D-861 | Handling things is content: the actions `pick_up`, `drop`, `wear`, `take_off`, `stow`, `put_in`, `take_out` and `give` in `mods/core/data/holding.json`, each the engine's holding verb inside an action with a time and a hand, whose `requires` and `fail_text` read `self.hold_fault` and which ask it again against the truth when the action ends and say what is wrong then. `give` is refused, in words, by one who opposes or fears the giver. The inventory's popup names them bare, `e` lists what lies loose within reach to pick up, and the `ui` mod's control `pick_up` (`g` and `,`) picks up the one thing within reach or asks which. | Design, from the first player's report | The inventory named actions no mod defined, and nothing lying loose could be picked up, so the armoury's key on the sergeant's desk could not be taken. Actions keep timing and refusals in data; the engine supplies only what a mind context cannot read. | 06 §9.2; 12 §10.7; plans/first-playable.md U9. |
| D-862 | A thing with no name of its own is called by what it was made from: an item by its definition's `name` (a stack by its count and the plural), a device by its definition's, anything made from templates by the first template's name; only what has none of them reads "something". Ordinary screens name everyone but the player's character as the character knows them, through the reader lens with the character as the reader, and the character itself by its own name. | Design, from the first player's report | The reader lens answered only an entity's own name, so the errand's parcel was "something" in the status column and the inventory; screens named strangers by their true names, which a character may not know (01 R11). | 05 §4.6; 12 §3.5. |
| D-863 | A body plan's part may carry a `name` for readers (`left arm`); the part property `label` gives it, or else the part's side and its definition's name, or the definition's name alone. Screens show `label` and keep `name`, the part's name in the body, for what reads it as an id. | Design, from the first player's report | The inventory, the condition screen and the part picker showed `l_arm` and `r_eye`. | 06 §6.2; 12 §10.7. |
| D-864 | An attack's `with` may say in words what the blow needs (`needs`: `a blade`), read "Slash needs a blade in hand."; left out, the tag or the part is named in plain words with its article. No refusal a player reads names an id. | Design, from the first player's report | A blow that could not be made said it needed something in hand that is `` `blade` ``. | 07 §3.1. |
| D-865 | The properties `hold_fault(verb, what, other?)` and `loose_near` let an item action's `requires` say, as the character believes, why a holding verb could not be done (hands full, out of reach, nothing to carry it in, nowhere to wear it) and what lies loose within reach that it sees. Read of the character itself in a mind context. What lies in a cell counts as seen where the cell was in the member's last view, or is its own cell; for one not of the party, whatever is within reach. | Design, from building the item actions | A mind context may not read another entity's holder or an item's definition, and the verbs' own refusals are written for rules and the console. | 06 §9.2; reference. |
| D-866 | Keys are written for people wherever a player reads them: `app.controls` and `app.keys` give each row's keys also as `shown` (`Shift+J`, `Tab`, `F1`, `Esc`, `Page Up`), which the keys screen shows; a keymap still writes them `shift+j`. Every line of the message log begins with a capital letter, made so once as it is added. The setup screen says how the world's seed is chosen in words. | Design, from the first player's report | The keys screen showed `shift+j` and `tab`; a refusal written by the interface began in lower case; the setup screen said "Seed: ask". | 12 §3.2, §7.4, §9.2, §10.7. |
| D-867 | `app.defines(type)` says whether the loaded mods define anything of a definition type, so that a screen offers a setting only for a system that has something loaded: the options screen offers Cutscenes only where there are cutscenes. | Design, from the first player's report | Options offered Cutscenes though nothing defines one. | 12 §10.4, §10.7. |
| D-870 | The movement verbs `step`, `plan_path` and `follow_path` answer what moving comes to with an event and a null result, not a refusal: a step the world refuses raises `step_refused` and gives null; no way there raises `path_failed` and gives null; a refused step, a stale path or none gives null from `follow_path`. They decline (a fault of the rule) only for arguments that are wrong. The `ui` mod shows a refused step of the party's own in the log. | Play-testing the beta: walking the market town filled the log with `Fault: follow_path declined` and `Fault: plan_path declined`, dozens a minute | `go_to` plans, strides and plans again when a step is refused or no way is found: those are the outcomes it is written for, which `path_failed` and `step_refused` already report, and a rule's verb that declines is reported as a fault of the content. Every resident walking about produced them, and the player was shown them. | 06 §11; `clash_sim::movement::verbs`; the show `ui:step_refused`. No test script's hash moved for it. |
| D-871 | `interrupt_plan` also ends a wait in progress (`Sim::end_wait`), so that a rule can break off a long wait. The `ui` mod binds `w` to wait a minute and `shift+w` to wait an hour (the commands `wait { dur: 60 }` and `wait { dur: 3600 }`); the `core` mod's rule group `interrupts` breaks off the wait or the plan of a character someone plays when it comes to see someone it holds a foe or fears, by sight, or is wounded. | Play-testing: a game played against the clock (the errand's nightfall, the last stand's dawn) could only be waited out a second at a time, 28,800 key presses for the last stand | The engine decides no interruption (03 §5.3): the rules do, and they read the character's own stances. Hearing a foe's footsteps out of sight does not break a wait, or the last stand's would break every few seconds. | 03 §5.3; 12 §10.7; `mods/core/data/rules/interrupts.json`. |
| D-872 | `loc.time_of_day` is safe for belief: anyone may know the hour where they stand. The `core_ui` mod shows it in the play screen's status column (`Time 14:05`), with bars for stamina and reserve while the character has them and for toxin and sedation while either is above nothing, and the condition screen lists nourishment, toxin and the powers' reserves. | Play-testing: the errand is to be done before nightfall and the last stand held until dawn, and no screen said what time it was; abilities cost stamina and reserve that no screen showed | The sky is common knowledge; a mind reading the hour reads nothing it could not see. | `clash_sim::props`; `mods/core_ui/data/bars.json`. |
| D-873 | The last stand's refuge is the nearest door to where the game begins (a `cells` query), not a fixed offset, and a lamp is lit beside it; the strangers are given the senses of a person in `start.do`, the house is their beat (`set_home`), and the character holds each of them hostile. | Play-testing: the journal's marker stood on a wall (the door is two cells east at the seeds tried), the lane was dark but for the cell the character stood in, and the strangers never came: a mind given a stance (`set_stance`) before it first thinks has its state made then, and `ai::think` gives a mind its senses only when it makes that state, so they were blind | Content of the scenario. The engine's fault (a mind made by a stance never gets its mind's senses) is in the AI's package, which another stream is rewriting; it is reported there and worked round here with `attach_sense`. | `mods/core/data/scenarios/last_stand.json`; `scenarios/game/fresh_start.scn` checks the marker's new way (`north-east, 3 m`) and was re-blessed. |
| D-874 | The application's log line for a fault names the code of what refused and the definition and path of the effect: `Fault: \`set_fixture\` declined (world.unknown_state) in scenario core:last_stand at start.do[6]`. | Play-testing: `Fault: \`set_fixture\` declined` said nothing a modder could act on | The values were in the fault's reason already. | 12 §3.2; `clash_app::game::fault_line`. |
| D-875 | `Cosmos::occupant_ids_within` gives who is within a distance, each once by id, without sorting the occupancy entries by cell; a sense's sweep gathers its candidates with it. `occupants_within` sorts with an unstable sort, which gives the same order because no two entries share a cell and an id. | Measuring the market town: four fifths of a minute's wait went on sense sweeps, half of that on gathering and sorting candidates | Same results (the test scripts' hashes hold); less work for the sweep that every creature with senses makes twice a second. The rest of the cost is the number of sweeps, which is a model change (09 hot and warm detail) and is left recorded. | `clash_world::occupancy`, `clash_sim::perception::signal::candidates`. |
| D-876 | Enter in a field that takes a name does what the screen is for: on `ui:save_game` it saves, on `ui:new_game` it begins the game selected. Each screen binds `accept` in its `keys`, which a field that edits text does not use. | Play-testing: typing a save's name and pressing Enter did nothing | A screen's keys are offered what its focused widget does not use (12 §10.3). | `mods/ui/data/saving.json`, `mods/ui/data/app.json`. |
| D-877 | Work, and the modes of powers, say what came of them in the log: `ui` shows `recipe_started`, `recipe_stalled` (why, in words) and `recipe_completed` (and where what it gave is) for the party, `core_ui` shows `mode_started` and `mode_ended` (run dry, let go, failed); the Make screen gives the selected recipe's whole reason under the list; the abilities screen gives its modes more room so that a fifth is seen. | Play-testing: baking bread at the oven took two minutes in one key press and left the loaf in the oven with no word; a power held up by its reserve ended without a word; the Make screen's reasons were cut off at the edge | Long work and modes play out in one pass, so the log is how a player learns of them. | `mods/ui/data/making.json`, `mods/core_ui/data/shows.json`, `mods/ui/data/abilities.json`. |
| D-878 | The service `talk` gathers what can be said to someone the character sees standing in view now (the party's sight as the last batch ended, what the target cycle offers), as well as to someone it has detected; the refusal "You cannot see or hear them now." is for neither. The unconscious and the dead are refused acting with a sentence that names nobody by number (`unconscious, and cannot act until they come to`). | Play-testing: Tab chose a resident across the market as the target and the talk screen then said the character could not see or hear them; pressing keys while knocked out logged `0v0 is unconscious` | Sight in view is what the character perceives; awareness reaching detection takes a moment longer, and a player sees the person at once. | 12 §10.7; `clash_app::app::gather_talk`, `clash_sim::mind::can_act`. |
